Résumé en Français [SOURCE: Page_11]

Chaque année, le Forum Economique Mondial publie une cartographie des risques [SOURCE: Page_11]

mondiaux : le Global Risk Landscape. Tous les ans, le risque lié à des crises de l’eau apparaît [SOURCE: Page_11]

imperturbablement parmi les 5 risques mondiaux à plus fort impact (WEF, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_11]

Sous l’effet de pressions démographiques, du développement socio-économique et de [SOURCE: Page_11]

l’évolution des modèles de consommation, la demande mondiale en eau augmente de 1% par [SOURCE: Page_11]

an depuis les années 1980 (WWAP, 2019). Dans certains cas, l’augmentation globale de la [SOURCE: Page_11]

température moyenne (IPCC, 2018) et des tendances vers une hausse de l’évapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_11]

potentielle (voir, par exemple, Dinpashoh et al., 2019) pourraient accélérer ce phénomène. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Les ressources en eau disponibles varient grandement dans l’espace. Si les ressources [SOURCE: Page_11]

en eau par habitant représentaient 519 264 m^3 par an en Islande en 2014, elles se limitaient à [SOURCE: Page_11]

3m^3/an au Bahreïn (FAO-AQUASTAT, 2014). L’offre en eau varie aussi dans le temps : le [SOURCE: Page_11]

stress hydrique peut se faire ressentir à une période spécifique de l’année, par exemple l’été [SOURCE: Page_11]

dans le cas Français, quand les besoins en irrigation sont élevés (Barthélémy et Verdier, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_11]

La demande en eau peut donc approcher ou dépasser l’offre disponible dans différents [SOURCE: Page_11]

contextes. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Ainsi, le déséquilibre entre demande et offre en eau peut générer des épisodes de stress [SOURCE: Page_11]

hydrique. On définit généralement le stress hydrique au travers de l’indice proposé par [SOURCE: Page_11]

Falkenmark et al. (1989). Un pays est considéré comme étant en situation de stress hydrique si [SOURCE: Page_11]

les ressources en eau renouvelables par habitant y sont inférieures à 1700 m^3/an. En dessous de [SOURCE: Page_11]

1000m^3/an/habitant, l’indice considère une situation de rareté de l’eau chronique. Falkenmark [SOURCE: Page_11]

et al. définissent un seuil de 500m^3 en dessous duquel la capacité de gérer les ressources en eau [SOURCE: Page_11]

10 [SOURCE: Page_11]

n’est pas assurée, suggérant l’émergence de conflits liés à l’eau. Une définition alternative de [SOURCE: Page_12]

la rareté de l’eau compare la demande au volume total disponible (Raskin et al., 1997). Selon [SOURCE: Page_12]

cette définition, plus de 2 milliards de personnes vivraient aujourd’hui dans des pays subissant [SOURCE: Page_12]

un stress hydrique élevé, et environ 4 milliards de personnes vivraient un épisode majeur de [SOURCE: Page_12]

rareté de l’eau au moins un mois par an (WWAP, 2019). Dans ces circonstances, les outils de [SOURCE: Page_12]

politique publique permettant une gestion améliorée de la rareté des ressources en eau [SOURCE: Page_12]

constituent un enjeu majeur. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Deux types de mesures sont généralement envisagés pour gérer le risque lié à la rareté [SOURCE: Page_12]

de l’eau : l’augmentation de l’offre et la gestion de la demande. L’augmentation de l’offre [SOURCE: Page_12]

implique l’utilisation d’infrastructures de mobilisation de la ressource en eau (barrages, [SOURCE: Page_12]

pompage…), de manière à augmenter le volume d’eau disponible en période d’étiage ou de [SOURCE: Page_12]

rareté. La gestion par la demande peut référer à des mesures éducatives, réglementaires et/ou [SOURCE: Page_12]

de planification, ainsi qu’à l’usage d’incitations économiques (Wheeler et al., 2017). Depuis les [SOURCE: Page_12]

années 1950, l’augmentation de l’offre a souvent prédominé de façon à satisfaire une demande [SOURCE: Page_12]

croissante pour l’eau (Grimble, 1999 ; Saleth, 2011). Au-delà d’un certain stade, toutefois, [SOURCE: Page_12]

l’économie de l’eau atteint une phase décrite par Randall (1981) comme ‘mature’. Dans cette [SOURCE: Page_12]

situation, les coûts liés à une augmentation de l’offre (par un nouveau pompage, barrage…) [SOURCE: Page_12]

dépassent les bénéfices d’une augmentation de la consommation d’eau. En d’autres termes, le [SOURCE: Page_12]

bénéfice marginal obtenu par la mise à disposition d’une unité d’eau supplémentaire est [SOURCE: Page_12]

supérieur au bénéfice marginal obtenu par sa consommation. Des mesures de gestion de la [SOURCE: Page_12]

demande en eau peuvent donc être requises dans des situations de rareté de l’eau. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Les marchés de droits d’eau sont des outils économiques permettant de gérer la rareté [SOURCE: Page_12]

de l’eau du côté de la demande. Un marché de droit d’eau, parfois également appelé marché de [SOURCE: Page_12]

l’eau, est un système où des droits d’eau peuvent être prêtés, achetés ou vendus entre des [SOURCE: Page_12]

11 [SOURCE: Page_12]

individus, ou des institutions, de façon temporaire ou permanente. La détention d’un droit d’eau [SOURCE: Page_13]

permet à son détenteur d’accéder à un volume d’eau (ou à son équivalent en débit ou temps) [SOURCE: Page_13]

chaque saison, pour un usage généralement défini par le droit (irrigation, industrie, eau [SOURCE: Page_13]

potable…). Dans le cas de la gestion de l’eau en France par exemple, les droits d’eau pour [SOURCE: Page_13]

l’irrigation sont matérialisés par des autorisations de prélèvement annuelles, accordées aux [SOURCE: Page_13]

agriculteurs via des Organismes Uniques de Gestion Collective (OUGC), souvent les Chambres [SOURCE: Page_13]

d’Agricultures locales. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Les marchés de droits d’eau peuvent être formels, c’est-à-dire encadrés par un cadre [SOURCE: Page_13]

législatif dédié, ou informels (Easter et al., 1999). En pratique, ils peuvent être établis en [SOURCE: Page_13]

autorisant des transactions de gré à gré ou au travers de banques de l’eau, qui centralisent alors [SOURCE: Page_13]

les transactions (Montilla-Lopez et al., 2016). Les droits d’eau échangés sur un marché peuvent [SOURCE: Page_13]

être liés à un même usage pour l’eau : c’est le cas par exemple des marchés de droits d’eau liés [SOURCE: Page_13]

à l’irrigation. Les marchés peuvent également encadrer des transferts entre usages différents, [SOURCE: Page_13]

impliquant l’agriculture, l’industrie, les usages environnementaux et les usages urbains. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Les marchés de droits d’eau sont conçus en deux temps. Un plafond (cap) est d’abord [SOURCE: Page_13]

établi sur les droits d’eaux accordés, c’est-à-dire sur les volumes totaux prélevés chaque année. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Il devient ensuite autorisé d’acheter ou vendre des droits d’eau. Certains marchés se sont [SOURCE: Page_13]

développés en l’absence d’une limite claire et respectée sur les volumes prélevables, mais ils [SOURCE: Page_13]

sont souvent associés à des phénomènes de surexploitation de la ressource (voir, par exemple, [SOURCE: Page_13]

Bitran et al., 2014, pour le cas du Chili). [SOURCE: Page_13]

Des marchés de droits d’eau ont été établis dans des contextes culturels et économiques [SOURCE: Page_13]

variés. Des marchés formels ont été établis en Australie (Grafton et al., 2016), dans l’ouest des [SOURCE: Page_13]

Etats-Unis (Colby, 1990), au Chili (Bitran et al., 2014), en Espagne (Palomo-Hierro et al., 2015) [SOURCE: Page_13]

et en Chine (Zhang, 2007). Divers marchés informels ont été décrits par la littérature, [SOURCE: Page_13]

12 [SOURCE: Page_13]

notamment en Inde (Mukherji, 2007) et au Pakistan (Razzaq et al., 2019). Dans la mesure où [SOURCE: Page_14]

l’établissement de marchés de droits d’eau a été envisagé dans divers autres contextes [SOURCE: Page_14]

(Shatanawi et Al-Jayousi, 1995 ; Wheeler et al., 2017 ; Mellah, 2018), le nécessaire débat sur [SOURCE: Page_14]

leurs avantages et inconvénients potentiels se doit d’être informé par des études empiriques [SOURCE: Page_14]

fondées sur des exemples de marchés fonctionnels. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Cette thèse a pour but d’améliorer la compréhension du rôle que peuvent jouer les [SOURCE: Page_14]

marchés de droits d’eau comme outils de gestion de la rareté de l’eau. Elle aborde d’abord les [SOURCE: Page_14]

bénéfices économiques associés à l’usage de marchés de droit d’eau dans l’agriculture, [SOURCE: Page_14]

considère ensuite deux dysfonctionnements possibles liés à leur établissement en pratique, puis [SOURCE: Page_14]

conclut en questionnant leur potentiel dans un contexte Français. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Bien que divers exemples de marchés de droits d’eau fonctionnels aient été présentés [SOURCE: Page_14]

par la littérature, les études empiriques sur les marchés de droits d’eau sont souvent limitées par [SOURCE: Page_14]

la rareté des données existantes, en particulier liées au marché. Un autre obstacle fréquent est [SOURCE: Page_14]

lié à un nombre souvent faible de transactions constatées. Pour tenter de contenir ces problèmes, [SOURCE: Page_14]

les trois premiers chapitres de cette thèse se basent sur les marchés de droits d’eau formels en [SOURCE: Page_14]

Australie, et ce pour trois raisons. Premièrement, les premiers marchés Australiens ont été [SOURCE: Page_14]

établis dans les années 1980, dans le bassin de Murray-Darling. Cet historique de [SOURCE: Page_14]

fonctionnement relativement long permet un retour d’expérience sur les succès et échecs liés à [SOURCE: Page_14]

l’établissement de marchés de droits d’eau en pratique, qui peut être exploité dans d’autre [SOURCE: Page_14]

contextes si les marchés sont considérés comme des outils potentiels. Deuxièmement, [SOURCE: Page_14]

l’existence d’une large zone hydrologiquement connectée dans le sud du bassin de Murray- [SOURCE: Page_14]

Darling permet des études de cas impliquant un grand nombre d’acteurs potentiels. Au cours [SOURCE: Page_14]

du temps, l’usage du marché par les irrigants y a considérablement augmenté : en 2015, environ [SOURCE: Page_14]

la moitié des irrigants avaient effectué au moins une transaction permanente de droits d’eau et [SOURCE: Page_14]

13 [SOURCE: Page_14]

78% des irrigants avaient été impliqués dans au moins une transaction temporaire. Le Bassin [SOURCE: Page_15]

de Murray-Darling est donc souvent considéré par la littérature empirique sur les marchés de [SOURCE: Page_15]

droits d’eau : il constitue un laboratoire pour l’usage de ces outils dans un contexte de stress [SOURCE: Page_15]

hydrique croissant et de rareté régulière de la ressource en eau. Troisièmement, l’existence de [SOURCE: Page_15]

données publiques en lien avec les achats et ventes de droits d’eau publiés par l’Office [SOURCE: Page_15]

Australien des Statistiques (Australian Bureau of Statistics, ABS) et le Bureau météorologique [SOURCE: Page_15]

(Bureau of Meteorology, BoM) permet des analyses empiriques de marchés fonctionnels. De [SOURCE: Page_15]

façon générale, les études de cas portant sur les marchés Australiens peuvent donc fournir des [SOURCE: Page_15]

éléments utiles à la connaissance de ces systèmes d’échanges, et pouvant informer le débat sur [SOURCE: Page_15]

les marchés de droits d’eau dans d’autres contextes. Le chapitre 4 de cette thèse en fournit une [SOURCE: Page_15]

illustration, en questionnant la transférabilité des marchés de droits d’eau à deux contextes [SOURCE: Page_15]

Français (le bassin du Marais Poitevin, et le système Neste) via un cadre d’analyse proposé par [SOURCE: Page_15]

la littérature Australienne (Wheeler et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_15]

L’usage du marché en matière de gestion des ressources en eau a été proposé par la [SOURCE: Page_15]

littérature économique comme un moyen d’améliorer l’efficience des usages en eau. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Différentes définitions de l’efficience peuvent être envisagées. L’efficience technique renvoie [SOURCE: Page_15]

à la capacité d’une unité de production d’opérer sur la frontière de production, c’est à dire de [SOURCE: Page_15]

produire le maximum possible pour une quantité donnée de facteur de production (Coelli et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

2005). Dans le cas de l’eau, l’efficience technique renvoie donc au fait de produire un maximum [SOURCE: Page_15]

pour une quantité d’eau donnée. L’efficience allocative renvoie à l’allocation de l’eau basée sur [SOURCE: Page_15]

un usage générant un revenu plus élevé (Wheeler et al., 2014). Les marchés de droits d’eau [SOURCE: Page_15]

peuvent augmenter l’efficience allocative en réallouant l’eau depuis des usages à moins forte [SOURCE: Page_15]

valeur ajoutée vers des usages à plus forte valeur ajoutée (Dinar et al., 1997), et vers des [SOURCE: Page_15]

activités plus productives (Hodgson, 2006). Ils peuvent également améliorer l’efficience [SOURCE: Page_15]

technique en permettant l’accès à l’eau à des nouveaux usagers très efficients, l’adoption de [SOURCE: Page_15]

14 [SOURCE: Page_15]

technologies visant à économiser l’eau et la diminution des usages peu efficients pour l’eau [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Qureshi et al., 2009). La plupart des études empiriques dédiées à la mesure de l’impact [SOURCE: Page_16]

économique des marchés de droits d’eau recourent à la méthode de l’équilibre général (Peterson [SOURCE: Page_16]

et al., 2005 ; NWC, 2012). Le premier chapitre de cette thèse tente de mesurer les gains de [SOURCE: Page_16]

productivité dans l’agriculture liés à l’utilisation de marchés de droits d’eau dans le contexte [SOURCE: Page_16]

Australien via un modèle de frontière stochastique (Battese et Coelli, 1995). Deux questions de [SOURCE: Page_16]

recherches sont abordées dans ce chapitre. Premièrement, l’existence de marchés de l’eau est- [SOURCE: Page_16]

elle associée à une efficience plus grande des usages en eau, et donc de la production agricole ? [SOURCE: Page_16]

Deuxièmement, des marchés plus développés – donc des volumes échangés plus importants – [SOURCE: Page_16]

traduisent-ils un gain d’efficience plus élevé ? Un modèle de frontière stochastique est appliqué [SOURCE: Page_16]

à des données annuelles sur la production agricole, les circonstances climatiques, l’existence [SOURCE: Page_16]

d’un marché et l’intensité des transactions éventuelles à l’échelle de régions Australiennes, [SOURCE: Page_16]

entre 2011 et 2017. Notre modèle mesure l’efficience de chaque région dans son utilisation de [SOURCE: Page_16]

deux facteurs de production : l’eau et la surface agricole. Pour chaque observation, nous [SOURCE: Page_16]

mesurons l’inefficience comme la distance à la frontière de production, c’est-à-dire la [SOURCE: Page_16]

différence de production à utilisation de facteurs égale vis-à-vis de l’observation la plus efficace [SOURCE: Page_16]

de la base de données. L’étude du lien entre existence du marché d’une part, définie par une [SOURCE: Page_16]

variable binaire égale à 1 lorsqu’un marché existe dans une région pour une année donnée, et [SOURCE: Page_16]

inefficience d’autre part révèle une association positive entre présence du marché et efficience [SOURCE: Page_16]

de la production agricole. En d’autres termes, les régions disposant d’un marché utilisent leurs [SOURCE: Page_16]

facteurs de production (dont l’eau) de façon plus efficace en moyenne. Dans un deuxième [SOURCE: Page_16]

temps, nous remplaçons la variable d’existence du marché par l’intensité de transactions, [SOURCE: Page_16]

définie comme le volume d’eau acheté dans chaque région là où un marché existe. Afin de [SOURCE: Page_16]

prendre en compte le biais de sélection lié au fait que nous ne pouvons observer l’intensité des [SOURCE: Page_16]

transactions que lorsqu’un marché existe, nous utilisons la méthode des résidus généralisés [SOURCE: Page_16]

15 [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Gourieroux et al., 1987). Les résultats n’indiquent pas de lien significatif entre intensité des [SOURCE: Page_17]

transactions et efficience. Nos résultats confirment donc l’existence d’une relation positive [SOURCE: Page_17]

entre existence du marché et efficience des usages en eau, mais pas entre intensité et efficience. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Des analyses basées sur des données plus complètes seraient intéressantes pour renforcer et [SOURCE: Page_17]

approfondir ce résultat. [SOURCE: Page_17]

En parallèle avec la littérature dédiée aux bénéfices associés à l’usage du marché [SOURCE: Page_17]

appliqué à la gestion des ressources en eau, différentes préoccupations ont été exprimées dans [SOURCE: Page_17]

la littérature quant à leurs inconvénients potentiels. Les limites classiques de l’action des [SOURCE: Page_17]

marchés sont souvent amplifiées dans le cas de l’eau : l’eau est une ressource ‘massive’, et les [SOURCE: Page_17]

coûts liés à son transport sont élevés (Turner et al., 2004). En outre, différentes externalités [SOURCE: Page_17]

peuvent être associées aux transferts de droits d’eau : une modification de l’hydrologie [SOURCE: Page_17]

(localisation des prélèvements et des débits restitués) et l’impact des transferts sur la région où [SOURCE: Page_17]

l’eau était utilisée à l’origine sont fréquemment évoquées et peuvent venir limiter voire annuler [SOURCE: Page_17]

les gains de l’échange (Garrido Fernandez, 2016). Une externalité en particulier est liée aux [SOURCE: Page_17]

infrastructures associées à l’irrigation. Ces infrastructures sont généralement très coûteuses à [SOURCE: Page_17]

entretenir, et les irrigants se répartissent ces coûts fixes de façon collective. Si l’un des irrigants [SOURCE: Page_17]

décide de vendre son droit d’eau de manière permanente, les coûts de maintenance de [SOURCE: Page_17]

l’infrastructure vont devoir être assumés par ceux qui continuent à irriguer, qui sont [SOURCE: Page_17]

généralement en compétition avec le vendeur (Chong et Sunding, 2006 ; Heaney et al., 2006 ; [SOURCE: Page_17]

Bjornlund, 2008 ; Frontier Economics et al., 2007). De plus, si un irrigant cesse de produire, le [SOURCE: Page_17]

manque d’entretien de sa propriété augmente le risque de maladie sur les cultures de ses voisins [SOURCE: Page_17]

(Frontier Economics et al., 2007 : Bjornlund, 2008). Dans le cas australien, peu d’éléments ont [SOURCE: Page_17]

toutefois été publiés à ce jour attestant que les ventes de droits permanents génèreraient des [SOURCE: Page_17]

pertes significatives dans la zone d’origine du droit d’eau (Grafton et al., 2016 ; Haensch et al., [SOURCE: Page_17]

2019). [SOURCE: Page_17]

16 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Le second chapitre de la thèse se penche sur un dysfonctionnement possible du marché : [SOURCE: Page_18]

les délits d’initiés. Bien connue sur les marchés financiers, cette pratique peut être définie [SOURCE: Page_18]

comme l’achat ou la vente illégale de titres financiers par des individus ou des firmes possédant [SOURCE: Page_18]

une information importante et inconnue du public (Meulbroeuk, 1992). Sur les marchés [SOURCE: Page_18]

financiers, ces pratiques sont surveillées par des institutions comme la Securities and Exchange [SOURCE: Page_18]

Commission (SEC) aux Etats-Unis. Dans le cas des marchés de droits d’eau, cet enjeu n’a [SOURCE: Page_18]

encore suscité que peu d’attention. Sur les marchés Australiens, ces excès n’ont fait l’objet [SOURCE: Page_18]

d’une régulation spécifique que depuis 2014, au travers de l’introduction de nouvelles règles [SOURCE: Page_18]

portant sur les transactions. Des délits d’initiés ont été reportés de manière anecdotique [SOURCE: Page_18]

(Hancock, 2008), bien que non publiés dans une revue à comité de lecture. Pour la première [SOURCE: Page_18]

fois, nous questionnons l’existence de délits d’initiés sur les marchés de droits d’eau [SOURCE: Page_18]

Australiens. Pour ce faire, nous étudions les mouvements de prix autour d’importantes [SOURCE: Page_18]

annonces ayant un impact sur le prix de l’eau, en utilisant 10 ans de données de marché [SOURCE: Page_18]

quotidiennes associées à des variables connues pour influencer le prix de l’eau par ailleurs. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Notre modèle basé sur l’économétrie des séries temporelles montre l’existence de mouvements [SOURCE: Page_18]

de prix informés entre 2008 et 2014. Après 2014, la fréquence de ces mouvements de prix [SOURCE: Page_18]

suspect diminue fortement, et peut uniquement être ramenée à 3 occurrences. Ces mouvements [SOURCE: Page_18]

de prix peuvent être soit liés à des délits d’initiés (Keown et Pinkerton, 1981 ; Meulbroek, [SOURCE: Page_18]

1992 ; Maug et al., 2008), soit à une spéculation rationnelle conduisant certains agents à deviner [SOURCE: Page_18]

le contenu des annonces à venir (Jensen et Ruback, 1983 ; Jarrell et Poulsen, 1989 ; Aspris et [SOURCE: Page_18]

al., 2014 ; Gu and Kurov, 2018). La diminution de la fréquence des mouvements de prix [SOURCE: Page_18]

anormaux après le changement politique de 2014 pourrait constituer un argument en faveur de [SOURCE: Page_18]

l’interprétation de délits d’initiés (Gupta et Misra, 1988). Un autre argument en faveur de cette [SOURCE: Page_18]

interprétation est lié à nos tentatives de prédire le contenu des annonces pour lesquelles nous [SOURCE: Page_18]

détectons des mouvements de prix anormaux après 2014. En utilisant les données à disposition [SOURCE: Page_18]

17 [SOURCE: Page_18]

du public, aucun modèle testé ne peut prédire correctement le contenu de toute les annonces en [SOURCE: Page_19]

question. Toutefois, la sophistication accrue ainsi que l’accroissement des volumes échangés [SOURCE: Page_19]

sur les marchés Australiens dans le bassin de Murray-Darling rendent possible une [SOURCE: Page_19]

interprétation en faveur de comportements rationnels de spéculation. Dans tous les cas, [SOURCE: Page_19]

l’introduction de régulations interdisant les délits d’initiés semble indispensable dans le cadre [SOURCE: Page_19]

d’un marché de droit d’eau, le potentiel pour de telles pratiques étant établi. [SOURCE: Page_19]

Le troisième chapitre de la thèse est consacré à un autre dysfonctionnement potentiel, [SOURCE: Page_19]

lié cette fois à la substitution pouvant exister entre les ressources en eau de surface et en eau [SOURCE: Page_19]

souterraine. Nous considérons en particulier le cas du Murrumbidgee, en Australie, ou les deux [SOURCE: Page_19]

types de ressources sont disponibles et font l’objet de transactions sur des marchés. [SOURCE: Page_19]

Un effet de substitution entre eau de surface et eau souterraine peut en effet survenir et [SOURCE: Page_19]

affecter la manière dont les marchés fonctionnent. Dans différents contextes, la littérature [SOURCE: Page_19]

rapporte que les irrigants peuvent augmenter leur consommation d’eau souterraine de façon à [SOURCE: Page_19]

compenser des réductions liées à leurs droits d’eau de surface (Zhang, 2007; Wheeler et al., [SOURCE: Page_19]

2016). Dans le cadre de politiques visant à réduire les usages dans un contexte de sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_19]

ou à augmenter les débits minimums environnementaux (débits objectifs d’étiage en France), [SOURCE: Page_19]

cet effet peut conduire à des conséquences inattendues et compromettant la réussite de telles [SOURCE: Page_19]

politiques. Dans ce chapitre, nous abordons deux questions de recherche visant à mieux [SOURCE: Page_19]

comprendre les dynamiques des marchés de droits d’eau souterraine quand ils coexistent avec [SOURCE: Page_19]

des marchés de droits d’eau de surface. D’abord, nous questionnons l’influence du prix de l’eau [SOURCE: Page_19]

de surface dans la formation des prix sur le marché de l’eau souterraine. Analysant 10 ans de [SOURCE: Page_19]

données mensuelles sur ces deux types de marchés, nous utilisons un vecteur autorégressif avec [SOURCE: Page_19]

variables exogènes (VAR-X) pour questionner le rôle du marché de droits d’eau de surface dans [SOURCE: Page_19]

la formation des prix. Nous prouvons que l’information est d’abord assimilée par le marché de [SOURCE: Page_19]

18 [SOURCE: Page_19]

droits d’eau de surface, puis transmise au marché de droits d’eau souterraine par la suite. En [SOURCE: Page_20]

tenant compte de ce phénomène, nous étudions ensuite l’élasticité de la demande en eau [SOURCE: Page_20]

souterraine dans un contexte de marché. Nos résultats indiquent qu’une augmentation de 1% [SOURCE: Page_20]

du prix de l’eau souterraine conduite à une diminution de 1.05% de la demande, ce qui suggère [SOURCE: Page_20]

que la demande en eau souterraine est élastique mais proche de l’unité. De façon générale, nos [SOURCE: Page_20]

résultats soulignent la nécessité d’établir des politiques visant à la fois les ressources en eau de [SOURCE: Page_20]

surface et en eau souterraine : les marchés sont interconnectés, et l’élasticité de la demande [SOURCE: Page_20]

suggère que la demande en eau souterraine réagira à tout changement dans le prix de l’eau. Un [SOURCE: Page_20]

effet de substitution en Australie serait donc hautement probable, dans le cas où des politiques [SOURCE: Page_20]

affectant uniquement les ressources en eau de surface seraient mises en place. [SOURCE: Page_20]

En dehors du contexte Australien, l’adoption de marchés de droits d’eau a récemment [SOURCE: Page_20]

été testée (Zhang, 2007) ou questionnée (Mellah, 2018) dans différents cadres. Toutefois, les [SOURCE: Page_20]

marchés de droits d’eau s’inscrivent nécessairement dans un contexte socio-politique et ne [SOURCE: Page_20]

mènent pas nécessairement à des solutions efficientes ou effectives en matière de gestion des [SOURCE: Page_20]

ressources en eau (Breviglieri et al., 2018). Dans ce contexte, le cadre d’analyse de Wheeler et [SOURCE: Page_20]

al. (2017) suggère un ensemble de critères d’analyse afin d’identifier les facteurs facilitants, les [SOURCE: Page_20]

obstacles, les bénéfices et coûts éventuels liés à l’établissement de marchés de droits d’eau dans [SOURCE: Page_20]

un contexte donné. Le quatrième chapitre de cette thèse applique ce cadre d’analyse à deux [SOURCE: Page_20]

études de cas en France : le bassin versant du Marais Poitevin et le système Neste. Dans le cadre [SOURCE: Page_20]

de cette étude, 11 entretiens semi-directifs (6 concernant le système Neste, et 5 dans le bassin [SOURCE: Page_20]

du Marais Poitevin) ont été réalisés avec des acteurs clef de la gestion de l’eau dans chaque [SOURCE: Page_20]

bassin. Dans chaque cas, le chapitre présente le contexte géographique et hydrologique. La [SOURCE: Page_20]

France n’est pas un pays en situation de stress hydrique au sens de Falkenmark (1989) et il y [SOURCE: Page_20]

est illégal d’acheter ou de vendre les droits d’eau (autorisations de prélèvements). Toutefois, [SOURCE: Page_20]

nos deux études de cas sont soumises à des épisodes récurrents de déséquilibre entre offre et [SOURCE: Page_20]

19 [SOURCE: Page_20]

demande en eau en été, lorsque les besoins en prélèvements liés à l’irrigation sont les plus forts. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Dans la mesure où les politiques d’augmentation de l’offre ont récemment montré leurs limites [SOURCE: Page_21]

dans les deux cas, il peut être intéressant de considérer des moyens de gestion de la rareté par [SOURCE: Page_21]

la demande. Nous détaillons ensuite les besoins en matière de gestion de la ressource, et les [SOURCE: Page_21]

politiques actuellement mises en place pour y répondre. Nous abordons finalement les facteurs [SOURCE: Page_21]

facilitants, les obstacles et les bénéfices potentiels associés à l’établissement de marchés de [SOURCE: Page_21]

droits d’eau dans nos contextes. De façon générale, des prélèvements excessifs dans certains [SOURCE: Page_21]

aquifères, notamment en bordure du Marais Poitevin ont pu aboutir à un drainage des ressources [SOURCE: Page_21]

en eau du marais en période estivale. Pour faire face à ce problème, un ensemble de mesures de [SOURCE: Page_21]

gestion ont été mises en place par un établissement d’Etat, l’Etablissement Public du Marais [SOURCE: Page_21]

Poitevin (EPMP) en concertation avec les acteurs locaux. Des réserves substituant les usages [SOURCE: Page_21]

estivaux par des prélèvements en hiver ont en outre été réalisées dans la partie Nord du bassin. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Certaines de ces réserves ont soulevé d’importants mouvements de protestation, notamment [SOURCE: Page_21]

dans la partie sud aboutissant à des blocages qui persistaient encore au moment de notre étude. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Les obstacles à l’installation de marchés de droits d’eau dans le bassin versant du Marais [SOURCE: Page_21]

Poitevin incluent l’absence d’un volume prélevable maximum, et donc d’une limite sur les [SOURCE: Page_21]

droits d’eau totaux accordés, et une faible acceptabilité sociale des marchés dans le bassin telle [SOURCE: Page_21]

que rapportée dans la littérature (Kervarec, 2014) mais également au cours des entretiens. Les [SOURCE: Page_21]

nombreuses connections hydrologiques ainsi que la diversité des usages pour l’eau dans le [SOURCE: Page_21]

bassin suggèrent pourtant l’existence de bénéfices potentiels importants en cas de réallocation [SOURCE: Page_21]

des ressources en eau vers des usages plus créateurs de valeur. Le système Neste est un [SOURCE: Page_21]

ensemble de 18 cours d’eau de surface artificiellement réalimentés par un canal détournant une [SOURCE: Page_21]

partie du débit de la rivière Neste en provenance des Pyrénées. La Compagnie d’Aménagement [SOURCE: Page_21]

des Coteaux de Gascogne (CACG) gère les débits dans le système en ajustant les diversions de [SOURCE: Page_21]

la rivière Neste de façon à assurer un respect des droits d’eau accordés aux agriculteurs irrigants [SOURCE: Page_21]

20 [SOURCE: Page_21]

ainsi qu’aux autres usagers et à maintenir un débit objectif d’étiage. Depuis la mise en place [SOURCE: Page_22]

d’un volume prélevable maximum en 1992, la demande en eau dans le système excède l’offre [SOURCE: Page_22]

disponible. Une liste d’attente a donc été créée pour les agriculteurs souhaitant obtenir un droit [SOURCE: Page_22]

d’eau. Les obstacles potentiels liés à l’établissement de marchés de droits d’eau dans le système [SOURCE: Page_22]

Neste incluent : le cadre actuel, basé sur des autorisations de prélèvement accordées par un [SOURCE: Page_22]

OUGC et interdisant les ventes de droits d’eau ; le principe du droit acquis, qui établit qu’un [SOURCE: Page_22]

irrigant ayant obtenu un droit d’eau une année est en droit d’obtenir le même droit l’année [SOURCE: Page_22]

suivante ; et une opposition culturelle et politique à l’utilisation du marché pour gérer les [SOURCE: Page_22]

ressources en eau, déjà retrouvée dans le contexte du Marais Poitevin. Toutefois, les [SOURCE: Page_22]

infrastructures considérables permettent des transferts d’eau facilités, et les marchés de droits [SOURCE: Page_22]

d’eau pourraient flexibiliser la demande en eau, permettant aux irrigants sur la liste d’attente [SOURCE: Page_22]

d’acheter de l’eau aux irrigants ayant des cultures à valeur ajoutée relativement faible (telles [SOURCE: Page_22]

que le maïs). [SOURCE: Page_22]

En questionnant l’impact des marchés de droits d’eau sur l’efficience des usages en eau [SOURCE: Page_22]

(Chapitre 1), l’existence de délits d’initiés (Chapitre 2), l’articulation entre marchés de droits [SOURCE: Page_22]

d’eau souterraine et de surface (Chapitre 3) et en questionnant leur transférabilité à des [SOURCE: Page_22]

contextes Français (Chapitre 4), cette thèse contribue à l’amélioration de la connaissance [SOURCE: Page_22]

empirique sur l’usage du marché appliqué à la gestion des ressources en eau. Nos résultats [SOURCE: Page_22]

peuvent être utilisés pour informer les débats et politiques publiques sur ces systèmes et ce [SOURCE: Page_22]

qu’ils peuvent apporter pour gérer la rareté de la ressource en eau, dans la mesure où les [SOURCE: Page_22]

épisodes de stress hydriques pourraient gagner en fréquence et en intensité sous l’effet du [SOURCE: Page_22]

changement climatique (IPCC, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_22]

General Introduction [SOURCE: Page_27]

Every year, the Davos World Economic Forum publishes the Global Risks Landscape. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Invariably, the occurrence of water crises appears among the top 5 risks with the biggest [SOURCE: Page_27]

potential impact (WEF, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_27]

Water demand has been increasing worldwide by about 1% per year since the 1980s. [SOURCE: Page_27]

This can be attributed to various influences including population growth, socioeconomic [SOURCE: Page_27]

development, and evolving consumption patterns (WWAP, 2019). In many areas, the overall [SOURCE: Page_27]

increase in observed monthly global mean surface temperature (IPCC, 2018) and upward trends [SOURCE: Page_27]

in potential evapotranspiration (see, for example, Dinpashoh et al., 2019) might lead to even [SOURCE: Page_27]

greater water demands in the decades to come. [SOURCE: Page_27]

In parallel to this, the amount of renewable internal freshwater resources available can [SOURCE: Page_27]

vary greatly in space: water resources in Bahrein represented 3 cubic meters per capita per year [SOURCE: Page_27]

in 2014, and 519,264 m^3/capita/year in Iceland (FAO-AQUASTAT, 2014). They can also vary [SOURCE: Page_27]

in time: some countries – as France - can experience water stress at specific times such as [SOURCE: Page_27]

summer months, when irrigation water needs are high (Barthélémy and Verdier, 2008). Thus, [SOURCE: Page_27]

water demand can approach or exceed water supply in various times and contexts. [SOURCE: Page_27]

As a result, surface and groundwater resources are put under stress in many areas of the [SOURCE: Page_27]

world. Water stress is often defined using the water stress index proposed by Falkenmark et al. [SOURCE: Page_27]

(1989): a country is said to experience water stress if the available renewable water resources [SOURCE: Page_27]

per capita is less than 1700 m^3 per year. Chronic water scarcity is said to occur below 1000 [SOURCE: Page_27]

m^3/capita. Finally, Falkenmark et al. define a water barrier (under 500 m^3/capita), under which [SOURCE: Page_27]

the capacity to manage water resources is endangered, suggesting the occurrence of water- [SOURCE: Page_27]

26 [SOURCE: Page_27]

related conflicts. An alternative measure of water scarcity compares water demand to the total [SOURCE: Page_28]

amount of freshwater resources available (Raskin et al., 1997). In this sense, over 2 billion [SOURCE: Page_28]

people live in countries experiencing high water stress and about 4 billion people experience [SOURCE: Page_28]

severe water scarcity at least one month a year (WWAP, 2019). Considering the potential tools [SOURCE: Page_28]

able to help policymakers manage water scarcity is therefore of interest. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Two diverse arrangements are usually considered in order to deal with water scarcity [SOURCE: Page_28]

risk: Supply augmentation and demand-side management. Supply augmentation implies to [SOURCE: Page_28]

build infrastructure, in order to provide more water in times of needs. Demand-side [SOURCE: Page_28]

management refers to educational measures, regulatory or planning processes and economic [SOURCE: Page_28]

incentives (Wheeler et al. 2017). Since the 1950s, supply augmentation has been predominantly [SOURCE: Page_28]

used in order to satisfy the growing demand for water: usual approaches involve a water [SOURCE: Page_28]

development based on the creation of additional water storages and allocations (Grimble, 1999; [SOURCE: Page_28]

Saleth, 2011). In many countries, however, the water economy has reached a ‘mature’ phase [SOURCE: Page_28]

(Randall, 1981) where the cost of supply augmentation offsets the potential benefits of an [SOURCE: Page_28]

increased water use. In other words, the marginal cost of obtaining an additional unit of water [SOURCE: Page_28]

often exceeds the marginal benefit that can be obtained from it. Demand-side management [SOURCE: Page_28]

measures have therefore been considered as cost-effective approaches to deal with water [SOURCE: Page_28]

scarcity. [SOURCE: Page_28]

In this perspective, water markets emerged as economic tools that can be used in order [SOURCE: Page_28]

to manage water on the demand side. By water markets, we mean systems where rights to water [SOURCE: Page_28]

can be bought, sold or leased between individuals or institutions, in a temporary or permanent [SOURCE: Page_28]

manner. Water markets can be either formal (i.e. with a dedicated legal system) or informal [SOURCE: Page_28]

(Easter et al., 1999). In practice, they can function by simply allowing transactions between [SOURCE: Page_28]

private individuals or through the use of water banks centralizing transactions within a more [SOURCE: Page_28]

27 [SOURCE: Page_28]

institutionalized context (Montilla-Lopez et al., 2016). Water resources can be exchanged [SOURCE: Page_29]

within one economic sector (i.e. irrigation) or transferred between different sectors (e.g. rural [SOURCE: Page_29]

to urban). Water markets are theoretically designed as cap and trade systems: a ‘cap’ on water [SOURCE: Page_29]

rights (i.e. a maximal total amount of water rights) is first defined, and individuals are then [SOURCE: Page_29]

allowed to trade water rights. Some examples of water markets functioning without a clearly [SOURCE: Page_29]

defined and enforced cap can be found, but they can be associated with groundwater aquifer [SOURCE: Page_29]

depletion (See Bitran et al., 2014, for Chile). [SOURCE: Page_29]

Water markets have been established in various cultural and economic backgrounds. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Examples of formal water markets include Australia (Grafton et al., 2016), the western United [SOURCE: Page_29]

States (Colby, 1990), Chile (Bitran et al., 2014), Spain (Palermo-Hierro et al., 2015) and China [SOURCE: Page_29]

(Zhang, 2007). Examples of informal water markets can be found in India (Mukherji, 2007) or [SOURCE: Page_29]

Pakistan (Razzaq et al., 2019). Considering the fact that establishing water markets can be [SOURCE: Page_29]

considered in various other contexts (Shatanawi and Al Jayousi, 1995; Wheeler et al., 2017; [SOURCE: Page_29]

Mellah, 2018), the debate over the relevance of such tools needs to be informed by empirical [SOURCE: Page_29]

research focusing on existing examples of water markets. [SOURCE: Page_29]

This thesis aims to improve the knowledge on water markets as tools to deal with water [SOURCE: Page_29]

scarcity. We first focus on the economic benefits associated with water markets, then consider [SOURCE: Page_29]

two potential problems arising from their use in practice and conclude by questioning their [SOURCE: Page_29]

potential in a French context. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Although different examples of functioning water markets have been presented in the [SOURCE: Page_29]

literature, empirical studies on the water markets are often lacking due to limited data [SOURCE: Page_29]

availability or insufficient transaction levels. In order to avoid such problems, the first three [SOURCE: Page_29]

chapters of this thesis focus on formal water markets in Australia for several reasons. First, [SOURCE: Page_29]

Australian water markets were initially established in the 1980s, in the Murray-Darling Basin. [SOURCE: Page_29]

28 [SOURCE: Page_29]

The relatively long history of water markets in Australia allows a review of the successes and [SOURCE: Page_30]

failures related to the establishment of water markets, that can be used in many other contexts [SOURCE: Page_30]

where the use of water markets is considered. Second, the existence of a large hydrologically [SOURCE: Page_30]

inter-connected area in the Southern Murray-Darling Basin allows for a cases study implying a [SOURCE: Page_30]

large number of potential actors. Within the basin, irrigators’ participation in water trade has [SOURCE: Page_30]

consistently increased with time: in the southern MDB, approximately half of all irrigators had [SOURCE: Page_30]

made at least one permanent water trade, whereas 78% had conducted at least one temporary [SOURCE: Page_30]

water trade by 2015-16 (Grafton and Wheeler 2018). Therefore, the MDB is often used as a [SOURCE: Page_30]

case study in the literature, as a laboratory to the use of water markets. Third, the existence of [SOURCE: Page_30]

publicly available water market data^1 allows empirical analyses of a functioning water market [SOURCE: Page_30]

example. Overall, insights from Australian case studies can be used to inform the debates in [SOURCE: Page_30]

other contexts where the use of market mechanisms applied to water resources management is [SOURCE: Page_30]

considered. An illustration of this is provided in the fourth and last chapter of this thesis, where [SOURCE: Page_30]

we question the potential generalization of water markets – and especially their potential [SOURCE: Page_30]

application to the French case – using a general framework published by the Australian water [SOURCE: Page_30]

markets literature (Wheeler et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_30]

The use of market mechanisms applied to water resources has been promoted by the [SOURCE: Page_30]

economic literature as a mean to increase the efficiency of water use. Different definitions of [SOURCE: Page_30]

water use efficiency can be used. For example, allocative efficiency refers to water being [SOURCE: Page_30]

allocated to where it generates the most income, while technical efficiency refers to [SOURCE: Page_30]

improvements in the efficient use of water through technology (Wheeler et al., 2014). In this [SOURCE: Page_30]

perspective, Water markets can foster allocative efficiency gains by redirecting water from low- [SOURCE: Page_30]

valued to higher valued uses (Dinar et al., 1997), and towards more productive activities [SOURCE: Page_30]

1 Available online on the Australian Bureau of Meteorology’s Website. [SOURCE: Page_30]

29 [SOURCE: Page_30]

(Hodgson, 2006). They can also increase technical efficiency through the expansion of water [SOURCE: Page_31]

use by highly efficient new water users, adoption of water conserving technologies and [SOURCE: Page_31]

elimination of inefficient uses for water (Qureshi et al., 2009). Most of the studies attempting [SOURCE: Page_31]

to measure the economic impacts of water markets at an aggregated level use a general [SOURCE: Page_31]

equilibrium approach (Peterson et al., 2005; NWC, 2012). The first chapter of this thesis [SOURCE: Page_31]

attempts to measure the corresponding economic benefits in total agricultural production in the [SOURCE: Page_31]

context of Australian water markets through the use of a stochastic frontier model (Battese and [SOURCE: Page_31]

Coelli, 1995). We attempt to answer two questions. First, is the existence of water markets in a [SOURCE: Page_31]

region associated with a higher efficiency of agricultural production, through an increase in [SOURCE: Page_31]

water use efficiency? Second, does a higher water trade intensity imply higher efficiency gains? [SOURCE: Page_31]

A stochastic frontier model is applied to annual agricultural, climatic and market data in [SOURCE: Page_31]

Australia between 2011-12 and 2016-17. We first include a dummy variable noting the [SOURCE: Page_31]

existence of water markets in a given region within a conditional mean model of the stochastic [SOURCE: Page_31]

frontier’s residual inefficiency term, while controlling for various other factors. We find [SOURCE: Page_31]

evidence of a positive association between the existence of water markets and the efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_31]

agricultural production. Second, we replace the market existence dummy with a variable [SOURCE: Page_31]

measuring the intensity of temporary and permanent water trade, while controlling for the [SOURCE: Page_31]

potential selection bias through the use of generalized residuals (Gourieroux et al., 1987). We [SOURCE: Page_31]

find no evidence to support the hypothesis of a link between trade intensity and efficiency gains. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Overall, our results suggest a positive association between water markets and water use [SOURCE: Page_31]

efficiency. However, further research using more detailed data would be useful, in order to [SOURCE: Page_31]

strengthen this result. [SOURCE: Page_31]

In parallel with the literature dedicated to their economic benefits, various concerns have [SOURCE: Page_31]

been expressed in relation to the use of water markets as tools to reallocate water resources in [SOURCE: Page_31]

a context of water scarcity. Classical limits to the use of markets are often amplified in the case [SOURCE: Page_31]

30 [SOURCE: Page_31]

of water: as water is a massive resource, the costs raising from moving the resource can be high [SOURCE: Page_32]

(Turner et al., 2004). Concerns about potential externalities from water trade have been [SOURCE: Page_32]

expressed in the literature. Changes in stream flows, return flows and impacts on water’s area [SOURCE: Page_32]

of origin are frequently cited in that matter and can mitigate the gains obtained by buyers and [SOURCE: Page_32]

sellers (Garrido Fernandez, 2016). Furthermore, an externality often described by the literature [SOURCE: Page_32]

is the ‘stranded asset’ problem (Chong and Sunding, 2006; Heaney et al., 2006; Bjornlund, [SOURCE: Page_32]

2008; Frontier Economics et al., 2007): as irrigation water use requires heavy investment in [SOURCE: Page_32]

infrastructures, these infrastructures are often shared by different users. If one of these users [SOURCE: Page_32]

decides to sell his or her water entitlement, the maintenance costs of the infrastructure will be [SOURCE: Page_32]

supported by the remaining users, who generally compete with the leaver. When an irrigator [SOURCE: Page_32]

sells his rights permanently, the lack of maintaining work on his property can bring weeds and [SOURCE: Page_32]

increase disease risk for the neighbors (Frontier Economics et al., 2007; Bjornlund, 2008) or [SOURCE: Page_32]

even cause soil erosion (Chong and Sunding, 2006. In the Australian case, there is only limited [SOURCE: Page_32]

evidence to suggest that permanent water sales might generate significant economic losses in [SOURCE: Page_32]

the sellers’ area of origin (Grafton et al., 2016; Haensch et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_32]

The second chapter of this thesis focuses on a different market failure: insider trading. [SOURCE: Page_32]

In financial markets, insider trading is defined as the illegal trading in securities by individual [SOURCE: Page_32]

or firms possessing important non-public information (Meulbroek 1992). It is subject of intense [SOURCE: Page_32]

scrutiny from institutions such as the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. In water [SOURCE: Page_32]

markets, however, the topic had not been considered yet. In the Australian water markets, the [SOURCE: Page_32]

first regulation related to insider trading was established in 2014, through the introduction of [SOURCE: Page_32]

new trading rules. However, anecdotal evidence of insider trading practices (e.g. Hancock, [SOURCE: Page_32]

2008) has been reported, although not published in the peer-reviewed literature. For the first [SOURCE: Page_32]

time, we question the occurrence of insider trading practices in Australian water markets. To [SOURCE: Page_32]

do so, we look for informed price movements around significant market announcements [SOURCE: Page_32]

31 [SOURCE: Page_32]

implying changes in water prices, using 10 years of daily trade data and other known market [SOURCE: Page_33]

price determinants. Our times-series model detects evidence of informed price movements [SOURCE: Page_33]

between 2008 and 2014. This evidence weakens after 2014, with only one specification [SOURCE: Page_33]

showing significance at a 10% level. Informed price movements can either be related to insider [SOURCE: Page_33]

trading (Keown and Pinkerton, 1981; Moelbreuk, 1992; Maug et al., 2008), or to other factors [SOURCE: Page_33]

including an increased sophistication of trading behaviour, i.e. rational speculation (Jensen and [SOURCE: Page_33]

Ruback, 1983; Jarrell and Poulsen, 1989; Aspris et al., 2014; Gu and Kurov, 2018). Following [SOURCE: Page_33]

a reasoning suggested by Gupta and Misra (1988), the fact that informed price movements [SOURCE: Page_33]

almost disappear after the 2014 insider trading regulations could argue in favour of an ‘insider [SOURCE: Page_33]

trading’ interpretation. However, the increased sophistication and volumes exchanged in water [SOURCE: Page_33]

markets within the Murray-Darling Basin support a ‘rational speculation’ interpretation. In any [SOURCE: Page_33]

case, introducing insider trading regulations seems like a necessary water market policy, as the [SOURCE: Page_33]

potential for such practices clearly exist. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The third chapter considers another potential problem arising from the substitution [SOURCE: Page_33]

between ground- and surface water markets. To do so, we consider the case of Australia’s [SOURCE: Page_33]

Murrumbidgee river region, where both type of water resources are available. [SOURCE: Page_33]

A substitution effect can arise between ground- and surface water and affect the way [SOURCE: Page_33]

water markets work. In various contexts, it has been reported that irrigators could increase [SOURCE: Page_33]

groundwater consumption in order to compensate reductions in surface water rights (Zhang, [SOURCE: Page_33]

2007; Wheeler et al., 2016). This can lead to unexpected negative consequences of policies [SOURCE: Page_33]

aiming to reduce water use in the context of a drought or attempting to increase environmental [SOURCE: Page_33]

flows. Analysing 10 years (2008-2018) of monthly surface and groundwater market data, we [SOURCE: Page_33]

first use a vectorial autoregression with exogenous variables (VAR-X) to question the way [SOURCE: Page_33]

market information is assimilated in ground- and surface water markets. Specifically, we [SOURCE: Page_33]

32 [SOURCE: Page_33]

investigate the existence of a price leadership phenomenon. We find that past values of the [SOURCE: Page_34]

surface water market price significantly influence the groundwater market price, while the [SOURCE: Page_34]

opposite is not true. Accounting for this market interconnection, we then attempt to provide an [SOURCE: Page_34]

estimate of groundwater demand elasticity using water market data. We find that a 1% increase [SOURCE: Page_34]

in water price is associated with a 1.05 decrease in groundwater demand, suggesting that [SOURCE: Page_34]

irrigation groundwater in the Murrumbidgee is elastic but close to the unit elasticity. Overall, [SOURCE: Page_34]

our results stress the necessity to design regulations affecting both surface and groundwater [SOURCE: Page_34]

resources and markets in the Murrumbidgee river region: surface and groundwater markets are [SOURCE: Page_34]

interconnected, and the elasticity of groundwater demand suggests that irrigators are likely to [SOURCE: Page_34]

be responsive to any change in water price. Thus, the occurrence of a substitution effect in [SOURCE: Page_34]

Australia is highly probable, in case of regulations solely affecting surface water use. [SOURCE: Page_34]

In recent years, water markets have been tested (Zhang, 2007) or considered (Mellah, [SOURCE: Page_34]

2018) as potential tools in different contexts. However, water markets are embedded in various [SOURCE: Page_34]

context-dependent socio-political contexts, and do not always lead to efficient or effective [SOURCE: Page_34]

solutions for water management (Breviglieri et al., 2018). In this perspective, Wheeler et al. [SOURCE: Page_34]

(2017) presented a framework to analyse the reforms necessary to establish water markets in [SOURCE: Page_34]

different contexts, by identifying enablers and impediments to the use of markets in a given [SOURCE: Page_34]

context but also the potential costs and benefits associated. The fourth chapter applies the Water [SOURCE: Page_34]

Market Readiness Assessment (WMRA) to two French case studies: The Poitevin Marsh [SOURCE: Page_34]

Wetlands and the Neste system. In order to inform this study, 11 semi-structured interviews (6 [SOURCE: Page_34]

in the Neste system, and 5 in the Poitevin Marsh Basin) were held with key local stakeholders. [SOURCE: Page_34]

In each case, we first provide an overall presentation of the context’s geography, water [SOURCE: Page_34]

resources and hydrology. France is not a water scarce country in the sense of Falkenmark [SOURCE: Page_34]

(1999), and it is illegal to buy and sell water rights in the French water management system. [SOURCE: Page_34]

However, our case studies both experience recurrent episodes of physical water scarcity in [SOURCE: Page_34]

33 [SOURCE: Page_34]

summer months (June to September), when irrigation water needs are high and water demand [SOURCE: Page_35]

can exceed the available supply, suggesting the use of demand-management tools. In each case, [SOURCE: Page_35]

we address the current needs in relation to water management, and the framework in place to [SOURCE: Page_35]

answer those needs. Finally, we identify the potential enablers, impediments and benefits [SOURCE: Page_35]

associated with the implementation of water markets in our contexts. Overall, irrigation-related [SOURCE: Page_35]

water withdrawals in some parts of the Poitevin Marsh Basin have damaged the nearby and [SOURCE: Page_35]

hydrologically connected wetlands. In the recent years, a framework of demand management [SOURCE: Page_35]

measures has been established by a state authority (Etablissement Public du Marais Poitevin, [SOURCE: Page_35]

EPMP) in cooperation with irrigators representatives, alongside with supply augmentation [SOURCE: Page_35]

projects (‘substitution reservoirs’) that have been generating political controversy in some parts [SOURCE: Page_35]

of the Basin. Important impediments to the use of water markets identified include the absence [SOURCE: Page_35]

of a cap on water rights in the Basin, and a low social acceptability of the use of markets applied [SOURCE: Page_35]

to water resources (Kervarec, 2014). The existence of large hydrologically connected area [SOURCE: Page_35]

within the basin and a diversity of uses for irrigation water, however, suggest significant [SOURCE: Page_35]

benefits associated with the reallocation of water towards higher-valued uses. The Neste river [SOURCE: Page_35]

system is a surface water system including 18 rivers artificially replenished by a channel [SOURCE: Page_35]

diverting water from the Neste river, flowing from the Pyrenees mountains. The system [SOURCE: Page_35]

operator (Compagnie d’Aménagement des Coteaux de Gascogne, CACG) monitors water [SOURCE: Page_35]

diversions upstream in order to match the granted irrigation water rights each year. However, [SOURCE: Page_35]

since the establishment of a cap in 1992, water demand in the system has exceeded water [SOURCE: Page_35]

supply, leading to the establishment of a waiting list. Identified impediments to the use of water [SOURCE: Page_35]

markets include: the existing water management framework, based on an annual administrative [SOURCE: Page_35]

allocation of water extraction authorizations and forbidding water sales; the droit acquis [SOURCE: Page_35]

principle, implying a priority over water for irrigators who were allocated water rights in [SOURCE: Page_35]

previous years; and a cultural and political opposition to the use of markets applied to water [SOURCE: Page_35]

34 [SOURCE: Page_35]

resources. However, the extended available infrastructure would considerably facilitate water [SOURCE: Page_36]

transfers, and water markets could increase the flexibility of water demand by allowing [SOURCE: Page_36]

irrigators in the waiting list to buy water from irrigators growing lower-valued crops (such as [SOURCE: Page_36]

maze and other cereals), thus fostering a transition towards a higher water use efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Overall, by questioning the impact of water markets on water use efficiency (Chapter [SOURCE: Page_36]

1), the potential occurrence of insider trading (Chapter 2), the articulation between ground- and [SOURCE: Page_36]

surface water markets (Chapter 3) and their potential establishment in two French contexts [SOURCE: Page_36]

(Chapter 4), this thesis attempts to contribute to the empirical knowledge on water markets. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Insights from our results can be used by water policymakers and water management [SOURCE: Page_36]

stakeholders to inform the debates on the relevance of water markets in order to manage water [SOURCE: Page_36]

scarcity. [SOURCE: Page_36]

38 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Chapter 12: More market, more efficiency? Water market impacts on water use efficiency in the Australian agricultural sector [SOURCE: Page_40]

Abstract Water markets emerged as economic tools to deal with water scarcity. By reallocating existing water resources instead of using costly engineering projects to extend the existing supply, they are expected to increase the efficiency of water resources allocation. In this paper we question empirically the impacts of water markets on the efficiency of agricultural production, as defined by a stochastic frontier approach. Using regional data on agricultural production and climatic factors, we analyze the association between the existence of water markets, the intensity of water trade and the efficiency of agricultural production in Australia, home to some of the most developed water markets in the world. We find that the existence of water markets in a region is associated with a higher agricultural production efficiency, but no significant relationship is identified between the intensity of water trade and efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Keywords: Water markets; Stochastic frontier; Technical efficiency in agricultural production; Murray-Darling Basin. [SOURCE: Page_40]

JEL Classification: Q56; Q25; Q15 [SOURCE: Page_40]

2 This chapter refers to the article cowritten with Phu Nguyen-Van and Anne Rozan. [SOURCE: Page_40]

39 [SOURCE: Page_40]

### 1.     Introduction

In 2016, the World Economic Forum published the Global Risks Landscape (World [SOURCE: Page_41]

Economic Forum, 2015). Among all the risks presented the report rank water crisis as the most [SOURCE: Page_41]

important risk in terms of potential impact. Demographic pressures and the expected impacts [SOURCE: Page_41]

of climate change endanger the balance between water supply and demand, while the means of [SOURCE: Page_41]

increasing water supply become less and less cost efficient. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Water markets have emerged as potential tools to manage the demand for water. Such [SOURCE: Page_41]

markets can be defined as systems of rules and regulations that govern the buying, selling and [SOURCE: Page_41]

leasing of water use rights (Debaere et al., 2014). They can be used within the agricultural [SOURCE: Page_41]

sector; or they can allow inter-sectoral trades, as in the case of rural to urban transfers. The main [SOURCE: Page_41]

justification for the use of market mechanisms applied to water resources is that they are [SOURCE: Page_41]

expected to increase the overall efficiency of water use. Various forms of water use efficiency [SOURCE: Page_41]

can be considered. Technical efficiency refers to the ability of a production unit to operate on [SOURCE: Page_41]

the production frontier, i.e. to produce the maximum attainable output from each input level [SOURCE: Page_41]

(Coelli et al., 2005). In the case of water, water use technical efficiency thus implies to produce [SOURCE: Page_41]

the maximum possible amount for a fixed amount of water. More specifically, water use [SOURCE: Page_41]

allocative efficiency refers to water being allocated to where it generates the most income, while [SOURCE: Page_41]

other efficiency improvements in the use for water can be obtained through technology [SOURCE: Page_41]

(Wheeler et al., 2014). Water markets can foster allocative efficiency gains by redirecting water [SOURCE: Page_41]

from low-valued to higher valued uses (Dinar et al., 1997), and towards more productive [SOURCE: Page_41]

activities (Hodgson, 2006). They can also increase technical efficiency through the expansion [SOURCE: Page_41]

of water use by highly efficient new water users, adoption of water conserving technologies [SOURCE: Page_41]

and elimination of inefficient uses for water (Qureshi et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_41]

40 [SOURCE: Page_41]

Water markets have been established in various parts of the world. Examples of formal [SOURCE: Page_42]

(i.e. regulated and designed by a central authority) water markets include Australia (Grafton et [SOURCE: Page_42]

al., 2016), the western United States (Colby, 1990), Chile (Bitran et al., 2014), Spain (Palermo- [SOURCE: Page_42]

Hierro et al., 2015) and China (Zhang, 2007). Examples of informal (i.e. transactions happening [SOURCE: Page_42]

under limited or no scrutiny from the central authority) water markets can be found in India [SOURCE: Page_42]

(Mukherji, 2007) or Pakistan (Razzaq et al., 2019). Recently, the use of water markets in other [SOURCE: Page_42]

contexts has been considered, in order to face the challenges induced by water scarcity (Mellah, [SOURCE: Page_42]

2018; Wheeler et al., 2017). As water markets showed a low social acceptability in many [SOURCE: Page_42]

contexts as France (Figureau et al, 2015) or Italy (Zavalloni et al., 2014), informing the debate [SOURCE: Page_42]

on their empirical effects in the context of existing water markets is important. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Australia is a good case study for the use of water markets and economic tools in a [SOURCE: Page_42]

context of water scarcity. For the most part of its territory, Australia is facing significant [SOURCE: Page_42]

physical water scarcity (UN, 2012), as water resources development is approaching or has [SOURCE: Page_42]

already exceeded sustainable limits. The first water markets in Australia were established in the [SOURCE: Page_42]

early 1980s. Since then, water markets developed through progressive reforms while trading [SOURCE: Page_42]

volumes and irrigators’ participation in water markets have consistently increased through time [SOURCE: Page_42]

(Wheeler et al., 2014) and water markets in the Murray-Darling Basin have been described as [SOURCE: Page_42]

some of the most advanced water markets in the world (Grafton et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_42]

Different studies have attempted to demonstrate the economic benefits of water markets [SOURCE: Page_42]

in Australia, using a general equilibrium approach (Peterson et al., 2005; NWC, 2012) or [SOURCE: Page_42]

analyzing market bid and ask transactions at the micro-economic level (Brooks and Harris, [SOURCE: Page_42]

2008). However, no empirical study has considered the impacts of Australian water markets [SOURCE: Page_42]

on water use efficiency in practice. This study contributes to the empirical literature focusing [SOURCE: Page_42]

on water markets’ economic impacts by analyzing the relationship between water markets and [SOURCE: Page_42]

41 [SOURCE: Page_42]

the efficiency of water use in Australia. To do so, we used a stochastic frontier approach and [SOURCE: Page_43]

agricultural, climatic and market data between 2011 and 2017. Results from this study can be [SOURCE: Page_43]

used to inform the debates related to water markets performance in the Australian context and [SOURCE: Page_43]

to their potential adoption in other contexts. [SOURCE: Page_43]

2.     Literature review: water markets, expected benefits and impacts on water allocation efficiency [SOURCE: Page_43]

Markets emerged in the early literature as a feasible alternative to central water [SOURCE: Page_43]

management, described as limited in its ability to reallocate resources efficiently. In this [SOURCE: Page_43]

perspective, it focuses on the benefits expected from water transfers. Resorting to the private [SOURCE: Page_43]

sector in the field of water allocation decisions was for example advocated by Milliman (1959), [SOURCE: Page_43]

or Hartman and Seastone (1970). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Different studies dedicated to water market impacts simulate their existence to estimate [SOURCE: Page_43]

potential benefits. Vaux and Howitt (1984) considered the possibility of interregional water [SOURCE: Page_43]

transfers in California. Using a general equilibrium approach, the authors compared the costs [SOURCE: Page_43]

of such transfers to those of a gradual supply extension in water’s area of arrival to meet the [SOURCE: Page_43]

expected demand. The net benefits estimated from the transactions for buyers and sellers [SOURCE: Page_43]

amount to USD$66 million for the year 1980, and are expected to increase to USD$220 million [SOURCE: Page_43]

for the year 2020. Dinar and Letey (1991) estimate profit functions for farmers in the San [SOURCE: Page_43]

Joaquin Valley and consider the ability to trade water. Their results show better abilities to [SOURCE: Page_43]

invest in irrigation technology, decreased environmental pollution and a potential reallocation [SOURCE: Page_43]

of water towards the urban sector. Whittlesey and Willis (1998) analyze different alternatives [SOURCE: Page_43]

aimed at maintaining a minimum flow in the Walla Walla River Basin (State of Washington, [SOURCE: Page_43]

42 [SOURCE: Page_43]

USA). Using a model predicting agricultural behavior and stream flows in the basin, they find [SOURCE: Page_44]

markets as the most cost-effective approach. In Australia, Peterson et al. (2005) use general [SOURCE: Page_44]

equilibrium modelling to introduce the ability to trade water in the Australian economy. Their [SOURCE: Page_44]

results indicate important gains in Regional Domestic Product where water is traded with a [SOURCE: Page_44]

positive global impact on Australia’s GDP. This impact is described as particularly important [SOURCE: Page_44]

in years of drought (AUD$555 million in a year subjected to important water scarcity, and [SOURCE: Page_44]

AUD$201 million in a year subject to a relative abundancy), suggesting water markets might [SOURCE: Page_44]

alleviate the economic effect of droughts on the Australian economy. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Another section of the empirical literature attempting to measure water market’s [SOURCE: Page_44]

economic impacts analyzes actual transaction data at a microeconomic level. Hearne and Easter [SOURCE: Page_44]

(1997) analyzed transactions from water markets in Chile in the agricultural sector. They [SOURCE: Page_44]

compared water values determined by crop budget to prices included in water trades. They [SOURCE: Page_44]

found gains from trade varying from $1000 per share to $10 000 per share, depending on the [SOURCE: Page_44]

time and location of trades. In Australia, Bjornlund (1999) focused on transactions in two [SOURCE: Page_44]

specific areas of the Murray-Darling Basin and related them to the characteristics of the [SOURCE: Page_44]

irrigators involved. He found that water was in average moving towards more efficient buyers [SOURCE: Page_44]

that were also growing higher-valued crops. Brooks and Harris (2008) analyze data from three [SOURCE: Page_44]

trading zones in northern Victoria to determine consumer and producer surplus. They find [SOURCE: Page_44]

surpluses of $20 000 a week in the Greater Goulburn area. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Besides gains from trade, different empirical studies showed that water markets are used [SOURCE: Page_44]

by irrigators to improve their risk management. Farmers tend to be risk averse, under different [SOURCE: Page_44]

modalities (Nauges, Wheeler and Zuo, 2015); water markets can provide a reliable source of [SOURCE: Page_44]

water in times of needs or an additional source of income, thereby positively affecting farm [SOURCE: Page_44]

budgets (Wheeler et al., 2014). This has been shown empirically in Australia, particularly in [SOURCE: Page_44]

43 [SOURCE: Page_44]

the horticultural sector as permanent trees and vines could die if they are exposed to excessive [SOURCE: Page_45]

water stress (Loch et al., 2012). Besides, farmers experiencing a high variability in profits have [SOURCE: Page_45]

incentives to trade more on water markets (Cristi, 2007; Calatrava and Garrido, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_45]

Therefore, water markets are expected to improve farmer’s ability to manage their water related [SOURCE: Page_45]

risks (Zuo et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_45]

In parallel to these benefits, limits to the use of water markets that could prevent them [SOURCE: Page_45]

to improve efficiency in the use of water resources have been widely commented, often in a [SOURCE: Page_45]

context of limited market development. Classical limits to the use of markets are often amplified [SOURCE: Page_45]

in the case of water: as water is a massive resource, the costs raising from moving the resource [SOURCE: Page_45]

can be high (Turner et al., 2004). Some of the transaction costs related to water trading are [SOURCE: Page_45]

analyzed by Colby (1990) in the western United States, who concludes that the administrative [SOURCE: Page_45]

costs are not to be considered as ‘overly burdensome’ to transactions in the western United [SOURCE: Page_45]

States water markets around 1990. Moreover, the potential for externalities is important. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Changes in streamflows, return flows and impacts on water’s area of origin are frequently cited [SOURCE: Page_45]

in that matter and can mitigate the gains obtained by buyers and sellers (Garrido Fernandez, [SOURCE: Page_45]

2016). Furthermore, an externality often described by the literature is the ‘stranded asset’ [SOURCE: Page_45]

problem (Chong and Sunding, 2006; Heaney et al., 2006; Bjornlund, 2008; Frontier Economics [SOURCE: Page_45]

et al., 2007): as irrigation water use requires heavy investment in infrastructures, these [SOURCE: Page_45]

infrastructures are often shared by different users. If one of these users decides to sell his or her [SOURCE: Page_45]

water entitlement, the maintenance costs of the infrastructure will be supported by the [SOURCE: Page_45]

remaining users, who generally compete with the leaver. When an irrigator sells his rights [SOURCE: Page_45]

permanently, the lack of maintaining work on his property can bring weeds and increase disease [SOURCE: Page_45]

risk for the neighbors (Frontier Economics et al., 2007; Bjornlund, 2008) or even cause soil [SOURCE: Page_45]

erosion (Chong and Sunding, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_45]

44 [SOURCE: Page_45]

In relation to these market failures, different authors notice the very limited development [SOURCE: Page_46]

of water markets in terms of transactions between 1980 and 2000, mainly in the United States. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Some attempt to explain this phenomenon, as Rosen and Sexton (1993) who suggest the low [SOURCE: Page_46]

level of transactions is due to a lack of cooperation between market actors and operational [SOURCE: Page_46]

institutions actually owning the water. Saliba et al (1987) also question the limited amount of [SOURCE: Page_46]

water trade that was undertaken at the time, as compared to the potential benefits that could be [SOURCE: Page_46]

realized from it according to Vaux and Howitt (1984).                 They conclude that the [SOURCE: Page_46]

interdependencies and the public good characteristics of water make perfectly competitive [SOURCE: Page_46]

markets purely infeasible in practice. [SOURCE: Page_46]

This paper questions the ability of water markets to enhance water use efficiency. We [SOURCE: Page_46]

apply a panel data stochastic frontier model to regional Australian data on agricultural [SOURCE: Page_46]

production, climatic factors, and market variables in order to analyse the relationship between [SOURCE: Page_46]

water markets and efficiency of water use. [SOURCE: Page_46]

### 3.     Research hypotheses

In the Australian case, Bjornlund (1999) studies two specific areas presenting water [SOURCE: Page_46]

markets in the Murray-Darling Basin. He noticed that water was sold to more efficient farmers [SOURCE: Page_46]

in terms of water use and value generated. In a similar perspective, Wheeler et al. (2014) [SOURCE: Page_46]

reported that in the decade preceding 2014, water has been sold from annual crops (rice, cotton, [SOURCE: Page_46]

mixed farming) to horticultural crops, due to a more inelastic demand from vegetables and [SOURCE: Page_46]

perennial horticultural activities. These transactions implied a higher value-added use per unit [SOURCE: Page_46]

of water, considering marginal contribution of irrigation water to profit of $547/ML and [SOURCE: Page_46]

$61/ML, for horticulture and broadacre crops respectively (Nauges, Wheeler and Zuo, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_46]

45 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Generalizing these arguments at the regional level, we expect water market transactions from [SOURCE: Page_47]

lower-valued uses towards higher valued uses to increase the allocative efficiency overall. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Furthermore, we expect water markets to generate incentives from higher technical efficiency [SOURCE: Page_47]

users to buy water from lower technical efficiency users (Qureshi et al., 2009). Thus, we [SOURCE: Page_47]

formulate our first research hypothesis: [SOURCE: Page_47]

H$_1$: In regions where water markets have been established, water use efficiency (i.e. as [SOURCE: Page_47]

measured by the output value generated by one unit of water) of agricultural water use is [SOURCE: Page_47]

greater. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Water markets have developed in different scales throughout Australia. As described in [SOURCE: Page_47]

Figure 3, the MDB represents about 85% of all water market transactions in Australia. This is [SOURCE: Page_47]

related to the fact that the southern MDB represented a large hydrologically connected area, [SOURCE: Page_47]

unlike other parts of Australia, thus involving more potential users (Wheeler et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_47]

Besides, more active water markets imply an increased access to market infrastructure and [SOURCE: Page_47]

information. Therefore, we expect more active water markets such as markets within the [SOURCE: Page_47]

southern MDB to facilitate water use efficiency enhancement: [SOURCE: Page_47]

H$_2$: In regions where more active water markets are in place (i.e. more transactions occur), [SOURCE: Page_47]

water use efficiency should be higher. [SOURCE: Page_47]

This paper therefore questions the impact of water markets existence (H$_1$) and intensity [SOURCE: Page_47]

(H$_2$) on water use efficiency between 2011-12 and 2016-17 in Australia. To do so, we use a [SOURCE: Page_47]

stochastic frontier model at a regional level, and Australian data on market existence and [SOURCE: Page_47]

intensity, agricultural production, inputs, and climatic circumstances. [SOURCE: Page_47]

46 [SOURCE: Page_47]
