### 4.        Background: Agriculture and water markets in Australia

In the last decades, agriculture in Australia has been evolving under the impact of the [SOURCE: Page_48]

Millenium Drought, that occurred between 2002-03 and 2010-11. Our period of study begins [SOURCE: Page_48]

in 2011-12, in a relatively wet year marking the end of the Millenium Drought. Between 2011- [SOURCE: Page_48]

12 and 2017-18, the total Australian agricultural production value increased from 45.5 to 51.3 [SOURCE: Page_48]

billions AUD$: [SOURCE: Page_48]

Figure 1: Total agricultural production value in Australia, overall and by category, 2011-12 and 2016-17 [SOURCE: Page_48]

51.3 [SOURCE: Page_48]

45.5 Total agriculture [SOURCE: Page_48]

Broadacre crops (incl.cereals and hay) [SOURCE: Page_48]

Horticulture (incl. Fruits, vegetables and nuts)                                                                                    26.3 Livestock and related products 21.1 16.2 14.6 10.4 8.2 [SOURCE: Page_48]

2011-12                         2016-17 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Source: data from the Australian Bureau of Statistics (2013; 2018). Figures are in billion $AUD, corrected for inflation using constant 2011 prices. [SOURCE: Page_48]

The overall 11% increase in total production value is related to a strong development of [SOURCE: Page_48]

horticulture (the fruits and nuts industry in particular) and livestock, in spite of a decline the [SOURCE: Page_48]

production value associated with broadacre crops. In particular, cereal production value [SOURCE: Page_48]

dropped by about 15% (ABS, 2013; 2018). Thus, over our period of study, a decline in lower- [SOURCE: Page_48]

47 [SOURCE: Page_48]

valued crops (as cereals) and a development of higher valued crops (fruits and nuts, vegetables) [SOURCE: Page_49]

can be noted. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Water trade is, for logistical and juridical reasons, only possible between hydrologically [SOURCE: Page_49]

connected zones. As a consequence, there is not one national water market in Australia but [SOURCE: Page_49]

many trading zones based on hydrological connectivity. There are two types of market [SOURCE: Page_49]

transactions ongoing in the Australian water markets. Entitlement trading implies the exchange [SOURCE: Page_49]

of ongoing rights to exclusive access to a share of water otherwise known as permanent water. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Water allocations trading involves the exchange of a specific volume of water allocated to water [SOURCE: Page_49]

entitlements in a given season otherwise known as temporary water (Haensch et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_49]

During the fiscal year 2016-17, approximately 7500 GL were traded in Australian water [SOURCE: Page_49]

markets, representing a global turnover of AUD$131 millions (ABARES, 2018): [SOURCE: Page_49]

Figure 2: Temporary and permanent water trading prices and volumes in Australia, 2011-12 to 2016-17 [SOURCE: Page_49]

7000                                                                       3000 Mean yearly price (AUD/ML) [SOURCE: Page_49]

6000                                                                       2500 Volume traded (GL) [SOURCE: Page_49]

5000 2000 4000 1500 3000 1000 2000 [SOURCE: Page_49]

1000                                                                       500 [SOURCE: Page_49]

0                                                                        0 2011-12   2012-13     2013-14        2014-15   2015-16    2016-17 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Temporary trade vol.           Permanent trade vol. Temporary trade price          Permanent trade price [SOURCE: Page_49]

Source: data in ABARES (2018) [SOURCE: Page_49]

48 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Between 2011-12 and 2016-17, the volume of temporary water rights traded [SOURCE: Page_50]

(allocations) has consistently increased, while allocation prices fluctuated depending on [SOURCE: Page_50]

climatic circumstances. Besides, although the volume of permanent water rights traded [SOURCE: Page_50]

remained globally stable, the price of permanent water rights also increased, under the joint [SOURCE: Page_50]

influence of water scarcity and federal environmental water buyback programs. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Australian water markets historically involved irrigators as the most important actors of [SOURCE: Page_50]

water trade. Other actors involved in the process of exchanging water rights in Australia include [SOURCE: Page_50]

water brokers who provide market information and trading platforms to irrigators, federal and [SOURCE: Page_50]

national authorities who launched an important buyback program destined to restitute water to [SOURCE: Page_50]

the environment, and Irrigation Infrastructure Operators (IIOs) who typically own blocks of [SOURCE: Page_50]

water rights on behalf of irrigators, and redistribute these rights to their members. These actors [SOURCE: Page_50]

trade under federal, national and sometimes local regulations that have been progressively [SOURCE: Page_50]

adapted to increase irrigators’ participation to water markets. [SOURCE: Page_50]

The process historically establishing Australian water markets implied different steps. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Australian water markets were historically created around the agricultural sector in the Murray- [SOURCE: Page_50]

Darling Basin (MDB) (Maziotis et al., 2013) and it is where water trade is the most developed [SOURCE: Page_50]

and established, as illustrated by Figure 2: [SOURCE: Page_50]

49 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Figure 3: Proportion of Australian water trades (temporary and permanent) occurring within and outside the Murray-Darling Basin, 2016-17 [SOURCE: Page_51]

15% [SOURCE: Page_51]

9% Southern MDB [SOURCE: Page_51]

Northern MDB [SOURCE: Page_51]

Rest of Australia [SOURCE: Page_51]

76% [SOURCE: Page_51]

Source: data in ABARES (2018) [SOURCE: Page_51]

The basin involves parts of four Australian States: New South Wales, Victoria, [SOURCE: Page_51]

Queensland, and South Australia. It also includes the Australian Capital Territory. As the MDB [SOURCE: Page_51]

is subject to a climate favoring irrigated agriculture in comparison with the semi-arid climate [SOURCE: Page_51]

found northwards, agriculture covers 67% of its territory and represents about 40% of the total [SOURCE: Page_51]

Australian agricultural production. In 2015, the ratio of water demand to available water [SOURCE: Page_51]

resources was superior to 0.4 in most of the basin’s area, defining a “high” water stress (UN [SOURCE: Page_51]

Water report 20153). This and the prevalence of irrigated agriculture contribute to explain the [SOURCE: Page_51]

emergence of water markets in the area, as market for water resources potentially appears when [SOURCE: Page_51]

water demand approaches water availability (Debaere et al., 2014). The important volume of [SOURCE: Page_51]

water trade and the institutional framework fostering water markets in the southern MDB led [SOURCE: Page_51]

3 Source: UN Water report 2015, cited by Le Monde (March 20th, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_51]

50 [SOURCE: Page_51]

Grafton et al. (2011) to describe water markets in the Murray-Darling Basin as the most [SOURCE: Page_52]

advanced in the world. [SOURCE: Page_52]

In 1994, a cap was established on total water extraction in the Murray-Darling Basin. [SOURCE: Page_52]

This decision set the maximum level of water extraction in the basin at the 1994 extraction [SOURCE: Page_52]

level. This step caused a large increase in the water traded on the market, as additional needs [SOURCE: Page_52]

for water had to be fulfilled through the market. In 2004, the National Water Initiative (NWI) [SOURCE: Page_52]

precisely defined the generic terms ‘entitlement’ and ‘allocation’, common to all Australian [SOURCE: Page_52]

States, in an effort to unify the existence of many different water markets. It recognized the [SOURCE: Page_52]

need for better designed water markets to improve efficiency in water uses, in a context of low [SOURCE: Page_52]

participation to such markets. In 2007, the national ‘Water Act’ took additional steps to decrease [SOURCE: Page_52]

barriers to trade. In 2012, the Murray-Darling Basin Plan defined freedom of trade as the norm [SOURCE: Page_52]

and restrictions to trade in the Basin as exceptions, while establishing an authority in the Basin [SOURCE: Page_52]

responsible for the management of water resources. Towards the end of the Millenium drought, [SOURCE: Page_52]

the Australian Federal government dedicated AUD$3.1 billion to buy water rights from about [SOURCE: Page_52]

4500 willing irrigators in order to increase environmental flows in the MDB (Wheeler and [SOURCE: Page_52]

Cheeseman, 2013). Furthermore, the SRWUI program planned an additional AUD$5.8 billion [SOURCE: Page_52]

for water-related investments (Haensch et al., 2019). While the ‘buyback’ program reduced the [SOURCE: Page_52]

overall water use in some areas within the Murray-Darling Basin (Department of Land, Water [SOURCE: Page_52]

and Environment, 2018), investments aiming to develop on-farm water use efficiency (Haensch [SOURCE: Page_52]

et al., 2019) have been widely criticized by economists for various reasons, including higher [SOURCE: Page_52]

costs (Grafton et al., 2015) and a lack of accounting for return flows (Williams and Grafton, [SOURCE: Page_52]

2019) that could eventually increase consumptive use at the expense of environmental flows [SOURCE: Page_52]

(Loch and Adamson, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

### 5.     Data

The data analyzed in the next sections was obtained through different sources than can [SOURCE: Page_53]

be found in Appendix 1, along with descriptive statistics. The analysis was conducted on 54 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Australian Natural Resource Management Regions, as defined by the ABS, between the fiscal [SOURCE: Page_53]

years 2011-12 and 2016-17. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Agricultural data on water use and total agricultural area was extracted from the [SOURCE: Page_53]

Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS). As climate has been described as the most important [SOURCE: Page_53]

determinant of agricultural productivity, mainly through its influence on temperature and water [SOURCE: Page_53]

regimes (Kang et al, 2009), we included rainfall and potential evapotranspiration to the analysis. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Such variables were sourced from the Australian Bureau of Meteorology. As estimates of [SOURCE: Page_53]

rainfall or temperature at the NRM region level were not available, the data has been computed [SOURCE: Page_53]

based on the rainfall, latitude and temperature of 5 (temperature) to 10 individual stations across [SOURCE: Page_53]

each natural resource management region in Australia. In particular, the mean monthly [SOURCE: Page_53]

temperature was defined based on the mean maximum temperature (defined as the average of [SOURCE: Page_53]

daily maximum temperatures in a given month) and the mean minimum temperature (the [SOURCE: Page_53]

average of daily minimum temperatures in a given month), following Allen et al. (1998): [SOURCE: Page_53]

𝑀𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑀𝑎𝑥 𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 + 𝑀𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑀𝑖𝑛 𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 𝑀𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 = 2 [SOURCE: Page_53]

As potential evapotranspiration (PE) is often described as a better predictor than [SOURCE: Page_53]

temperature and is widely used in the literature (see Webb, 2006 or Blanc et al., 2014 for [SOURCE: Page_53]

examples), we computed the mean monthly PE based on the FAO Penman-Monteith equation, [SOURCE: Page_53]

as recommended by Allen et al. (1998). Some missing climatic data (wind speed, radiations, [SOURCE: Page_53]

etc.) was simulated according to Allen et al.’s advice. [SOURCE: Page_53]

52 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Finally, two market variables were defined at the region level, based on our two research [SOURCE: Page_54]

hypotheses. First, the existence of a functioning water market in region 𝑖 (i.e the occurrence of [SOURCE: Page_54]

at least one recorded transaction in the past) was coded through a binary variable., Second, we [SOURCE: Page_54]

used the volume of the additional water bought through water markets in each region as a proxy [SOURCE: Page_54]

for water trade intensity. [SOURCE: Page_54]

### 6.1.    A panel data stochastic frontier model

This section presents the stochastic production frontier model applied to our data. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Widely used in the literature dedicated to the analysis of technical efficiency in agriculture (see, [SOURCE: Page_54]

for example, Nguyen-Van and To-The (2016)), such frontiers have been previously applied to [SOURCE: Page_54]

the Australian grape production by Hughes (2011) or Coelli and Sanders (2012). Specifically, [SOURCE: Page_54]

we use the inefficiency frontier model for panel data presented by Battese and Coelli (1995). [SOURCE: Page_54]

We assume that output 𝑦𝑖𝑡 of farmer 𝑖, 𝑖 = 1,2, … , 𝑛 at time t, 𝑡 = 1,2, … , 𝑇 is subject to [SOURCE: Page_54]

random shocks 𝑣𝑖𝑡 and a degree of technical efficiency 𝑇𝐸𝑖𝑡 ∈ (0,1] : [SOURCE: Page_54]

𝑦𝑖 = 𝑓(𝑥𝑖𝑡 ; 𝛽)𝑇𝐸𝑖𝑡 exp(𝑣𝑖𝑡 ), 𝑖 = 1,2, … , 𝑛,                                                (1) [SOURCE: Page_54]

where 𝑥𝑖 is a Kx$_1$ vector of inputs, 𝛽 a Kx$_1$ vector of parameters to be estimated. [SOURCE: Page_54]

By assuming 𝑇𝐸𝑖𝑡 = exp(−𝑢𝑖𝑡 ) 𝑤𝑖𝑡ℎ 𝑢𝑖𝑡 ≥ 0, we obtain [SOURCE: Page_54]

𝑦𝑖 = 𝑓(𝑥𝑖𝑡 ; 𝛽) exp(𝑣𝑖𝑡 − 𝑢𝑖𝑡 ) , 𝑖 = 1,2, … , 𝑛,                                             (2) [SOURCE: Page_54]

Note that 𝑣𝑖𝑡 corresponds to the usual error term capturing random variation in output due to [SOURCE: Page_54]

factors beyond the control of producers and is assumed to be independent and identically [SOURCE: Page_54]

53 [SOURCE: Page_54]

distributed 𝑁(0, 𝜎𝑣2 ). Technical inefficiency is captured in 𝑢𝑖𝑡 which is assumed to be [SOURCE: Page_55]

independent and identically distributed non-negative truncations of the 𝑁(𝜇, 𝜎𝑢2 ) distribution. [SOURCE: Page_55]

The condition 𝑢𝑖𝑡 ≥ 0 ensures that all observations lie on or beneath the production frontier. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Applying log transformation to equation (2) we get [SOURCE: Page_55]

𝑙𝑛𝑦𝑖𝑡 = 𝑙𝑛𝑓(𝑥𝑖𝑡 ; 𝛽) + 𝑣𝑖𝑡 − 𝑢𝑖𝑡                                                               (3) [SOURCE: Page_55]

Note that, following Battese and Coelli (1995), we can specify a conditional mean model for [SOURCE: Page_55]

𝑢𝑖𝑡 as 𝜇 = 𝑧𝑖𝑡 𝛿 , or equivalently [SOURCE: Page_55]

𝑢𝑖𝑡 = 𝑧𝑖𝑡 𝛿 + 𝜀𝑖𝑡                                                                              (4) [SOURCE: Page_55]

where 𝑧𝑖𝑡 is a Jx$_1$ vector of explanatory variables. This vector includes the existence or intensity [SOURCE: Page_55]

of water markets in the considered NRM region and climatic variables (potential [SOURCE: Page_55]

evapotranspiration and Rainfall) and year dummies. 𝜀𝑖𝑡 is defined by the truncation of the [SOURCE: Page_55]

normal distribution with zero mean and variance 𝜎², such that 𝜀𝑖𝑡 ≥ −𝑧𝑖𝑡 𝛿 (see Battese and [SOURCE: Page_55]

Coelli, 1995, for details). [SOURCE: Page_55]

We simultaneously estimate the technical inefficiency 𝑢𝑖𝑡 and a conditional mean model [SOURCE: Page_55]

for 𝑢𝑖𝑡 using a vector of explanatory variables in order to analyze their respective impacts on [SOURCE: Page_55]

technical inefficiency. Note that we are especially interested in the sign of our market variable’s [SOURCE: Page_55]

parameter in this regard. [SOURCE: Page_55]

In this model, the technical efficiency of a given region i at time t is defined as the ratio [SOURCE: Page_55]

of its production to its corresponding production if the region used its inputs in a perfectly [SOURCE: Page_55]

efficient way. An estimation for technical efficiency 𝑇𝐸𝑖𝑡 = exp (−𝑢𝑖𝑡 ) can be given by (see [SOURCE: Page_55]

Battese and Coelli, 1993, for panel data, or Jondraw et al, 1982 for cross-sectional data): [SOURCE: Page_55]

𝜇 Ф[( ∗ )−𝜎∗ ]                 1 𝜎∗ 𝑇𝐸𝑖𝑡 = 𝐸{exp(−𝑢𝑖𝑡 ) |𝑣𝑖𝑡 − 𝑢𝑖𝑡 } = {         𝜇         } exp [−𝜇∗ + 2 𝜎∗ 2 ] , Ф( ∗ ) 𝜎∗ [SOURCE: Page_55]

54 [SOURCE: Page_55]

[ 𝑧𝛿𝜎𝑣2 −(𝑣𝑖𝑡 −𝑢𝑖𝑡 )𝜎2 ]          𝜎²𝑣 𝜎² where: 𝜇∗ =                           , 𝜎 ∗² =              , and Ф(. ) is the distribution function of the 𝜎𝑣2 +𝜎²                   𝜎𝑣 ²+𝜎² [SOURCE: Page_56]

standard normal distribution. [SOURCE: Page_56]

In order to compute the technical efficiency scores, we need to estimate the parameters [SOURCE: Page_56]

from the equations (3) and (4). This can be performed by Maximum Likelihood (See Battese [SOURCE: Page_56]

and Coelli, 1993 for a detailed equation of this model’s log-likelihood). However, in order to [SOURCE: Page_56]

estimate the vector of parameters 𝛽, we have to specify the 𝑓 function. As described with our [SOURCE: Page_56]

data, we consider 2 inputs in the production function (agricultural area and water use) and a [SOURCE: Page_56]

range of control variables including climatic variables (rainfall, temperature, potential [SOURCE: Page_56]

evapotranspiration) and other variables (existence of a water market, location within the [SOURCE: Page_56]

Murray-Darling Basin…). [SOURCE: Page_56]

Two different specification strategies were tested in this paper. We consider a Cobb- [SOURCE: Page_56]

Douglas function, i.e.: [SOURCE: Page_56]

ln 𝑓(𝑥𝑖𝑡 , 𝛽) = 𝛽0 + 𝛽1 ln 𝐴𝑅𝐸𝐴𝑖𝑡 + 𝛽2 ln 𝑊𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑖𝑡 , [SOURCE: Page_56]

and a more general function (Translog), i.e.: [SOURCE: Page_56]

ln 𝑓(𝑥𝑖𝑡 , 𝛽) = 𝛽0 + 𝛽1 ln 𝐴𝑅𝐸𝐴𝑖𝑡 + 𝛽2 ln 𝑊𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑖𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_56]

+ 𝛽3 (ln 𝐴𝑅𝐸𝐴𝑖𝑡 )2 + 𝛽4 (ln 𝑊𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑖𝑡 )2 + 𝛽5 ln 𝐴𝑅𝐸𝐴𝑖𝑡 ln 𝑊𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑖𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Akaike and Schwarz’s Bayesian Information criteria suggest a higher goodness of fit of the [SOURCE: Page_56]

Translog specification, confirmed by the likelihood ratio test. Consequently, the translog model [SOURCE: Page_56]

is used in the final analysis. [SOURCE: Page_56]

55 [SOURCE: Page_56]

### 6.2.   Endogeneity issues

It should be noted that the problem of endogenous regressors may arise with the [SOURCE: Page_57]

specification above. This issue is especially related to the water trade intensity variables. As we [SOURCE: Page_57]

can only observe production where a water market exists, a selection bias can arise. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Water markets tend to be established in areas suffering from high water stress [SOURCE: Page_57]

(Breviglieri et al., 2018). Thus, rainfall and potential evapotranspiration are likely predictors of [SOURCE: Page_57]

the existence of water markets in a region. Besides, historically, the Murray-Darling basin [SOURCE: Page_57]

hosted the first water markets in Australia and has developed an extended institutional [SOURCE: Page_57]

framework for the use of water markets (Grafton et al., 2016). The geographic location of a [SOURCE: Page_57]

region (inside or outside the MDB) can therefore influence the probability of finding a water [SOURCE: Page_57]

market. [SOURCE: Page_57]

We first performed a probit regression of our market existence variable on the set of [SOURCE: Page_57]

explanatory variables 𝑤, which includes 𝑧, as well as 𝑀𝐷𝐵𝑖 , 𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑓𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑖𝑡 and 𝑃𝑜𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐸𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑖𝑡 . [SOURCE: Page_57]

We then computed the generalized residuals (Gourieroux et al., 1987): [SOURCE: Page_57]

ĝ𝑟𝑖 = 𝑀𝑎𝑟𝑘𝑒𝑡𝑖 𝜆(𝑤𝑖′ ˆ ) − (1 − 𝑀𝑎𝑟𝑘𝑒𝑡𝑖 )𝜆(−𝑤𝑖′ ˆ ), [SOURCE: Page_57]

where 𝜆(. ) is the inverse Mills ratio, 𝜆(. ) =  (.)/(.) . Finally, we simultaneously estimate the [SOURCE: Page_57]

production frontier model in (3) and (4) as explained above, but with an additional regressor [SOURCE: Page_57]

corresponding to the estimated generalized residuals ĝ𝑟𝑖 . As recommended by Woolridge [SOURCE: Page_57]

(2014), we test the existence for endogeneity of 𝑀𝑎𝑟𝑘𝑒𝑡𝐸𝑥𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒𝑖𝑡 by using a robust t-test [SOURCE: Page_57]

for the significance of coefficient of ĝ𝑟𝑖 in this regression. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Market existence and trade intensity are closely related. In order to avoid collinearity, [SOURCE: Page_57]

we ran the analysis using market existence and trade intensity separately. The first analysis runs [SOURCE: Page_57]

the model while including a dummy for market existence in the mean conditional inefficiency [SOURCE: Page_57]

56 [SOURCE: Page_57]

model, in order to test the validity of H$_1$. The second analysis includes a continuous trade [SOURCE: Page_58]

intensity variable and residuals based on a linear regression of trade intensity on potential [SOURCE: Page_58]

determinants instead, in order to test the validity of H$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_58]

### 7.     Results

Results from the probit and linear regression estimations can be found in Appendix 2. [SOURCE: Page_58]

The robust t-test (Woolridge, 2014) shows that the generalized residual’s coefficient is not [SOURCE: Page_58]

significant at the 5% level, indicating that 𝑀𝑎𝑟𝑘𝑒𝑡𝐸𝑥𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 is not subject to endogeneity. In [SOURCE: Page_58]

other words, regression with endogenous Market gives qualitatively the same results than that [SOURCE: Page_58]

with exogenous Market. Therefore, generalized residuals were not used in the final regressions. [SOURCE: Page_58]

On average, high technical efficiency regions are subject to lower temperatures (-1.61 [SOURCE: Page_61]

to -2.53 °C) and potential evapotranspiration (-0.30 to -0.47 mm/day). They also produce [SOURCE: Page_61]

AUD$6.2 million to AUD$7.5 million more in terms of production value. Interestingly, the [SOURCE: Page_61]

probability of finding a water market is about 20% higher among the most efficient regions. [SOURCE: Page_61]

Agricultural area, rainfall or variables measuring the intensity of market transactions do not [SOURCE: Page_61]

appear to be clear distinctive characteristics. Thus, mean comparisons tests seem to support H$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_61]

(technical efficiency is higher where water markets can be found) but not H$_2$ (more transactions [SOURCE: Page_61]

imply a higher technical efficiency). Evidence supporting the validity of H$_1$ was also found in [SOURCE: Page_61]

the conditional mean inefficiency model: [SOURCE: Page_61]

Table 4: Results of the mean conditional inefficiency model using Market Existence, 2011-2017 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Total agricultural production Coefficient         Std. Error Technical inefficiency determinants Rainfall                                                          0.805*             0.421 Potential evapotranspiration                                      0.0158              0.42 Market existence                                                -2.249***            0.784 Intercept                                                         1.435*             0.838 𝜎𝑢                                                                -0.435             0.426 𝜎𝑣                                                              -3.541***            0.366 Observations                                                        302 AIC                                                                447.9 BIC                                                                492.4 Log-likelihood                                                    -211.9 Significance levels: * 0.1, ** 0.05, *** 0.01 [SOURCE: Page_61]

The existence of a water market is found to decrease technical inefficiency, confirming [SOURCE: Page_61]

the mean comparison test results and supporting the validity of H$_1$. Potential evapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_61]

is expected to increase water use, thus decreasing technical efficiency if water use is set [SOURCE: Page_61]

constant. However, it also provides clear incentives for technical efficiency investments. In our [SOURCE: Page_61]

case, no clear association was found between potential evapotranspiration and technical [SOURCE: Page_61]

efficiency. Rainfall has an ambiguous impact on agricultural productivity: on one side, it [SOURCE: Page_61]

Overall, our results are in line the expectations formulated by the literature on water [SOURCE: Page_64]

estimation between 2010 and 2017. However, we find no association between a higher intensity [SOURCE: Page_64]

(the existence of water markets in a region is associated with a higher technical efficiency) but [SOURCE: Page_64]

not H$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_64]

### 8.        Robustness tests

In order to improve the validity of our results, different robustness and sensitivity tests [SOURCE: Page_64]

were conducted. First, we considered a potential bias in relation to the collection methodology [SOURCE: Page_64]

for some of the variables used. All variables sourced from the ABS have been collected by [SOURCE: Page_64]

random sampling. However, in 2015, the ABS has changed its data collection methodology by [SOURCE: Page_64]

excluding economic agents whose (agricultural) income is under AUD$40 000 from the [SOURCE: Page_64]

collection process (this threshold was AUD$5000 previously). Thus, the two last years of our [SOURCE: Page_64]

sample are potentially affected by this methodological change. In order to avoid the potential [SOURCE: Page_64]

bias arising from this new random sampling methodology, Kruskall Wallis mean comparison [SOURCE: Page_64]

tests by groups were applied to all variables sourced from the ABS, in order to see whether a [SOURCE: Page_64]

significant difference could exist. All tests were negative (no significant mean difference), [SOURCE: Page_64]

except for the extra volume of permanent water bought, where a significant increase in trading [SOURCE: Page_64]

63 [SOURCE: Page_64]

was detected after 2015. We suggest that this difference does not undermine our results in a [SOURCE: Page_65]

major way for two reasons: first, an alternative proxy free of the previous problem (no [SOURCE: Page_65]

significant mean difference after 2015) used instead: the total cost of extra permanent water [SOURCE: Page_65]

bought. The estimation generated identical results (no significance of permanent water trade [SOURCE: Page_65]

intensity and parameters of a similar magnitude). Second, the results of both estimations related [SOURCE: Page_65]

to permanent water trade intensity are similar to those using temporary trade, that show no [SOURCE: Page_65]

significant mean difference after 2015. [SOURCE: Page_65]

VIFs were generated using linear regressions after the frontier estimation. No VIF under [SOURCE: Page_65]

5 was detected in the conditional mean inefficiency model. However, the squared input and [SOURCE: Page_65]

interaction terms related to the Translog specifications generated high VIFs. Regressions [SOURCE: Page_65]

excluding squared input terms and the Translog interaction (Cobb-Douglas specifications) were [SOURCE: Page_65]

also tested. Similar results were found. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Finally, in order to check the influence of potential outliers, Cooks distances and [SOURCE: Page_65]

leverages were generated following linear regressions of technical inefficiency scores. No [SOURCE: Page_65]

Cook’s distance over 1 was detected. However, we ran the analysis while excluding [SOURCE: Page_65]

observations whose leverage values were found over the (K+1/N) threshold. The results were [SOURCE: Page_65]

qualitatively the same. [SOURCE: Page_65]

### 9.     Conclusion

The purpose of this paper was to question the effect of water markets on water use [SOURCE: Page_65]

efficiency in Australia, as a complementary analysis to the General Equilibrium approach [SOURCE: Page_65]

(Peterson et al., 2005) that has generally been used in that matter. To our knowledge, this paper [SOURCE: Page_65]

is the first to use a stochastic frontier approach and regional data in order to measure the impact [SOURCE: Page_65]

64 [SOURCE: Page_65]

of water market’s existence and trade intensity in Australia. We gathered a database crossing [SOURCE: Page_66]

agricultural data from the Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS), climatic data from the Bureau [SOURCE: Page_66]

of Meteorology (BoM) and market data from the National Water Commission (NWC). We find [SOURCE: Page_66]

a positive impact of the existence of water markets in a NRM region on technical efficiency in [SOURCE: Page_66]

the Australian agricultural sector, between 2011 and 2017. However, we found no evidence [SOURCE: Page_66]

showing that a higher trade intensity would be associated to a higher water use efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Important limits to our results have to be stated. We measured market impacts at an [SOURCE: Page_66]

aggregated regional level: it would be interesting to conduct the analysis at a farm level, which [SOURCE: Page_66]

would require more detailed data. Due to limited data availability, we do not control for inputs [SOURCE: Page_66]

such as labor or capital other than agricultural area. Further empirical research on this topic in [SOURCE: Page_66]

other contexts, using more detailed data where available, could confirm our results and would [SOURCE: Page_66]

be of interest, given the fact that the economic benefits associated with water markets will be [SOURCE: Page_66]

of major interest to the future debates on water markets and policy. [SOURCE: Page_66]

65 [SOURCE: Page_66]

Chapter 24: Is there insider trading in Australian water markets? [SOURCE: Page_75]

Abstract Insider trading is a much studied form of market manipulation in the financial markets literature. However, studies addressing the issue of insider trading in resource markets, and in particular water markets, are rare. This study investigates the occurrence of insider trading practices around important water market allocation announcements in the Goulburn temporary water market trading zone in the Murray-Darling Basin, Australia, which is one of the largest and longest operating water market districts in the world. Nine years of daily water allocation volume and price transactions between 2008 and 2017 are modelled, with some evidence of abnormal price movements in the three or five days preceding water allocation announcements, especially before the introduction of insider trading rules in 2014. However, although the results do provide some very weak statistically significant evidence to suggest insider trading may still be present in Murray-Darling Basin water markets post 2014, it is just as feasible that our results may also reflect an increased sophistication of trader behaviour over time. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Keywords: Insider trading; Murray-Darling Basin; Water allocations; Water markets. [SOURCE: Page_75]

4 This chapter refers to the article published in Australian Journal of Agricultural and Resource Economics 63 (2019) cowritten with Alec Zuo and Sarah Ann Wheeler. [SOURCE: Page_75]

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

### 1.     Introduction

Where there are strong institutions and secure property rights, water markets have long [SOURCE: Page_76]

been promoted by economists as one of the most efficient ways to share water (Brooks and [SOURCE: Page_76]

Harris 2008; Peterson et al. 2005; Randall 1981; Vaux and Howitt 1984; Crase et al. 2000; [SOURCE: Page_76]

Wheeler et al. 2017). Australia has established the most extended water markets in the world [SOURCE: Page_76]

(Grafton et al. 2011; 2016), especially in the Murray-Darling Basin (MDB), and within this [SOURCE: Page_76]

area markets have increasingly been used as means to reallocate water among irrigation [SOURCE: Page_76]

enterprises as well as other consumptive users to the environment (Qureshi et al. 2007; Lee et [SOURCE: Page_76]

al. 2009; Quiggin et al. 2010). The development and adoption of water markets as a key [SOURCE: Page_76]

instrument for water reallocation in Australia have played an integral role in water policy [SOURCE: Page_76]

implementation (Wittwer and Griffith 2011); farm risk management (Brooks and Harris 2008; [SOURCE: Page_76]

Connor et al. 2012; Nauges et al. 2015); farm and structural adjustment within the irrigation [SOURCE: Page_76]

sector (Bjornlund and McKay 1998); and off-farm (non-production) income supplementation [SOURCE: Page_76]

(Loch et al. 2012). Despite markets’ key importance in improving efficiency, there has been [SOURCE: Page_76]

limited academic analysis of the impact of institutional and policy changes in water markets, [SOURCE: Page_76]

nor how a number of well-known financial market risks and behaviour (such as insider trading) [SOURCE: Page_76]

have potentially influenced water market outcomes. The main reason for this lack of [SOURCE: Page_76]

investigation is the paucity of water market data (and its time coverage). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Since their early implementation in the 1980s, water markets in the MDB have evolved [SOURCE: Page_76]

considerably. Water trade in the Goulburn-Murray Irrigation District (GMID), the current [SOURCE: Page_76]

biggest and most active trading zone in the MDB, was initially relatively low (Tural et al. 2005). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Since then, markets have been increasingly adopted by farmers. By the year 2002-03 more than [SOURCE: Page_76]

60% of all farm businesses had been active on either buying or selling water (Bjornlund 2006). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Trading volumes consistently increased in the following years under the impacts of the [SOURCE: Page_76]

75 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Millennium Drought (common time-frame of 2002-03 to 2009-10) and the progressive [SOURCE: Page_77]

reduction of trading restrictions. In the southern MDB, approximately half of all irrigators had [SOURCE: Page_77]

made at least one water entitlement trade, whereas 78% had conducted at least one water [SOURCE: Page_77]

allocation trade by 2015-16 (Grafton and Wheeler 2018). In the GMID, temporary water [SOURCE: Page_77]

markets have been found to generate significant efficiency gains (Brooks and Harris 2008). In [SOURCE: Page_77]

addition to that, water markets in the MDB have become increasingly sophisticated with the [SOURCE: Page_77]

emergence of additional market products, such as future contracts and leasing (Bayer and Loch [SOURCE: Page_77]

2017), evidence of price clustering (Brooks and Harris 2012; Brooks et al. 2013), as well as [SOURCE: Page_77]

improved market information sources from the Australian Bureau of Meteorology (BoM). [SOURCE: Page_77]

These findings and developments underline the fact that water markets are similar to financial [SOURCE: Page_77]

markets. As such, they may be subject to the same market failures. Particularly, where there is [SOURCE: Page_77]

a context of asymmetrical information, diverse forms of market manipulations might affect [SOURCE: Page_77]

water markets. [SOURCE: Page_77]

One of the most studied and debated financial market manipulations is insider trading. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Insider trading is defined as the illegal trading in securities by individual or firms possessing [SOURCE: Page_77]

important non-public information (Meulbroek 1992). On the one hand, insider trading can [SOURCE: Page_77]

undermine participation in a market and decrease liquidity (Leland 1992), and socially it is [SOURCE: Page_77]

considered unfair if some people lose when other people win from having inside knowledge. [SOURCE: Page_77]

On the other hand, it has been argued that insider trading allows for all information to be [SOURCE: Page_77]

reflected in a security’s price, and overall increases market efficiency as prices start moving [SOURCE: Page_77]

quicker than they would have otherwise (Grossman and Stiglitz 1976). Hence, different [SOURCE: Page_77]

countries have different rules in regard to the legality of insider trading (Bhattacharya and [SOURCE: Page_77]

Daouk 2002). In Australia it is an offence under the Corporations Act 2001 (Commonwealth [SOURCE: Page_77]

of Australia 2001, p.202-220) to trade or communicate inside information. [SOURCE: Page_77]

76 [SOURCE: Page_77]

In the case of Australian temporary water markets, which have been in operation since [SOURCE: Page_78]

the early 1980s, a number of authors have reported anecdotal evidence of insider trading [SOURCE: Page_78]

occurring (Hancock 2008; NWC 2011; BDO 2014). The potential for insider trading in [SOURCE: Page_78]

Australian water markets arose because traditionally information was available to a number of [SOURCE: Page_78]

people regarding important upcoming fortnightly announcements that explicitly changes the [SOURCE: Page_78]

amount of water available for irrigation, which directly impacts on the demand for, and supply [SOURCE: Page_78]

of, water in the water market and consequently the equilibrium price for that water (NWC, [SOURCE: Page_78]

2011). [SOURCE: Page_78]

Two types of water rights are traded in the MDB: water entitlements (or permanent [SOURCE: Page_78]

water rights), which are an exclusive access to a share of the water resources within an area, [SOURCE: Page_78]

and water allocations (or temporary water rights), which are the actual volume (or allocations) [SOURCE: Page_78]

of water assigned to the permanent water access entitlement. Allocations vary depending on [SOURCE: Page_78]

water availability and expected inflows, and also depend on the reliability of the water [SOURCE: Page_78]

entitlements owned. Announcements are made fortnightly regarding the volume of water [SOURCE: Page_78]

represented by water allocations, from the start of the water season. The total volume of water [SOURCE: Page_78]

announced to be available is called the water allocation level and is expressed as a percentage [SOURCE: Page_78]

of the water provided from water entitlements (Wheeler et al. 2008). [SOURCE: Page_78]

If water is scarce at the beginning of the season, each fortnight authorities can either [SOURCE: Page_78]

announce an increase in water allocations, i.e., an increase in the size of the pool of available [SOURCE: Page_78]

water, or they can announce that water allocations remain unchanged, i.e., no additional water [SOURCE: Page_78]

is made available. In Victoria, water allocation announcements are currently made by the [SOURCE: Page_78]

Northern Victoria Resource Manager (NVRM). Allocation announcements change water [SOURCE: Page_78]

supply and/or demand: if water allocations increase, farmers receive additional water that they [SOURCE: Page_78]

can use for irrigation purposes. This may decrease the need to buy water on the market, and [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

therefore decreases water demand overall. Thus, allocation announcements can have major [SOURCE: Page_79]

impacts on water price (Wheeler et al. 2008). [SOURCE: Page_79]

The National Water Commission (2011, p.72) stated that there was ‘a need for all market [SOURCE: Page_79]

participants to have equivalent opportunity of access to market-sensitive information and at the [SOURCE: Page_79]

same time, to guard against insider trading or other situations in which some traders gain a [SOURCE: Page_79]

market advantage by having prior access to allocation decisions’. Hence, water allocation [SOURCE: Page_79]

announcements represent a key area where there can be a leak of insider information. Various [SOURCE: Page_79]

irrigation organizations over time have put different voluntary codes in place to deal with [SOURCE: Page_79]

insider information. For example, in 2007 the irrigation organization Sunwater introduced a [SOURCE: Page_79]

voluntary code of conduct including ring-fencing practices to prevent the leakage of market- [SOURCE: Page_79]

sensitive information (BDO 2014). Officially, insider trading only became illegal after the new [SOURCE: Page_79]

trading rules for the MDB Plan in July 1st, 2014 (MDBA 2014) were introduced. [SOURCE: Page_79]

To date there has not been a comprehensive study that has sought to investigate if [SOURCE: Page_79]

evidence of insider trading can be detected from water market data, in Australia or around the [SOURCE: Page_79]

world, despite legislation having been put in place in part to address the issue. Questioning the [SOURCE: Page_79]

occurrence of insider trading on water markets is particularly important, as such markets are [SOURCE: Page_79]

less liquid than financial markets. Therefore, water market trades are less diluted and the [SOURCE: Page_79]

consequences of insider trading are potentially greater. This study investigates the occurrence [SOURCE: Page_79]

of insider trading within Australian water markets in relation to water allocation announcements [SOURCE: Page_79]

and any observed price movements, in two key time-periods (before and after the 2014 MDB [SOURCE: Page_79]

trading rules on insider trading). The findings of this study provide insights for institutional [SOURCE: Page_79]

property rights, monitoring and governance for resource markets and for other jurisdictions [SOURCE: Page_79]

around the world that are considering implementing water markets. [SOURCE: Page_79]

### 2.1.    Insider trading and financial markets

As already commented, it is important to note that trading as an insider 5 is not [SOURCE: Page_80]

necessarily illegal. In most cases, insiders are allowed to trade on the market. Transactions made [SOURCE: Page_80]

by insiders can become illegal if insiders use important, non-public information to inform their [SOURCE: Page_80]

trades (Meulbroek 1992). Before 1990, the issue of insider trading was mostly ignored. In 1998, [SOURCE: Page_80]

out of the 103 countries with stock markets, 87 of them had insider trader regulation, although [SOURCE: Page_80]

only 38 of them regulated insider trading rules (Bhattacharya and Daouk 2002). [SOURCE: Page_80]

The potential impacts of insider trading have been widely debated in the financial [SOURCE: Page_80]

literature, although empirical analyses are often lacking due to the absence of reliable data. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Studying litigation cases on actual insider trading potentially suffers from selection bias [SOURCE: Page_80]

(Bhattacharya and Daouk 2002). The downside of insider trading includes the fact that it may [SOURCE: Page_80]

increase the cost of issuing new shares, as investors demand a premium over the risk-free rate [SOURCE: Page_80]

to compensate for the risk of trading with informed traders in the future (Grégoire and Huang [SOURCE: Page_80]

2008), and decrease market confidence and hence market liquidity (Leland 1992; Fishe and [SOURCE: Page_80]

Robe 2004). From a social equity point of view, insider trading is seen as benefitting insiders [SOURCE: Page_80]

and owners of investment projects (e.g. the wealthy and powerful), and harming outside [SOURCE: Page_80]

investors and liquidity traders. As a final potential benefit, insider trading might increase real [SOURCE: Page_80]

investment as it improves the market incorporation of information and thus reduces risk for [SOURCE: Page_80]

investors (Leland 1992). [SOURCE: Page_80]

Insider trading can impact stock prices, trading volumes or trade count ahead of [SOURCE: Page_80]

significant announcements. Kyle (1985) elaborated a theoretical trading model in the presence [SOURCE: Page_80]

5 The term ‘insider’ can refer to a variety of situations. In the water markets case, we refer to any individual or entity possessing information about the content of a future announcement that is unrelated to rational speculation. [SOURCE: Page_80]

79 [SOURCE: Page_80]

of private information and found that trade made by informed parties moved stock prices, which [SOURCE: Page_81]

was also found by Chakravarty (2001). As information leakage moves stock prices in the same [SOURCE: Page_81]

direction as the announcement (Sinha and Gadarowski 2010), it is possible to analyse market [SOURCE: Page_81]

returns to investigate the existence of insider trading. Some studies therefore use abnormal [SOURCE: Page_81]

returns ahead of announcement as potential evidence of insider trading. Based on litigation [SOURCE: Page_81]

cases from the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), Meulbroek (1992) models [SOURCE: Page_81]

stocks prices in presence of insider trading and finds a mean 3% abnormal return due to insider [SOURCE: Page_81]

trading activity ahead of significant announcements. Keown and Pinkerton (1981) analyse [SOURCE: Page_81]

abnormal returns ahead of merger announcements. Olmo et al. (2011) used an asset pricing [SOURCE: Page_81]

model and developed structural break tests in the intercept in order to detect insider trading [SOURCE: Page_81]

activity. They applied it to 250 announcements in the FTSE 350 index and found suspicious [SOURCE: Page_81]

breaks for 38 of them. Park and Lee (2010) defined three detection criteria based on parameter [SOURCE: Page_81]

characteristics estimated from an autoregressive moving-average time series model of stock [SOURCE: Page_81]

returns, and suggested that 19% of major shareholder transactions in the Korean Exchange are [SOURCE: Page_81]

based on undisclosed information. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Detection of insider trading in stock markets can therefore be undertaken by analysing [SOURCE: Page_81]

abnormal returns related to price movements ahead of major announcements (Keown and [SOURCE: Page_81]

Pinkerton 1981; Meulbroek 1992; Park and Lee 2010). However, interpreting the occurrence [SOURCE: Page_81]

of pre-announcement abnormal price movements as insider trading evidence implies that such [SOURCE: Page_81]

price movements cannot be caused by other factors than insider trading. Several studies have [SOURCE: Page_81]

been published on the link between price movements ahead of major market announcements [SOURCE: Page_81]

and insider trading, particularly in the case of corporate take-overs. Keown and Pinkerton [SOURCE: Page_81]

(1981) found that half of the price movements related to take-over announcements in their US [SOURCE: Page_81]

sample happens before the actual announcement. They interpret it as prima facie evidence of [SOURCE: Page_81]

insider trading. A similar point is made by Meulbroek (1992). Gupta and Misra (1988) analysed [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

a major insider trading scandal attracting considerable public concern around the topic of [SOURCE: Page_82]

insider trading. Under the assumption that insider trading behaviour should therefore decrease, [SOURCE: Page_82]

price run-ups before and after the scandal were analysed, with no significant differences found. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Bernile et al. (2016) find significant evidence of informed trading 30 minutes before important [SOURCE: Page_82]

macro-announcements were made, in a context where information was provided to selected [SOURCE: Page_82]

news organizations ahead of the announcements under embargo agreements. They interpret this [SOURCE: Page_82]

result as a sign of information leakage or superior forecasting ability based on public [SOURCE: Page_82]

information. Indeed, other public aspects such as media speculation and the friendly or hostile [SOURCE: Page_82]

characteristic of the takeover (Jarrell and Poulsen 1989) have consequently been shown to [SOURCE: Page_82]

influence pre-announcement price run-ups, which argue for a market anticipation theory [SOURCE: Page_82]

(Aspris et al. 2014). Jensen and Ruback (1983) find that market speculation of industry [SOURCE: Page_82]

dynamics can explain pre-announcement price run-ups related to take-overs. Aspris et al. [SOURCE: Page_82]

(2014) control for different major market announcements for 450 takeover announcements and [SOURCE: Page_82]

find toehold investments and their timing explain a significant part of pre-announcement run- [SOURCE: Page_82]

ups. The influence of other public information is also stressed by Gu and Kurov (2018), who [SOURCE: Page_82]

find that public forecasts made by analysts with a superior historical forecasting ability explain [SOURCE: Page_82]

a significant part of the pre-announcement price run-ups before major gas inventory [SOURCE: Page_82]

announcements. However, as noted by Beny and Seyhun (2012), public rumours and/or public [SOURCE: Page_82]

information sources might be synonymous with insider trading as traders obtaining illegal [SOURCE: Page_82]

information are incentivized to spread rumours in the financial press to increase the value of [SOURCE: Page_82]

their position. Maug et al. (2008) analysed price run-ups in 48 countries and 18,752 takeover [SOURCE: Page_82]

announcements. They found that passing insider trading legislation affects the pre-bid stock [SOURCE: Page_82]

price run-ups: these run-ups explain less of the total price movements once insider trading [SOURCE: Page_82]

legislation is in place. This would suggest that at least some of the pre-announcement price [SOURCE: Page_82]

movements are caused by insider trading practices. [SOURCE: Page_82]

### 2.2.     Other influences on water prices

Price and volume determinants of water markets in the MDB have been well [SOURCE: Page_83]

documented in the literature, particularly for the GMID. There are a number of main price [SOURCE: Page_83]

determinants; including: i) rainfall which decreases water demand as farmers substitute [SOURCE: Page_83]

irrigation water for rainfall, while irrigation water is often acquired through markets (Bjornlund [SOURCE: Page_83]

and Rossini 2004; Bjornlund 2006; Wheeler et al. 2012);6 ii) water allocations or dam storages [SOURCE: Page_83]

which represent (or provide a proxy of) the total amount of seasonal water received, where an [SOURCE: Page_83]

increase in either negatively impacts water prices, as it increases water supply (Brennan 2006; [SOURCE: Page_83]

Wheeler et al. 2008; Loch et al. 2012); iii) irrigation agriculture output prices are usually [SOURCE: Page_83]

positive significant drivers of water prices (Brennan 2006; Wheeler et al. 2012) through their [SOURCE: Page_83]

impact on farm income; iv) some irrigation commodity input prices where certain inputs can [SOURCE: Page_83]

be used as a substitute for water, e.g. such as purchasing feed barley instead of using water to [SOURCE: Page_83]

grow pasture for dairy farmers or rising electricity prices can reduce irrigation water demand, [SOURCE: Page_83]

especially groundwater pumping; v) output dryland commodity prices (e.g. cattle for dairy) as [SOURCE: Page_83]

a land substitute for irrigation can be a negative driver of water prices; vi) policy intervention [SOURCE: Page_83]

can also positively or negatively impact market prices (Tisdell 2010; Loch et al. 2012); and vii) [SOURCE: Page_83]

macroeconomic drivers such as exchange rates and GDP growth (Bjornlund and Rossini 2004) [SOURCE: Page_83]

can influence water market prices. [SOURCE: Page_83]

To summarise, detecting abnormal price movements ahead of significant market [SOURCE: Page_83]

announcements while controlling for other influences is one necessary condition to detect the [SOURCE: Page_83]

6 In this perspective, drought (e.g. lack of rainfall) has been identified as a significant driver of water prices, as well as the time within the water season (Wheeler et al., 2008). [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

possibility of insider trading practices in the water market. The following section outlines more [SOURCE: Page_84]

water market background and formulates insider trading hypotheses. [SOURCE: Page_84]

### 3.       Water Markets Background

Water markets have been present in Australia since their early implementation in the [SOURCE: Page_84]

1980s. Following the establishment of a cap on total water extractions in 1995, the National [SOURCE: Page_84]

Water Initiative and wide-scale government involvement in the market in the 2000s and the [SOURCE: Page_84]

MDB Plan in 2012, markets have become a common tool of water policy management in [SOURCE: Page_84]

Australia (Wheeler et al. 2014). Insider trading in MDB water markets was not officially [SOURCE: Page_84]

regulated before 2014. On July 1st, 2014, new trading rules for the MDB formally forbid the [SOURCE: Page_84]

use of undisclosed information in relation to allocation announcements in the MDB (MDBA [SOURCE: Page_84]

2014). [SOURCE: Page_84]

As discussed previously, fortnightly announcements are made throughout the water year [SOURCE: Page_84]

that can either increase the percentage of water allocations attached to particular water [SOURCE: Page_84]

entitlements or leave it unchanged. This is of particular importance in periods of water scarcity; [SOURCE: Page_84]

in the drought year 2008-09, many water allocations to high security entitlements in a number [SOURCE: Page_84]

of districts started at 0% and only reached up to 35% at the end of the year with many fortnightly [SOURCE: Page_84]

announcements during the season keeping the allocation % unchanged. At the start of the year, [SOURCE: Page_84]

farmers had limited information in regard to what the final water allocation may be, nor when [SOURCE: Page_84]

it may next increase. When faced with this situation, farmers have a choice to use water [SOURCE: Page_84]

allocations from their water entitlements; if this is not enough, within the southern MDB they [SOURCE: Page_84]

can enter the water market to buy water (either temporary or permanent) - see Wheeler et al. [SOURCE: Page_84]

(2014) for more discussion on the Australian water market. Consequently, changes in water [SOURCE: Page_84]

allocations can have a direct impact on the supply and demand of water on the temporary market [SOURCE: Page_84]

83 [SOURCE: Page_84]

(Brennan 2006; Loch et al. 2012). Within the water allocation market, there are sellers of water [SOURCE: Page_85]

(usually farmers who are not farming that year or have seasonal surplus water that is not [SOURCE: Page_85]

needed), and buyers of water (usually those who do not own any water entitlements or do not [SOURCE: Page_85]

have enough water allocations to use at a point in time and need to enter the market to buy [SOURCE: Page_85]

seasonal water). [SOURCE: Page_85]

When analysing a whole season, generally an increase in water allocation levels [SOURCE: Page_85]

increases water allocation supply offered for sale and decreases demand for water allocations. [SOURCE: Page_85]

However, the impact within a season can be different, especially if it is early in the season and [SOURCE: Page_85]

the increase in water allocations was much less than expected (increasing demand and reducing [SOURCE: Page_85]

supply overall). In general, water allocation demand is stronger in the first half of the season [SOURCE: Page_85]

and weaker in the second half of the season when full water allocations are often reached [SOURCE: Page_85]

(Wheeler et al. 2008). Hence, an increase in water allocation increases supply and decreases [SOURCE: Page_85]

demand, and the water market price decreases (Loch et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Therefore, water allocation announcements, especially in times of drought and early [SOURCE: Page_85]

season, can have significant impacts on the water market. When analysing the past history of [SOURCE: Page_85]

Australian water markets, it is often claimed that disclosure of non-public information about [SOURCE: Page_85]

future water allocation announcements has occurred before the official announcement release. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Consultations and decisions about water allocation level changes do provide a select number of [SOURCE: Page_85]

actors with prior information about future water allocation announcements (NWC 2011). [SOURCE: Page_85]

However, these claims have been of an anecdotal nature only, with no formal evidence. [SOURCE: Page_85]

In the financial literature it has been shown that insider trading impacts on stocks market [SOURCE: Page_85]

prices (Chakravarty 2001) and pushes prices in the same direction as the return surprise due to [SOURCE: Page_85]

the announcement (Sinha and Gadarowski 2010). As water markets are less liquid than stock [SOURCE: Page_85]

markets (Crase et al. 2000; Brooks et al. 2009), this impact may be even more pronounced as [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

trades by insiders may be less diluted by trades of uninformed traders. If insider trading did [SOURCE: Page_86]

occur historically in water markets, it is expected that prices will move ahead of announcement [SOURCE: Page_86]

in the same direction as the announcement’s impact. Therefore, if insider trading is occurring, [SOURCE: Page_86]

we expect to detect a price drop in the days preceding an increase in water allocation levels, [SOURCE: Page_86]

hence we hypothesise: [SOURCE: Page_86]

H$_1$: A decrease in water allocation prices will be detected in the days before an increase in [SOURCE: Page_86]

water allocation level is announced, ceteris paribus. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Another situation that may occur is that an announcement is made that water allocations [SOURCE: Page_86]

will remain unchanged. Such announcements are less frequent, excluding the cases when water [SOURCE: Page_86]

allocations are already at their maximum possible level (100%). In this case, water availability [SOURCE: Page_86]

itself does not change but water allocation demand can increase and water allocation supply in [SOURCE: Page_86]

the market can decrease as farmers need more water as the season progresses. Therefore, if [SOURCE: Page_86]

insider trading is occurring, there may be a price increase in the days preceding an [SOURCE: Page_86]

announcement of no change in the allocation level as agents with inside information would [SOURCE: Page_86]

purchase water earlier or postpone selling water, in order to avoid or take advantage of an [SOURCE: Page_86]

increased price later. We hypothesise: [SOURCE: Page_86]

H$_2$: An increase in water allocation prices will be detected in the days before an unchanged [SOURCE: Page_86]

water allocation level announcement is made, ceteris paribus. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Both H$_1$ and H$_2$ generally assume that irrigators do not plan ahead within a season and [SOURCE: Page_86]

only buy (or sell) water allocations when water is needed (or not needed). This assumption is [SOURCE: Page_86]

more likely to hold when it is anticipated to be a normal/wet year, when irrigators perceive a [SOURCE: Page_86]

lower level of water scarcity risk and adopt a wait and see strategy in water trading. However, [SOURCE: Page_86]

these assumptions are not likely to hold in the presence of market expectations. In addition, in [SOURCE: Page_86]

the situation of H$_1$, an increase in water allocations results in a physical increase in available [SOURCE: Page_86]

85 [SOURCE: Page_86]

water supply, which in turn may reduce water prices. But, in the situation of a no change in [SOURCE: Page_87]

water allocation prices, where physical water supply does not change to irrigators, changes in [SOURCE: Page_87]

water market prices will be driven significantly by market expectations. Therefore, the same [SOURCE: Page_87]

impact of insider trading under this circumstance may be more difficult to detect. [SOURCE: Page_87]

It is also important to note that the occurrence of a price drop (increase) in the days [SOURCE: Page_87]

before an announcement increases (or does not change) water allocations does not provide [SOURCE: Page_87]

conclusive evidence of the occurrence of insider trading on water markets. If market [SOURCE: Page_87]

participants can accurately predict the announcement outcome, an abnormal price movement [SOURCE: Page_87]

may still be observed earlier than the announcement date. Although some literature considers [SOURCE: Page_87]

the occurrence of unusual price movements (abnormal returns) as evidence of insider trading [SOURCE: Page_87]

practices (e.g. Keown and Pinkerton 1981; Meulbroek 1992); other literature also suggests that [SOURCE: Page_87]

such price movements can also be related to early and informed market reactions to other public [SOURCE: Page_87]

information sources (Gupta and Misra 1988). [SOURCE: Page_87]

### 4.1.   Data and area

This study analyses nine years of available daily water trade representing 28,983 [SOURCE: Page_87]

transactions on the water allocation market for the trading zone 1A Greater Goulburn, from July [SOURCE: Page_87]

1st, 2008 to June 30th, 2017, collected from BoM. The Greater Goulburn trading zone is located [SOURCE: Page_87]

in Northern Victoria, along the Goulburn and Loddon rivers and within the Goulburn-Murray [SOURCE: Page_87]

Irrigation District (GMID). Although allocation trading was initially low in the area (Tural et [SOURCE: Page_87]

al. 2005), the GMID became the most active trading zone in the southern MDB (Wheeler et al. [SOURCE: Page_87]

2008). Greater Goulburn is of particular importance in the southern MDB, with evidence of [SOURCE: Page_87]

86 [SOURCE: Page_87]

price leadership across trading zones (Brooks and Harris 2014). Information was also collected [SOURCE: Page_88]

on all other drivers of water allocation market prices, such as commodity input and output [SOURCE: Page_88]

prices; water storage levels, rainfall, temperature, allocation announcements and percentages [SOURCE: Page_88]

and other macroeconomic variables. These variables are included in the model in order to limit [SOURCE: Page_88]

detection of price movements related to changes in price determinants such as rainfall that could [SOURCE: Page_88]

bias our results. All data sources and descriptive statistics can be found in Appendix I, Table [SOURCE: Page_88]

The early years of our sample include the end of the Millennium Drought. As a result, [SOURCE: Page_88]

2008 and 2009 show substantially high prices. By contrast, price is considerably lower for 2011 [SOURCE: Page_88]

and 2012, due to higher rainfall amounts. Volumes traded on the market also tend to increase [SOURCE: Page_88]

7 Note: Prices are adjusted and expressed in constant 2008 AUD$. We use the terminology 2008-09 for the water year starting on July 1st, 2008, and ending on June 30th, 2009. [SOURCE: Page_88]

87 [SOURCE: Page_88]

after the end of the Millennium Drought. There is a clear seasonal pattern, as volumes traded [SOURCE: Page_89]

are lower in July, as irrigators face a higher uncertainty at the start of the water year. This trend [SOURCE: Page_89]

is also found if we consider the time-period of our dataset as a whole, the mean monthly water [SOURCE: Page_89]

price increases from July (AUD$127.7/ML) to September (AUD$141.6/ML), then decreases [SOURCE: Page_89]

until the end of a water year in June (AUD$95.5/ML). This pattern supports the statement that [SOURCE: Page_89]

farmers tend to hold more water than necessary at the start of the water year (Bjornlund 2003; [SOURCE: Page_89]

Brennan 2006; Brooks and Harris 2008) until the uncertainty related to water availability [SOURCE: Page_89]

decreases. Therefore, a premium is paid by those buying water early in the water year. This is [SOURCE: Page_89]

perceived as an insurance-related premium by farmers (Loch et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Note that many trades in our database are registered with a price equal to zero. These [SOURCE: Page_89]

“zero-priced” trades represented 37% of the total GMID transactions. There are two kinds of [SOURCE: Page_89]

explanations for this phenomenon. First, the most common reason for zero-priced trades are [SOURCE: Page_89]

those trades where water is transferred between accounts without a valid contract to govern the [SOURCE: Page_89]

transfer. This can occur when individuals transfer water between accounts they own, family [SOURCE: Page_89]

members transferring between each other, intra-company transfers, or the Commonwealth [SOURCE: Page_89]

Environmental Water Holder (CEWH) transferring water between accounts (CEWH trades [SOURCE: Page_89]

make up the majority of zero-priced trades). Second, zero-priced trades can be trades where [SOURCE: Page_89]

price has not been reported. Although the trading rules introduced in 2014 formally require [SOURCE: Page_89]

water traders to report prices, there is a lack of enforcement in this matter (albeit the MDBA is [SOURCE: Page_89]

currently focusing more attention on water price reporting compliance (MDBA 2018)). Motives [SOURCE: Page_89]

for not reporting the price include the fact that it is not compulsory and hence just not provided, [SOURCE: Page_89]

but it may also include hiding prices from competitors, and the desire to remain anonymous. [SOURCE: Page_89]

Trades with zero prices were excluded from the database.8 [SOURCE: Page_89]

### 4.2.    Regression

Insider trading detection methodologies have been largely studied in the case of stocks [SOURCE: Page_90]

markets (e.g. Meulbroek 1992; Jackson 2007; Park and Lee 2010; or Olmo et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_90]

However, there are major differences between stocks markets and water allocation markets that [SOURCE: Page_90]

need to be considered. First, the dates of water allocation announcements are usually known by [SOURCE: Page_90]

market participants. Before the announcement, the unknown part is the allocation outcome [SOURCE: Page_90]

(either remain unchanged or increase). Therefore, we cannot use detection methods based on [SOURCE: Page_90]

unexpected events, as in Monteiro et al. (2007) or Park and Lee (2010). Second, trades from [SOURCE: Page_90]

insiders are not recorded by any water market authority. Thus, we cannot use insiders’ activity [SOURCE: Page_90]

on the market as an indicator of interest, as in Beneish and Vargus (2002). [SOURCE: Page_90]

We chose to analyse water allocation price movements in the days preceding water [SOURCE: Page_90]

allocation announcements, to investigate the existence of potentially abnormal price [SOURCE: Page_90]

movements. Different observation windows have been used in the literature (e.g. Monteiro et [SOURCE: Page_90]

al. 2007), hence we chose to study both the five and three day time-period before official [SOURCE: Page_90]

announcements, similar to other literature (e.g. Park and Lee (2010) used a five day observation [SOURCE: Page_90]

window).9 Kwiatkowski-Phillips-Schmidt-Shin (1992) stationarity tests (see Appendix I, Table [SOURCE: Page_90]

I.2) suggested that our dependent variable and many of our independent variables were not [SOURCE: Page_90]

stationary and their first difference series become stationary. Further co-integration tests [SOURCE: Page_90]

suggested there was no co-integration relationship between the dependent variable and any of [SOURCE: Page_90]

the non-stationary independent variables. Therefore, the first difference series were used for [SOURCE: Page_90]

### 4.2.    Robustness and sensitivity

All models were conducted with robust standard errors. In addition, different robustness [SOURCE: Page_92]

and sensitivity tests were conducted on our results. The residuals were checked to ensure that [SOURCE: Page_92]

serial correlation was not present (for an example, see Figure I.3 in Appendix I for [SOURCE: Page_92]

autocorrelations of the residual in the main regression). No serious multicollinearity was present [SOURCE: Page_92]

(e.g. no VIFs above five or correlation factors above 0.7). We added macroeconomic control [SOURCE: Page_92]

variables to check whether interactions between water markets and global market conditions [SOURCE: Page_92]

could bias our results. Similar sensitivity tests have been used for ongoing federal buybacks [SOURCE: Page_92]

13 Note that once water allocations reach 100%, the 𝑈𝑛𝑐ℎ𝑎𝑛𝑔𝑒𝑑𝐴𝑛𝑛𝑡 dummy takes the value 0 for the season remainder, as it becomes certain that allocations will remain unchanged. [SOURCE: Page_92]

91 [SOURCE: Page_92]

(periods when federal authorities were buying permanent water entitlements on the market to [SOURCE: Page_93]

return to non-consumptive use – see Grafton and Wheeler (2018) for further explanation), the [SOURCE: Page_93]

percentage of water allocations, major output prices (cheese, milk, feed barley) in the area, and [SOURCE: Page_93]

fixed monthly and yearly effects. Results remained robust to different specifications and testing. [SOURCE: Page_93]

### 5.1.    Insider trading evidence

Overall this study has sought to test whether there was evidence for insider trading [SOURCE: Page_95]

because of: H$_1$) a decrease in water allocation prices in the three or five day period before an [SOURCE: Page_95]

increase in water allocation levels was announced, ceteris paribus; and/or H$_2$) an increase in [SOURCE: Page_95]

water allocation prices in the three or five day time-period before an unchanged allocation [SOURCE: Page_95]

announcement was made, ceteris paribus. We were also interested in understanding if these [SOURCE: Page_95]

effects were different in the two time-periods, namely before July 1st, 2014 and after when [SOURCE: Page_95]

insider trading was officially regulated, with the expectation that if insider trading was [SOURCE: Page_95]

significant enough to be detected, it may be more likely to be detected before rules enforcing [SOURCE: Page_95]

knowledge and transfer came into place. In our total time-period model (2008-2017) and the [SOURCE: Page_95]

time-period before regulation on July 1st, 2014, the results suggested a significant decrease in [SOURCE: Page_95]

water prices^14 in the five days (and three-day time-period dummy) before an increase in water [SOURCE: Page_95]

allocation level announcement was made. After the new trading rules are introduced, no price [SOURCE: Page_95]

drop is detected by 𝐼𝑛𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑒𝐴𝑛𝑛𝑡 whatever observation time-period window was used. There [SOURCE: Page_95]

was therefore some support for Hypothesis 1 for the whole time-period, and particularly before [SOURCE: Page_95]

July 1st, 2014, the introduction of the new trading rules. [SOURCE: Page_95]

In terms of evidence for Hypothesis 2, Table 1’s results depict a significant price [SOURCE: Page_95]

increase three days before an announcement on unchanged water allocations, but only very [SOURCE: Page_95]

weak evidence in the time-period after June 2014. No significant price movement was detected [SOURCE: Page_95]

for the five days observation window. However, note that the dummy 𝑈𝑛𝑐ℎ𝑎𝑛𝑔𝑒𝑑𝐴𝑛𝑛𝑡 is [SOURCE: Page_95]

harder to interpret than the dummy 𝐼𝑛𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑒𝐴𝑛𝑛𝑡 . The reason is that an increase in water [SOURCE: Page_95]

allocations increases available water supply and hence induces a decrease in water prices, while [SOURCE: Page_95]

14 Note that the dependent variable in the models is water price change, which implies that changes in water price are more negative in the five days (or three days) before an increase in water allocation level announcement is made. As a result, this is equal to a price fall. [SOURCE: Page_95]

94 [SOURCE: Page_95]

an announcement that leaves water allocation unchanged is dependent on market agents’ [SOURCE: Page_96]

expectations. If irrigators’ expected water allocations to increase before the announcement, then [SOURCE: Page_96]

water prices may increase. Otherwise, it may remain unchanged. Thus, we find reasonable [SOURCE: Page_96]

evidence to support Hypothesis 1 before the new trading rules are introduced, and some [SOURCE: Page_96]

evidence to support Hypothesis 2 after the trading rules were enforced. It was expected that [SOURCE: Page_96]

stronger insider trading evidence would be found primarily in the period before July 2014, with [SOURCE: Page_96]

such price movements decreasing or disappearing after 2014, as insider trading practices [SOURCE: Page_96]

become officially illegal. Our H$_2$ results are therefore not totally confirming to a prior insider [SOURCE: Page_96]

trading expectation. [SOURCE: Page_96]

We suggest that the reason that our findings regarding abnormal price movements are [SOURCE: Page_96]

not completely confirming to insider trading expectations is that we believe that there has been [SOURCE: Page_96]

a general increase in informed water market trading and rational speculation by water owners [SOURCE: Page_96]

over this time-period. In July 2014, the new trading rules that entered into application in the [SOURCE: Page_96]

Australian water market explicitly forbid the use of privileged information related to allocation [SOURCE: Page_96]

announcements to trade on water markets in the MDB. However, we detected very weak [SOURCE: Page_96]

statistical evidence of abnormal price movements after 2014 in situations where allocations [SOURCE: Page_96]

remain unchanged (and were nowhere near fully allocated which represents a situation where [SOURCE: Page_96]

there is more economic incentive to ‘guess’ the markets). It is therefore highly probable that [SOURCE: Page_96]

these detected price movements are caused by rational and accurate speculation, as knowledge [SOURCE: Page_96]

about water markets and information has increased considerably. Appendix II provides [SOURCE: Page_96]

additional investigation. [SOURCE: Page_96]

95 [SOURCE: Page_96]

### 5.2.   Other price drivers

As all our variables are first differenced, there was a low level of significance of our [SOURCE: Page_97]

price determinants. It appears that daily water allocation trade amount has a negative impact on [SOURCE: Page_97]

water price before July 1st, 2014. This is consistent with the argument that larger trades imply [SOURCE: Page_97]

a lower mean price for water because of water’s declining marginal value (Colby et al. 1993), [SOURCE: Page_97]

as a higher daily water allocation trade amount also implies a higher trade count. The total [SOURCE: Page_97]

storage in major dams located within the area, such as Lake Eildon, has a significant negative [SOURCE: Page_97]

impact on water prices, as they denote a higher water supply, i.e. a higher water allocation level. [SOURCE: Page_97]

When more water is made available, water supply increases and prices tend to drop. The [SOURCE: Page_97]

ABARES index of commodity prices received by irrigators has a significant, positive impact [SOURCE: Page_97]

on water prices after 2014. As expected, higher commodity prices tend to increase irrigation [SOURCE: Page_97]

water demand. This effect was not found before 2014. This result may reflect changing [SOURCE: Page_97]

irrigation investment, especially the increase in permanent plantings (especially almonds) in [SOURCE: Page_97]

the southern MDB in the last few years, which has been driven considerably by higher [SOURCE: Page_97]

commodity prices. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Water market allocation prices progressively decrease and stabilise at the end of the [SOURCE: Page_97]

water year. This is shown by the respective negative and positive impacts of our month index [SOURCE: Page_97]

and squared month index. The alternative use of fixed monthly effects did not alter this result. [SOURCE: Page_97]

This seasonal pattern has been previously found, because at the beginning of the water year, [SOURCE: Page_97]

there is a high degree of uncertainty regarding the level of water allocations in the following [SOURCE: Page_97]

months; higher prices at the beginning of the water year are perceived by farmers as an [SOURCE: Page_97]

insurance premium (Wheeler et al. 2008; Loch et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

- Parent_Topic: 3137171
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 5.3.   summary
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

---
### 5.3.   Summary

Thus, our analysis of water allocation prices suggests that scarcity and seasonal factors [SOURCE: Page_98]

are the most important influences of water market price movements. In recent years, movements [SOURCE: Page_98]

in irrigation commodity prices have become more important, and this supports the general [SOURCE: Page_98]

finding that water market traders are becoming more sophisticated and speculative. Our results [SOURCE: Page_98]

suggest that insider trading may have been present before the introduction of insider rules in [SOURCE: Page_98]

2014, but only very weak evidence that it may have been present afterwards. Because most [SOURCE: Page_98]

announcements can be predicted using public information, it is entirely possible that successful [SOURCE: Page_98]

speculation is present in the water market. However, given that not all announcements can be [SOURCE: Page_98]

predicted, we cannot rule out the possibility of insider trading still existing to some extent within [SOURCE: Page_98]

Australian water markets, although it is clearly less than in earlier periods of time. [SOURCE: Page_98]

- Parent_Topic: 3137171
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 6.     conclusion
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 6.     Conclusion

This study provides the first systematic, comprehensive analysis of the occurrence of [SOURCE: Page_98]

insider trading in water markets. Australia provides a valuable case study to investigate the [SOURCE: Page_98]

occurrence of insider trading as it has the most developed water markets in the world and high- [SOURCE: Page_98]

quality water trade market information. There is also a natural experiment within the data that [SOURCE: Page_98]

allowed the presence of insider trading to be explored in different time-periods, with water [SOURCE: Page_98]

market rules introduced in 2014 that officially regulated insider trading. We analysed daily [SOURCE: Page_98]

water allocation price and volume market data (2008-2017) and sought to detect abnormal price [SOURCE: Page_98]

movements preceding water allocation announcements in the largest and most active trading [SOURCE: Page_98]

zone in the MDB, namely the Greater Goulburn. Controlling for known water market [SOURCE: Page_98]

influences, evidence was found of abnormal price movements (in the hypothesised direction) [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

preceding water allocation announcements, especially before 2014. There is also some evidence [SOURCE: Page_99]

that the new water trading rules introduced in 2014 may have decreased (or eliminated) the [SOURCE: Page_99]

incidence of such abnormal price movements, although there is still some very weak evidence [SOURCE: Page_99]

of abnormal price movements post 2014. However, it is entirely possible that detected abnormal [SOURCE: Page_99]

price movements post 2014 are related to an increase in informed and rationally speculative [SOURCE: Page_99]

trading behaviour in general in water markets, rather than insider trading per se. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Acknowledgements [SOURCE: Page_99]

The authors acknowledge the comments from two anonymous reviewers and an [SOURCE: Page_99]

associate editor in providing advice and support for this research, and for ABARES in providing [SOURCE: Page_99]

monthly commodity price information. Funding support was provided from Australian [SOURCE: Page_99]

Research Council FT140100773 and DP140103946, and from the GESTE laboratory. [SOURCE: Page_99]
