### 9.         Appendix II: Insider trading or rational speculation? Additional investigation

To test whether the weak evidence of a price increase in the three days before an [SOURCE: Page_109]

unchanged water allocation announcement could be related to either insider trading or rational [SOURCE: Page_109]

speculations after 2014, an additional database was gathered with 15815 water allocation [SOURCE: Page_109]

announcements between 2001 and June 2017 in the Goulburn, along with additional [SOURCE: Page_109]

information commonly available to irrigators, such as storage in major dams in the area and [SOURCE: Page_109]

rainfall data. Using this information, a probit model was estimated with a dependent variable [SOURCE: Page_109]

equal to one if an announcement increases water allocations and equal to zero otherwise. This [SOURCE: Page_109]

model was run using different time-periods (2001-2007; 2007-2014; 2001-2014). The [SOURCE: Page_109]

parameters of these models were then used to predict the content of announcements for future [SOURCE: Page_109]

time-periods. In order to avoid bias, the time-period (e.g. 2014-2017) for which the content of [SOURCE: Page_109]

announcement was predicted was not used to estimate the corresponding probit model. We [SOURCE: Page_109]

sought to answer two questions: i) whether the models could predict the announcements before [SOURCE: Page_109]

July 2014 that increased allocations, using publicly available information; and ii) whether the [SOURCE: Page_109]

models could predict the announcements after July 2014 that left water allocations unchanged, [SOURCE: Page_109]

using publicly available information. Tables II.1 and II.2 provides the prediction results. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Depending on the time-period used for the probit model, we correctly predict 85-89% [SOURCE: Page_109]

of the announcements between 2014 and 2017 while between 2007 and 2014, correct prediction [SOURCE: Page_109]

is 57.5%. The result of more accurate predictions increasing with time is also related to the fact [SOURCE: Page_109]

that after the drought, allocation announcements more frequently increased water allocation [SOURCE: Page_109]

levels. Before July 2014, we correctly predicted 41/58 announcements that increased [SOURCE: Page_109]

allocations. There were only four announcements after 2014 where the water allocation level [SOURCE: Page_109]

15 Announcements made after allocations reached 100% were not modelled, as their outcomes were certain. [SOURCE: Page_109]

108 [SOURCE: Page_109]

remained unchanged. Correct predictions for such announcements are 3/4 using the earliest [SOURCE: Page_110]

trading model (A) while it was much poorer for the other models (0/4 using models B and C). [SOURCE: Page_110]

Chapter 316: The Dynamics of Groundwater Markets: Price Leadership and Groundwater Demand Elasticity in the Murrumbidgee, Australia [SOURCE: Page_111]

Abstract Groundwater over-extraction is a problem facing many countries around the world. Water pricing and developing property rights to enable groundwater trade are a potential demand- based method to address the over-extraction of groundwater resources. However, successful implementation of groundwater trading requires knowledge about the dynamics of groundwater demand and their interaction/substitutability with surface water markets; and, given the paucity of empirical data available, price elasticities of groundwater trade are rare in the literature. We analyse 10 years of surface and groundwater market data (2008-2018) in temporary markets within the Murrumbidgee catchment of the Murray-Darling Basin, Australia, to explore a) the lead-lag relationship between surface and groundwater markets; and b) the price elasticity of groundwater demand to changes in prices. Results illustrate that surface water markets show price leadership to groundwater markets, and that groundwater market demand is elastic, with a -1.04 price elasticity estimate in our time-period. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Keywords: Murray-Darling Basin; Price Leadership; Groundwater demand elasticity; Groundwater markets. [SOURCE: Page_111]

16 This chapter refers to the article cowritten with Sarah Ann Wheeler and Alec Zuo. [SOURCE: Page_111]

110 [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 1.     Introduction

Water use has been increasing worldwide by about 1% per year, and global water [SOURCE: Page_112]

demand is expected to increase at a similar rate until 2050 (WWAP, 2019). As a result, over- [SOURCE: Page_112]

extraction of groundwater resources is a growing problem and a consequence of a common- [SOURCE: Page_112]

pool resource dilemma (Ostrom, 1990). Groundwater overuse represents significant costs to [SOURCE: Page_112]

society: it can increase withdrawal costs, as water has to be pumped from a greater depth; it [SOURCE: Page_112]

generates cones of depression (Wheeler et al., 2016); and can lead to groundwater depletion [SOURCE: Page_112]

and significant infrastructure costs (Asci et al., 2017). Given agriculture uses 69% of available [SOURCE: Page_112]

freshwater resources in the world (WWAP, 2019), the increased use of irrigation water pricing [SOURCE: Page_112]

and property rights have been suggested as a potential means to cope with water scarcity [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Blanco-Gutiérrez et al., 2011). The literature has shown that increased water prices (usually [SOURCE: Page_112]

through irrigation water charges) can favour the adoption of water conservation technologies [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Caswell and Zilberman, 1985; Caswell et al., 1990) and decrease irrigation groundwater use [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Smith et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Groundwater markets often emerge as formal or informal tools to put a price on water [SOURCE: Page_112]

and reallocate scarce groundwater resources. Examples of formal groundwater markets are [SOURCE: Page_112]

found in Australia (Wheeler et al., 2016), the western United States (Colby, 2000) and China [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Zhang et al., 2008); while informal markets exist in India (Manjunatha et al., 2011) and [SOURCE: Page_112]

Pakistan (Khair et al., 2012). Although groundwater markets remain limited in their extent, they [SOURCE: Page_112]

have been shown to generate significant benefits in various contexts. Groundwater markets can [SOURCE: Page_112]

involve significant gains from trading (Knapp et al., 2003; Gao et al., 2013; Palazzo and [SOURCE: Page_112]

Brozovic, 2014), with participants more efficient in their use of water (i.e. generating more [SOURCE: Page_112]

outputs with the same amount of water (Manjunatha et al., 2011; Razzaq et al., 2019)). In some [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

situations, groundwater markets have allowed resource-poor farmers access to irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_113]

(Mukherji, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_113]

Little empirical research on the demand elasticities and market dynamics of [SOURCE: Page_113]

groundwater markets has been conducted worldwide, largely because operating examples of [SOURCE: Page_113]

groundwater markets with sufficient data are rare. This is usually because the range of necessary [SOURCE: Page_113]

conditions for water markets to be successful have not been established (conditions include: (i) [SOURCE: Page_113]

well defined property rights; (ii) effective and adaptive governance and legislation regarding [SOURCE: Page_113]

enabling resources (including a ‘cap’ on water rights issued); (iii) sufficient knowledge about [SOURCE: Page_113]

hydrology and resource constraint, (iv) proper accounting and monitoring of water use; (v) a [SOURCE: Page_113]

sufficiently diverse potential market for water, notably in terms of diversity of value-added [SOURCE: Page_113]

uses; and (vi) the existence of institutional arrangements to address externalities (Wheeler et [SOURCE: Page_113]

al., 2017)). Allowing trade in the absence of adequate administrative arrangements can [SOURCE: Page_113]

endanger water security (Maestu and Gomez-Ramos, 2013; Young, 2014; Wheeler et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_113]

Where water use rights cannot be enforced, the risk of overuse is heightened (Diwakara and [SOURCE: Page_113]

Nagaraj, 2003; Zhang, 2007), as is the importance of understanding the connectivity and [SOURCE: Page_113]

substitutability between surface and groundwater resources. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Australia provides a rare example of a mature dataset on groundwater markets because [SOURCE: Page_113]

of its long-functioning history and development of markets over the past thirty years (Wheeler [SOURCE: Page_113]

et al., 2017). In many instances, groundwater markets in Australia coexist with surface water [SOURCE: Page_113]

markets. In cases where both type of water resources are available, the substitutability and [SOURCE: Page_113]

connectivity between surface and groundwater is a major water policy issue. For example, [SOURCE: Page_113]

understanding the actual impact of water use efficiency subsidies requires knowledge about the [SOURCE: Page_113]

volume represented by return flows (irrigation surface water flowing to aquifers following its [SOURCE: Page_113]

use) (Williams and Grafton, 2019). Furthermore, groundwater pumping in areas close to rivers [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

can decrease available surface water in nearby streams; this situation led to inter-state conflict [SOURCE: Page_114]

in the US (Kuwayama and Brozovic, 2013). There is also a well-known substitution [SOURCE: Page_114]

phenomenon between surface and groundwater (Haensch et al., 2016). These interactions affect [SOURCE: Page_114]

water markets when they coexist in the same area: as noted by Wheeler et al. (2016), farmers [SOURCE: Page_114]

sold their water to the government and compensated by increasing groundwater pumping during [SOURCE: Page_114]

the 1980 drought in California. Similar evidence has been found in Australia (Wheeler and [SOURCE: Page_114]

Cheeseman, 2013) and China (Zhang, 2007). Therefore, improving the knowledge about the [SOURCE: Page_114]

interconnections between surface and groundwater resource management is important (Ross, [SOURCE: Page_114]

2018; Williamson and Grafton, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Moreover, the appropriateness of property rights and pricing policies in general is [SOURCE: Page_114]

conditioned by a sufficiently high elasticity of irrigation water demand. In cases where water [SOURCE: Page_114]

demand is inelastic, a water pricing policy targeting a reduction in irrigation withdrawals would [SOURCE: Page_114]

need to considerably increase the water price in order to reduce irrigation water consumption, [SOURCE: Page_114]

thereby strongly affecting farm income (de Fraiture and Perry, 2007). With a perfectly inelastic [SOURCE: Page_114]

groundwater demand, the lack of a rationing mechanism based on the productivity of water use [SOURCE: Page_114]

can generate inefficiency (Wang and Seguarra, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Given this context, the interest of this research is twofold. First, this study adds to the [SOURCE: Page_114]

literature by providing a rare estimate of groundwater temporary market demand price [SOURCE: Page_114]

elasticities, using over a decade of exogenous water price variations from a key groundwater [SOURCE: Page_114]

use area, the Murrumbidgee in Australia. Second, we examine the dynamics of groundwater [SOURCE: Page_114]

temporary markets, by analysing drivers of prices and trade, along with the connectivity and [SOURCE: Page_114]

substitutability between surface and groundwater markets. Our results provide insights that can [SOURCE: Page_114]

be used to inform pricing and water market policies in the countries choosing to implement [SOURCE: Page_114]

such tools. [SOURCE: Page_114]

### 2.     Literature review: Groundwater markets, price leadership and groundwater demand elasticity

Studies on the price elasticity of irrigation surface water use (or irrigation water use in [SOURCE: Page_115]

general) often use mathematical programming methodologies. Estimates vary between 0 and [SOURCE: Page_115]

2.81 in absolute value, depending on study characteristics (Shumway, 1973; Frank and Beatie, [SOURCE: Page_115]

1979; Howitt et al., 1980; Pagan et al., 1997; Hooker and Alexander, 1998; Scheierling et al., [SOURCE: Page_115]

2004). Other studies use econometrics and water market transactions to estimate surface water [SOURCE: Page_115]

demand elasticity, assuming or holding other factors constant. Brooks and Harris (2008) [SOURCE: Page_115]

examined gains from temporary surface water trade in Victoria, Australia. Using bid and ask [SOURCE: Page_115]

offers, they found very high surface water demand elasticity estimates, ranging from 3.51-3.56. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Wheeler et al. (2008) used 2SLS regression methodology to model actual prices and quantity [SOURCE: Page_115]

traded on the Victorian temporary surface water market from 1997 to 2007 and found a demand [SOURCE: Page_115]

elasticity of -0.52 in the short-term and -0.81 in the long-term. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Studies estimating groundwater demand elasticity focus mainly on groundwater [SOURCE: Page_115]

irrigation charges only. Nieswiadomy (1985) used changes in pumping costs in Texas to report [SOURCE: Page_115]

a -0.80 price elasticity. Ogg and Gollehon (1989) found that groundwater demand in 16 western [SOURCE: Page_115]

states was relatively inelastic (-0.22 to -0.34). Moore et al. (1994) also found inelastic [SOURCE: Page_115]

groundwater demand (-0.10) in the western United States, and Gonzalez-Alvarez et al. (2006) [SOURCE: Page_115]

found a similar result in Georgia with a -0.27 elasticity of groundwater demand. Hendricks and [SOURCE: Page_115]

Peterson (2012) used Kansas field-level data and found an overall groundwater demand [SOURCE: Page_115]

elasticity of -0.10, most of which was due to changes in the intensive margin (changes in the [SOURCE: Page_115]

amount of water applied per acre). Pfeiffer and Lin (2014) employed a 2SLS panel data [SOURCE: Page_115]

approach, to study the effect of a public financing program targeting an overall increase in [SOURCE: Page_115]

groundwater use efficiency. They noted that, following the program, the expected fall in [SOURCE: Page_115]

114 [SOURCE: Page_115]

groundwater use did not occur, partly due to shifting crop patterns. Badiani-Magnusson and [SOURCE: Page_116]

Jessoe (2019) used variations in electricity subsidies across India and found that, overall, the [SOURCE: Page_116]

groundwater demand price elasticity was -0.18 in the short-term. Mieno and Brozovic (2016) [SOURCE: Page_116]

used extensive data on groundwater use and electricity consumption to estimate groundwater [SOURCE: Page_116]

elasticity in Nebraska (-0.5). They found strong biases could arise from measurement errors in [SOURCE: Page_116]

various components of the total irrigation costs, including marginal price of water, marginal [SOURCE: Page_116]

cost of energy, and pumping efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Many factors can influence the estimation of groundwater demand elasticity. In a meta- [SOURCE: Page_116]

analysis of 24 irrigation water price elasticity studies, Scheierling et al. (2006) noted that the [SOURCE: Page_116]

price at which elasticity is estimated, method of econometrics, and temperature all tend to [SOURCE: Page_116]

increase the absolute value of elasticity estimates. Their analysis involved both studies on [SOURCE: Page_116]

surface and groundwater, however they did not find significant differences in estimates based [SOURCE: Page_116]

on the type of resource considered. Conversely, the existence of high value crops in the area of [SOURCE: Page_116]

study tends to decrease the price elasticity of irrigation water. Indeed, Frija et al. (2011) noted [SOURCE: Page_116]

that farmers found to be less water efficient tended to have lower elastic water demand. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Irrigation methods therefore impact elasticities in the sense that they affect the amount of water [SOURCE: Page_116]

consumed per acre (Pfeiffer and Lin, 2014). Furthermore, measurement errors in irrigation [SOURCE: Page_116]

water costs can also impact the elasticity estimate and generate biases (Mieno and Brozovic, [SOURCE: Page_116]

2016). However, the same authors noted that using exogenous water price variations could [SOURCE: Page_116]

avoid this problem, although such cases are difficult to find. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Indeed, studies using exogeneous changes in water price to study irrigation groundwater [SOURCE: Page_116]

demand elasticity are rare. Alamdarlo et al. (2019) employed mathematical programming to [SOURCE: Page_116]

analyse the impact of supply and demand policies in an informal groundwater market [SOURCE: Page_116]

framework in the Qazvin plain in Iran, finding groundwater demand elasticities from 0 to -0.17. [SOURCE: Page_116]

115 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Apart from this, we found no study estimating groundwater demand elasticity using actual [SOURCE: Page_117]

groundwater market transactions. However, there has been some discussion regarding the [SOURCE: Page_117]

influences on groundwater markets. They are influenced by traditional surface-water market [SOURCE: Page_117]

drivers including rainfall and temperature, allocations received, dam storages, output prices for [SOURCE: Page_117]

commodities grown in the area, and seasonal factors (Wheeler et al., 2008). Evidence of price [SOURCE: Page_117]

clustering has also been reported (Brooks et al., 2013). In the case of groundwater markets, [SOURCE: Page_117]

salinity can also be of influence (Gill et al., 2017). Furthermore, Brooks and Harris (2014) found [SOURCE: Page_117]

evidence of price leadership between two water trading zones in the Goulburn-Murray irrigation [SOURCE: Page_117]

district. Price leadership is a lead-lag relationship between two indexes, whereby variations in [SOURCE: Page_117]

one index can be explained by past variations of another market index. Such lead-lag [SOURCE: Page_117]

relationships have been found in various contexts in financial markets, such as between futures [SOURCE: Page_117]

and their underlying stocks (Min and Najand, 1999) or between different markets offering [SOURCE: Page_117]

similar market products (Roope and Zurbruegg, 2002). However, no study to date has [SOURCE: Page_117]

considered the potential lead-lag relationship between ground- and surface water markets. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Nevertheless, as more liquid markets (such as surface water markets in Australia) react quickly [SOURCE: Page_117]

to new information, the existence of such a lead-lag relationship in the Murrumbidgee is likely [SOURCE: Page_117]

and needs to be examined. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Therefore, this study will first analyse dynamics of groundwater markets in a key [SOURCE: Page_117]

groundwater usage area (the Murrumbidgee in Australia) and investigate the existence of a lead- [SOURCE: Page_117]

lag relationship between surface and groundwater markets. It will also provide one of the first [SOURCE: Page_117]

estimates of groundwater market demand price elasticities using exogenous variations in [SOURCE: Page_117]

groundwater price, taking into account the various groundwater market dynamics identified. [SOURCE: Page_117]

116 [SOURCE: Page_117]

### 3.1.     Overview

Water markets in Australia’s Murray-Darling Basin (MDB) emerged in the 1980s and [SOURCE: Page_118]

evolved through progressive waves of reform to become the most active water market area in [SOURCE: Page_118]

the world (Grafton et al., 2011). Most water trades in the MDB are surface water transactions, [SOURCE: Page_118]

especially in the southern connected system. However, groundwater markets have also been [SOURCE: Page_118]

established in various part of Australia. The primary Australia-wide groundwater markets, as [SOURCE: Page_118]

well as permanent and temporary trades, appear in Table 1: [SOURCE: Page_118]

Murrumbidgee Alluvium has 33% zero priced trades. Reasons for not reporting prices can include (1) trades without a valid contract (within a single entity, with family or friends…); (2) not reporting prices during a transaction: price reporting is compulsory since 2014, but enforcement is sometimes lacking despite new trading rules in 2014 (MDBA, 2014). Between 2 July 2008 and 20 April 2018, 6,943 groundwater temporary trades were [SOURCE: Page_119]

recorded Australia-wide in the BoM water market database, along with 12,029 permanent [SOURCE: Page_119]

trades. While most groundwater trades occurred in New South Wales, Queensland and South [SOURCE: Page_119]

Australia also had a significant number of transactions. [SOURCE: Page_119]

We focus on temporary groundwater markets in the Murrumbidgee region for several [SOURCE: Page_119]

reasons. First, trading is generally not allowed between different groundwater systems (as in [SOURCE: Page_119]

the Murrumbidgee), which argues for the analysis of a single water system. Second, permanent [SOURCE: Page_119]

groundwater price transactions were found too rare to allow the creation of consistent time- [SOURCE: Page_119]

series data. Third, the Murrumbidgee groundwater market represents 9.5% of all groundwater [SOURCE: Page_119]

trades in Australia, but contained a considerably lower proportion of zero priced-trades (26%) [SOURCE: Page_119]

than most other regions. [SOURCE: Page_119]

### 3.2.   The Murrumbidgee

Groundwater extraction for irrigation purposes in the Murrumbidgee started in the early [SOURCE: Page_119]

1960s. Before 1982, groundwater access was unrestricted. Since 2006, the authorities stopped [SOURCE: Page_119]

issuing new water extraction licences, and licenses became fully tradeable and separated from [SOURCE: Page_119]

land titles (Green et al., 2011). The Murrumbidgee catchment (Figure 1) comprises 8% of the [SOURCE: Page_119]

MDB and 16% of its water. [SOURCE: Page_119]

118 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Figure 1: The Murrumbidgee region [SOURCE: Page_120]

Source: GIS data collected from the Murray-Darling Basin Authority (MDBA, 2013), the OpenStreetMap layer “Places and Boundaries”, and the HydroSHEDS project (Lehner et al., 2008). Note: Region represented as defined by the Murrumbidgee surface water resource management area. [SOURCE: Page_120]

The catchment hosts one of the most active groundwater markets in Australia, coexisting [SOURCE: Page_120]

with a significant surface water market. Irrigated agriculture in the Murrumbidgee mainly [SOURCE: Page_120]

includes rice and cotton, representing around 65% of the total agricultural water use in 2016- [SOURCE: Page_120]

17 (ABS, 2018). Other crops include cereals, pastures, fruits and nuts. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Water use in the Murrumbidgee is based on an annual accounting system. Along [SOURCE: Page_120]

regulated water sources such as the Murrumbidgee river or the Murrumbidgee Alluvium, each [SOURCE: Page_120]

water user must own a water license. An initial Available Water Determination is made at the [SOURCE: Page_120]

119 [SOURCE: Page_120]

beginning of the year^18, determining the amount of water delivered to each of the shares listed [SOURCE: Page_121]

on a user’s water license (Green et al., 2011). Water determinations may increase throughout [SOURCE: Page_121]

the year, depending on climatic circumstances. Different categories of surface and groundwater [SOURCE: Page_121]

licenses exist, defining access to surface or groundwater according to varying levels of priorities [SOURCE: Page_121]

(i.e. risk) on the use of surface water resources. The most common and popular surface water [SOURCE: Page_121]

license in the Murrumbidgee is the general security license, or aquifer access license in the case [SOURCE: Page_121]

of groundwater. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Groundwater trading has been occurring since 1987, mainly in the Lower [SOURCE: Page_121]

Murrumbidgee Deep Water Source. While early trades involved temporary transfers (called [SOURCE: Page_121]

allocation transfers), permanent groundwater transfers (called groundwater license entitlement [SOURCE: Page_121]

transfers) have been permitted since 2006. Groundwater markets are considerably less active [SOURCE: Page_121]

than surface water markets and are limited to a shared groundwater source (trading between [SOURCE: Page_121]

different groundwater sources is not permitted). Figure 2 illustrates water market prices and the [SOURCE: Page_121]

groundwater volume traded in the Murrumbidgee temporary water markets. [SOURCE: Page_121]

18 Water years in Australia begin on July 1st and end on June 30th. [SOURCE: Page_121]

120 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Groundwater markets in the Murrumbidgee show higher prices and a higher level of [SOURCE: Page_122]

market activity in times of scarcity: between 2008 and 2010, the height of the Millennium [SOURCE: Page_122]

Drought caused a sharp increase in surface and groundwater prices and an increase in the [SOURCE: Page_122]

quantity of water traded. In contrast, flooding in 2010-11 meant prices fell to under [SOURCE: Page_122]

AUD$20/ML, while the total quantity of groundwater traded remained low. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Note that the price of surface water (median price of AUD$94.62/ML) is consistently [SOURCE: Page_122]

higher than groundwater (AUD$22.93/ML), expressed in constant 2018 prices. This reflects [SOURCE: Page_122]

the fact that in many areas, irrigators favour surface water use over groundwater use. Reasons [SOURCE: Page_122]

for this preference include the energy costs of groundwater extraction (Mitchell et al., 2012); [SOURCE: Page_122]

greater tradability of surface water; and frequent presence of malfunctioning bores with high [SOURCE: Page_122]

salinity (Gill et al., 2017; Hooker and Alexander 1998). [SOURCE: Page_122]

### 4.      Data

The study uses 10 years (2008-2018) of surface and groundwater temporary market [SOURCE: Page_123]

trade data in the Murrumbidgee, Australia. Market data (price and quantity traded – our [SOURCE: Page_123]

dependent variables) for this study was extracted from the Australian Bureau of Meteorology’s [SOURCE: Page_123]

(BoM) national water market database. Independent variables collected included climate [SOURCE: Page_123]

variables (rainfall and mean temperature) using BOM’s Hay Airport Automatic Weather [SOURCE: Page_123]

Station (AWS), located in the Murrumbidgee. Diesel prices were collected from the Australian [SOURCE: Page_123]

Institute for Petroleum. Output prices were collected from the Australian Bureau for [SOURCE: Page_123]

Agricultural and Resources Economics and Sciences (ABARES). All prices were adjusted to [SOURCE: Page_123]

the consumer price index using values from the Reserve bank of Australia (RBA)19 and the base [SOURCE: Page_123]

year 2018. Descriptive statistics can be found in Appendix A. [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 5.      Methodology

The method follows two stages. First, we model the price of temporary groundwater, to [SOURCE: Page_123]

question the existence of a price leadership phenomenon between the surface and groundwater [SOURCE: Page_123]

markets. Second, we model the quantity of temporary groundwater traded, in order to [SOURCE: Page_123]

investigate the influence of the groundwater price and infer the price elasticity of groundwater [SOURCE: Page_123]

demand. [SOURCE: Page_123]

19 Several other variables were tested but not included in the final analysis, including the Groundwater level and Electricity prices. [SOURCE: Page_123]

122 [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 5.1.    Price leadership method

In order to investigate the existence of a lead-lag relationship in various markets, Vector [SOURCE: Page_124]

Auto-Regressive (VAR) frameworks have often been used in the literature (Brooks and Harris, [SOURCE: Page_124]

2014; Chordia and Swaminathan, 2000; Chevallier, 2010). Most VAR frameworks are [SOURCE: Page_124]

parsimonious (Chevallier, 2010; Brooks and Harris, 2014) in the sense that they only use a few [SOURCE: Page_124]

covariates. However, in the case of water markets, climatic variables have a clear influence on [SOURCE: Page_124]

market price and quantities traded (Wheeler et al., 2008; Brooks and Harris, 2008; Loch et al., [SOURCE: Page_124]

2012). Therefore, we used a VAR-X model including four market variables (price and quantity [SOURCE: Page_124]

traded for surface and groundwater trade) and two climatic variables: rainfall and mean [SOURCE: Page_124]

temperature. VAR-X models include endogenous variables, used as dependent and independent [SOURCE: Page_124]

variables successively, and exogenous variables that are only used as independent variables. [SOURCE: Page_124]

We defined market variables to be endogenous due to the potential interconnections between [SOURCE: Page_124]

surface and groundwater trade, while rainfall and the mean temperature were considered [SOURCE: Page_124]

exogenous. Thus, we alternatively used each market variable (price and quantity traded, for [SOURCE: Page_124]

surface (SW) and groundwater (GW)) as a dependent variable and regressed it on the past [SOURCE: Page_124]

values of other market variables, while controlling for climatic factors.20 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Augmented Dickey-Fuller and KPSS stationarity tests revealed that most market [SOURCE: Page_124]

variables (prices and quantities) were not stationary, but became stationary once logged and [SOURCE: Page_124]

first differenced. First differences (D.) of climatic variables were found stationary. Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_124]

market variables have been logged (log) and then first differenced, while climatic variables [SOURCE: Page_124]

were simply first differenced. [SOURCE: Page_124]

20 Monthly rainfall and Mean monthly temperature were used in the final results, although the cumulative three months rainfall has also been teste in the analysis. [SOURCE: Page_124]

123 [SOURCE: Page_124]

VAR-X frameworks can be applied using a various number of past values for each [SOURCE: Page_125]

variable. Indicators such as FPE, Akaike’s IC, HQIC or the Schwarz-Bayesian IC were used in [SOURCE: Page_125]

order to determine the number of lags to include. Each regression was run using an alternative [SOURCE: Page_125]

number of lags, and various information criteria were compared. The Australian water year [SOURCE: Page_125]

begins in July and ends in June, where the early months in the season usually show lower trade [SOURCE: Page_125]

intensities, as irrigators face higher uncertainty. Therefore, the maximum potential number of [SOURCE: Page_125]

lags in these tests was set to be nine. Results show that the FPE and AIC indicate the use of [SOURCE: Page_125]

nine lags, while the HQIC and SBIC suggest the use of one lag. In order to avoid overfitting, [SOURCE: Page_125]

we chose to use one lagged value for each variable in our analysis. [SOURCE: Page_125]

The VARX(1) model using logs and first differences estimates the following: [SOURCE: Page_125]

𝐷. 𝑙𝑜𝑔𝐺𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡 = α0 + α1 𝐿. 𝐷. 𝑙𝑜𝑔𝐺𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡−1 + α2 𝐿. 𝐷. 𝑙𝑜𝑔𝐺𝑊𝑄𝑢𝑎𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑡𝑦𝑡−1 + [SOURCE: Page_125]

β3 𝐿. 𝐷. 𝑙𝑜𝑔𝑆𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡−1 + β4 𝐿. 𝐷. 𝑙𝑜𝑔𝑆𝑊𝑄𝑢𝑎𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑡𝑦𝑡−1 + β5 𝐿. 𝐷. 𝑀𝑜𝑛𝑡ℎ𝑙𝑦𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑓𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑡−1 + [SOURCE: Page_125]

### 5.2.   Price elasticity of groundwater demand method

The groundwater price and the groundwater volume are simultaneously determined; this [SOURCE: Page_126]

raises the possibility of endogeneity. Thus, we use an instrumental variables approach. In order [SOURCE: Page_126]

to identify the demand equation, a valid instrument must be found for the groundwater price: [SOURCE: Page_126]

such a variable should impact water supply (e.g. influence water sellers) without affecting water [SOURCE: Page_126]

demand (e.g. water buyers). In a water market context, the difficulty in finding a proper [SOURCE: Page_126]

instrument is exacerbated by the fact that water sellers can also be water buyers. Several [SOURCE: Page_126]

instruments were tested and variables such as the groundwater level, electricity prices, and [SOURCE: Page_126]

various agricultural output prices were found to impact demand and hence were unsuitable. [SOURCE: Page_126]

However, rice price was identified as an appropriate instrument for groundwater price. Rice [SOURCE: Page_126]

growers tend to own large general security water licenses, grow rice in wet years, and often sell [SOURCE: Page_126]

water in drought years when water prices are high. Notably, it has been reported that selling [SOURCE: Page_126]

water allocations when water prices were superior to AUD$200/ML was perceived as a better [SOURCE: Page_126]

and less risky strategy than growing rice (Loch et al., 2012; Zuo et al., 2015). Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_126]

price of rice can influence water supply on the market, as rice producers are frequent water [SOURCE: Page_126]

sellers on the Murrumbidgee water markets, especially in times of water scarcity. Thus, we [SOURCE: Page_126]

expect rice price to mainly influence the groundwater supply and to have very limited effect on [SOURCE: Page_126]

groundwater demand, making it a valid instrument for groundwater price, and allowing price [SOURCE: Page_126]

elasticity of groundwater demand to be properly estimated. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Following Wheeler et al. (2008), a linear log-log specification was used. The [SOURCE: Page_126]

instrumental approach used two stage least squares (2SLS) estimation: [SOURCE: Page_126]

125 [SOURCE: Page_126]

𝐺𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡 = 𝑓(𝑋𝑡 , 𝑍𝑡 ) [SOURCE: Page_127]

Where: [SOURCE: Page_127]

X is a vector of explanatory variables, and Z the instrument (rice price in month 𝑡). We then [SOURCE: Page_127]

proceed to the estimation, using the predicted value of the groundwater price: [SOURCE: Page_127]

̂ 𝑡) 𝐺𝑊𝑉𝑜𝑙𝑡 = 𝑓(𝑋𝑡 , 𝐺𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Several control variables were included. Given that groundwater markets may be [SOURCE: Page_127]

potentially influenced by surface water markets in the Murrumbidgee, price of permanent and [SOURCE: Page_127]

temporary surface water rights were included. As many irrigators use diesel pumps to extract [SOURCE: Page_127]

groundwater, average annual diesel price^21 was included. Finally, climate variables (rainfall and [SOURCE: Page_127]

mean temperature in month), and seasonal dummies were used to consider climatic [SOURCE: Page_127]

circumstances and seasonal patterns. Several other control variables were tested 22 but not [SOURCE: Page_127]

included in the final model. Descriptive statistics can be found in Appendix A and maximum [SOURCE: Page_127]

likelihood estimation was used to estimate the model’s parameters. The regression is: [SOURCE: Page_127]

Stage 1: [SOURCE: Page_127]

𝑙𝑛𝐺𝑊𝑃𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡 = α0 + α1 𝑅𝑖𝑐𝑒𝑡 + α2 𝑙𝑛𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑆𝑢𝑟𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑒𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑝𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑒 + [SOURCE: Page_127]

Granger causality tests and the VARX results suggest the existence of a price leadership [SOURCE: Page_129]

phenomenon from surface water markets to groundwater markets. The percent change in [SOURCE: Page_129]

groundwater price is Granger-caused by the past change in the surface water price: the [SOURCE: Page_129]

parameter of the surface water price in regression (1) is positive and highly significant. Similar [SOURCE: Page_129]

evidence was found regarding groundwater quantity traded: the unit percent change in quantity [SOURCE: Page_129]

of groundwater traded can be explained by the past change of surface water quantity traded. In [SOURCE: Page_130]

order to analyse the duration and extent of the interaction between surface and groundwater [SOURCE: Page_130]

temporary markets (Roca and Tularam, 2012), orthogonalized impulse-response functions and [SOURCE: Page_130]

the forecast error variance decomposition were generated (see Appendix B). They suggest that [SOURCE: Page_130]

a one-unit percent change in the surface water market generates a response in groundwater price [SOURCE: Page_130]

that is positive and significantly different from zero at lag one (See figure B$_1$). This response [SOURCE: Page_130]

weakens in the second month and disappears four months later. Furthermore, the variance of [SOURCE: Page_130]

the forecast error for the groundwater price is due 83.2% to its own innovations and 14.4% to [SOURCE: Page_130]

innovations in the surface water price. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Note that Table B$_1$ also shows that the variance of the forecast error for the surface [SOURCE: Page_130]

water price is due 84% to its own innovations and 11.8% to innovations in the groundwater [SOURCE: Page_130]

price, which could suggest that the opposite (groundwater market characteristics impact surface [SOURCE: Page_130]

water markets) could be also true. However, Granger causality tests and regression estimates [SOURCE: Page_130]

do not show any impact of the past percent change in groundwater market characteristics, on [SOURCE: Page_130]

either surface water market prices or quantities traded. Impulse response functions also show [SOURCE: Page_130]

no significant response in surface water price for lags one to eight, following a unit percent [SOURCE: Page_130]

change in groundwater price. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Overall, these results suggest that market-sensitive information is first incorporated by [SOURCE: Page_130]

the surface water market, and then transmitted to groundwater markets. Such a result is coherent [SOURCE: Page_130]

with the financial literature, showing that more liquid markets tend to incorporate market [SOURCE: Page_130]

information faster (Roope and Zurbruegg, 2002; Brooks and Harris, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_130]

### 6.2.       Price elasticity of groundwater demand

Results from the 2SLS modelling are shown in Table 4. The Durbin-Wu Hausman test [SOURCE: Page_131]

confirmed the endogeneity of the groundwater price. Under-identification and weak instrument [SOURCE: Page_131]

tests (e.g. Kleibergen-Paap rk LM and Wald F statistics) suggest no under-identification or [SOURCE: Page_131]

weak instrument, and our instrument has a significant, positive impact on the groundwater price. [SOURCE: Page_131]

In this case, the rejection of the null hypothesis suggests that groundwater price is endogenous. [SOURCE: Page_131]

130 [SOURCE: Page_131]

Second stage estimates reveal a groundwater temporary demand price elasticity close to [SOURCE: Page_132]

the unit elasticity (-1.05): a one percent increase in the groundwater price leads to a 1.05 percent [SOURCE: Page_132]

decrease in groundwater demand. This is a relatively high estimate compared to the literature [SOURCE: Page_132]

dedicated to the price elasticity of groundwater use via irrigation charge. For example, Mieno [SOURCE: Page_132]

and Brozovic’s (2016) estimate was -0.5 for changes in groundwater use in reaction to changes [SOURCE: Page_132]

in pumping costs in northern America. However, the surface water market price elasticity [SOURCE: Page_132]

literature suggests even higher estimates (in absolute value): Wheeler et al. (2008) found a - [SOURCE: Page_132]

1.51 temporary surface water demand elasticity from 1997-2007 in the Goulburn. Our result [SOURCE: Page_132]

suggests that while the groundwater market demand seems generally more elastic than other [SOURCE: Page_132]

irrigation charging estimates, it is less elastic than surface water market demand. This is most [SOURCE: Page_132]

likely explained by the fact that surface water trade volume is much larger, price is generally [SOURCE: Page_132]

higher, the market is more liquid, and covers a far greater tradeable area across the southern [SOURCE: Page_132]

MDB than the Murrumbidgee groundwater market. [SOURCE: Page_132]

As expected, surface water market prices significantly affect groundwater prices in the [SOURCE: Page_132]

Murrumbidgee. This confirms the existence of a substitution effect between surface and [SOURCE: Page_132]

groundwater (Haensch et al., 2016). Diesel prices have a negative impact on groundwater price: [SOURCE: Page_132]

similar to a decrease in farmers’ income, an increase in diesel prices shifts the water demand [SOURCE: Page_132]

curve to the left as it costs more to pump groundwater. As water demand is reduced, [SOURCE: Page_132]

groundwater water prices fall. Mean temperature is positively associated with groundwater [SOURCE: Page_132]

price, as evapotranspiration increases crop water demand while not affecting supply. Rainfall [SOURCE: Page_132]

has no statistically significant impact. Finally, we find that groundwater prices are slightly [SOURCE: Page_132]

higher at the beginning of the water season (i.e. between July and September). This confirms a [SOURCE: Page_132]

seasonal trend already noted in the literature: as uncertainty related to water resources [SOURCE: Page_132]

availability is higher, farmers buying water early in the season tend to pay more. This is [SOURCE: Page_132]

131 [SOURCE: Page_132]

perceived as an insurance premium, and in the case of groundwater in Murrumbidgee we find [SOURCE: Page_133]

that this impact disappears after October. [SOURCE: Page_133]

### 7.     Conclusion

This study used a ten-year dataset of groundwater, surface water market prices and [SOURCE: Page_133]

quantity traded in the Murrumbidgee to identify two key results to inform water policy. First, [SOURCE: Page_133]

there is a significant price leadership phenomenon from surface water markets to groundwater [SOURCE: Page_133]

markets. Surface water markets are used the most by irrigators in the Murrumbidgee; hence [SOURCE: Page_133]

they incorporate market sensitive information first, and this information is then transmitted to [SOURCE: Page_133]

groundwater markets. The price of groundwater and its quantity traded are therefore dependent [SOURCE: Page_133]

on the price and quantity of the surface water traded. This suggests the existence of a [SOURCE: Page_133]

substitution effect between surface and groundwater, despite irrigator preference for surface [SOURCE: Page_133]

water. Therefore, the need for an integration of water policy that applies to both surface and [SOURCE: Page_133]

groundwater resources is imperative. Conjunctive management of water resources (Ross, 2018) [SOURCE: Page_133]

could offer several benefits in this perspective. If water policies only target surface water [SOURCE: Page_133]

resources, it is likely that irrigators will substitute groundwater for surface water: there is [SOURCE: Page_133]

increasing evidence of such behaviour (Zhang, 2007; Wheeler et al., 2016). Furthermore, [SOURCE: Page_133]

quantifying return flows is also important (Williams and Grafton, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_133]

Second, groundwater temporary market demand in the Murrumbidgee was unitary [SOURCE: Page_133]

elastic (-1.05) in our period of study. Therefore, any policy targeting increasing the water price [SOURCE: Page_133]

should reduce groundwater demand in the Murrumbidgee. However, any change in demand [SOURCE: Page_133]

would vary depending on climatic circumstances and price levels on both surface and [SOURCE: Page_133]

groundwater markets. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Chapter 429: Water markets in France: appropriate water scarcity management mechanisms? Case studies in the Poitevin Marsh Basin and the Neste system [SOURCE: Page_145]

Abstract Water resources in France are relatively abundant in comparison to Australia’s Murray-Darling Basin. However, water scarcity episodes can occur in summer (June to September), when most irrigation-withdrawals occur. In the last years, the French water management framework has evolved towards a reduction in water quotas and a more collective approach towards water resources allocation, under the influence of environmental issues and climate change. This study applies the WMRA framework to two French case studies: the Poitevin Marsh Basin and the Neste system. 11 semi-structured interviews with key local stakeholders were held in order to inform these case studies applications. Overall, the French water management framework is currently not designed for market instruments: buying, selling or transferring water extraction authorizations is currently not allowed in France. Water markets could be considered to mitigate losses associated to the planned reductions in water quotas (Poitevin Marsh) and to improve water demand management (Neste system). However, significant impediments have been identified, including a low social acceptability from local stakeholders. Establishing water markets in France seems to imply major changes in the social attitudes towards the use of market mechanisms applied to water management. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Keywords: Poitevin Marsh; Neste system; Water management; Water markets. [SOURCE: Page_145]

29 This chapter refers to the article cowritten with Arnaud de Bonviller. An adapted version of this chapter is forthcoming in the Book: Water Markets: A Global Assessment. Edward Elgar, UK. [SOURCE: Page_145]

144 [SOURCE: Page_145]

### 1.     Introduction

In France, available renewable water resources per person exceed 2600 m^3, while the [SOURCE: Page_146]

overall withdrawal rate is around 19% (Barthélémy and Verdier, 2008). France is therefore not [SOURCE: Page_146]

a water scarce country according to the Water Resources Vulnerability Index (Raskin et al., [SOURCE: Page_146]

1997; FAO-AQUASTAT, 2014) and water stress is local or occasional only (Falkenmark, [SOURCE: Page_146]

1989). However, in the regions with the most irrigation-related withdrawals, water extractions [SOURCE: Page_146]

often exceed the available renewable resource during low-flows months (Barthélémy and [SOURCE: Page_146]

Verdier, 2008). In many areas, excessive water use in times of scarcity (mainly the French [SOURCE: Page_146]

summer, between June and September) frequently necessitate administrative bans on certain [SOURCE: Page_146]

water uses. Such areas, mainly located in central and south-western France, have been [SOURCE: Page_146]

categorized Areas of Water Resources Management (ZRE). ZREs cover a significant part of [SOURCE: Page_146]

the French metropolitan territory (see Appendix 2). [SOURCE: Page_146]

Water management in France is mainly defined at the basin level. France is divided in [SOURCE: Page_146]

7 river basins, each under the authority of an administrative Water Authority (“Agences de [SOURCE: Page_146]

l’Eau”) established by the 1964 water law. Each basin undertakes a Master Plan for Water [SOURCE: Page_146]

Resource Management (SDAGE) defined by Basin Committees and the establishment of Local [SOURCE: Page_146]

Water Commissions (CLE). Additional Local Plans for Water Resource Management plans [SOURCE: Page_146]

(SAGE) can be established at the local level. The characteristics of each basin can vary widely [SOURCE: Page_146]

(summary characteristics for different basins can be found in Appendix 3). The 7 French basins [SOURCE: Page_146]

are relatively small (8700 to 155 000 km²) and highly populated (32 to 238 inhabitants per km²) [SOURCE: Page_146]

in comparison to Australia’s Murray-Darling Basin (1 059 000 km² and an average 1.9 [SOURCE: Page_146]

population density (ABS, 2008)). [SOURCE: Page_146]

Water has been defined as a common patrimony of the nation by the 1992 water law. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Therefore, water use rights in France are materialized by authorizations to extract water and [SOURCE: Page_146]

145 [SOURCE: Page_146]

cannot be owned individually and buying or selling water rights is currently illegal. Such [SOURCE: Page_147]

authorizations are renewed on an annual basis and can vary depending on the type of water use [SOURCE: Page_147]

considered (mainly irrigation, industry, and drinking water use). They are commonly [SOURCE: Page_147]

designated as water withdrawals authorizations or water extractions authorizations. [SOURCE: Page_147]

In 2004, the European Water Framework Directive defined a good ecological state to be [SOURCE: Page_147]

reached for all water bodies in the EU member States. In order to reach this objective, the 2006 [SOURCE: Page_147]

French Water Law defined a new approach to quantitative water management. First, it required [SOURCE: Page_147]

the definition of a maximum volume of water to be extracted (Cap) in each basin. This volume [SOURCE: Page_147]

(volume prélevable) was defined as the volume that can be fully extracted from the [SOURCE: Page_147]

environment, on average 8 years out of 10, all uses included, while ensuring the good [SOURCE: Page_147]

functioning of the aquatic environment. Second, it required the revision of the existing water [SOURCE: Page_147]

extraction authorizations in each basin, in order to comply with the cap. Third, it created Unique [SOURCE: Page_147]

Organisms for Collective Management (OUGC30) in areas where imbalance between water [SOURCE: Page_147]

demand and supply occur frequently (ZRE). OUGCs are responsible for irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_147]

management and the delivery of irrigation water rights in particular. The cap defined by the [SOURCE: Page_147]

state authorities (Prefects) in each basin often implies a diminution in water consumption, [SOURCE: Page_147]

particularly for irrigation water purposes. This and other reasons led to important delays in the [SOURCE: Page_147]

implementation of the 2006 water law (Martin, 2013). In this context, considering the adoption [SOURCE: Page_147]

of water demand and scarcity management mechanisms – such as water markets – in France [SOURCE: Page_147]

seems of interest. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Few studies have debated the use of water markets in France. Strosser and Montginoul [SOURCE: Page_147]

(2001) provided a review of the economic principles underlying water markets and suggested [SOURCE: Page_147]

two French contexts where the debate on water markets could be of interest: the Beauce aquifer [SOURCE: Page_147]

30 A list of all acronyms and related definitions can be found in Appendix 1. [SOURCE: Page_147]

146 [SOURCE: Page_147]

and the Neste basin. Barraqué (2002; 2004) reacted to this article by studying the history of [SOURCE: Page_148]

water law related to Californian water markets. He stressed the important influence of common [SOURCE: Page_148]

patrimony management in the Californian water law, the existence of high transaction [SOURCE: Page_148]

(especially infrastructure-related) costs, and externalities. He argued for common patrimony [SOURCE: Page_148]

water management instead of the use of water markets in France. Rinaudo et al. (2015) [SOURCE: Page_148]

suggested the use of tradeable water savings certificates to improve urban water use efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Besides, different studies focused the perception of water markets and economic incentives in [SOURCE: Page_148]

general by stakeholders in France. Figureau et al. (2015) conducted a qualitative analysis of [SOURCE: Page_148]

water markets in France using focus groups and different policy scenarios. They found a strong [SOURCE: Page_148]

opposition to the use of markets applied to water, seen is a ‘common good’. Rinaudo et al. [SOURCE: Page_148]

(2012) conducted scenario workshops in France and Portugal and included a market related [SOURCE: Page_148]

scenario. They found strong objections to consider water as a commodity and a fear of market [SOURCE: Page_148]

power, especially from small farmers who consider markets as threatening their farm [SOURCE: Page_148]

subsistence. Once these initial objections were stated, debates revealed potential benefits in [SOURCE: Page_148]

orchard production, as farmers could lease their rights in the years preceding production, but [SOURCE: Page_148]

farmers also worried about high transaction costs, third party-impacts, and an increase in the [SOURCE: Page_148]

financial resources needed to enter the sector. Strong opposition to the use of water markets on [SOURCE: Page_148]

ethical grounds was also found by Rinaudo et al. (2014; 2016), although the authors find an [SOURCE: Page_148]

acknowledgement of informal remunerated water transfers in practice. Therefore, qualitative [SOURCE: Page_148]

analysis shows that French farmers tend to show a preference for policies strengthening social [SOURCE: Page_148]

incentives in relation to water over market mechanisms, although a contradiction with [SOURCE: Page_148]

individualistic behaviours can be noted in practice (Rinaudo et al., 2012; 2016). [SOURCE: Page_148]

Some studies have attempted to models gains from trade arising from the potential use [SOURCE: Page_148]

of water markets in France. Graveline and Mérel (2014) modelled potential market mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_148]

in the Beauce aquifer, considered as ‘France’s cereal belt’. Considering a 30% reduction in [SOURCE: Page_148]

147 [SOURCE: Page_148]

water availability, they found that water markets would compensate 2% of the economic losses [SOURCE: Page_149]

generated by the reduction in water availability, equivalent to 3 cents per cubic meter. Potential [SOURCE: Page_149]

explanations for this result include the fact that only 23% of production was irrigated, and the [SOURCE: Page_149]

possibility of using deficit irrigation without much yield loss on certain crops. These modest [SOURCE: Page_149]

gains of economic benefits are consistent with the findings of other French (Bouscasse and [SOURCE: Page_149]

Duponteil, 2014), Spanish (e.g. Kahil et al., 2015) or Italian (Zavalloni et al., 2014) case studies [SOURCE: Page_149]

integrated in the Water Cap and Trade European project (Rinaudo et al., 2014b). [SOURCE: Page_149]

Given the fact that different local authorities have been devolved responsibilities in [SOURCE: Page_149]

terms of water management at the local level, water management in France is highly context [SOURCE: Page_149]

dependent. Therefore, this analysis will apply the Water Market Readiness Framework [SOURCE: Page_149]

(Wheeler et al., 2017) to two case studies at the local level: The Poitevin Marsh Basin and the [SOURCE: Page_149]

Neste river system. These two cases are representative of the two different irrigation [SOURCE: Page_149]

development patterns commonly found in France (Martin, 2013). The Poitevin Marsh Basin [SOURCE: Page_149]

(Section 1) is located in western France along the Atlantic Ocean. Agriculture is the main source [SOURCE: Page_149]

of income in this Basin, in a context of varying water availability and rich environmental values. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Irrigation in the Poitevin Marsh Basin has developed in 1970s and 1980s, often on an individual [SOURCE: Page_149]

basis, without prior rules of water allocations. The Neste system (Section 2), located in south- [SOURCE: Page_149]

western France, is a river system artificially replenished by the Neste canal, diverting water [SOURCE: Page_149]

from the Neste river to the Neste system. It is supplying water to irrigation and to multiple other [SOURCE: Page_149]

water uses in a context of frequent water scarcity episodes. In the Neste system, irrigation [SOURCE: Page_149]

development at the end of the 20th century has been accompanied by Compagnie [SOURCE: Page_149]

d’Aménagement des Coteaux de Gascogne (CACG), a company originally created in 1959 by [SOURCE: Page_149]

the State, and a set of water allocations rules elaborated by decree in the early 20th century. [SOURCE: Page_149]

These areas are both categorized as zones of water allocations (ZRE), as they experience [SOURCE: Page_149]

recurrent episodes of water scarcity. In order to document these WMRA case study [SOURCE: Page_149]

148 [SOURCE: Page_149]

applications, 11 semi-structured interviews were realized within the areas of study with key [SOURCE: Page_150]

public, private and civil society stakeholders^31, along with an extended literature review. In both [SOURCE: Page_150]

cases, we first provide an overall presentation of the hydrological context (A). We then discuss [SOURCE: Page_150]

the current water management needs (B), the current water management framework (C) and the [SOURCE: Page_150]

potential benefits and impediments identified to the use of water markets (D). [SOURCE: Page_150]

### 2.        The Poitevin Marsh Basin

### 2.1.     Context: geography, water resources and hydrology

The Poitevin Marsh Basin (PMB) is a 6500 km² area located in western France, along [SOURCE: Page_150]

the Atlantic Ocean. The Poitevin Marsh itself (Marais Poitevin) represents 1000 km². Its land [SOURCE: Page_150]

has been progressively recovered from the sea, from the 13th to the 20th century. [SOURCE: Page_150]

The Basin has a semi-circular shape and its external part supplies water to the Marsh. [SOURCE: Page_150]

Altitude remains stable across the basin: the highest elevation point is 300m high, with an [SOURCE: Page_150]

average elevation of 2 to 3m NGF within the Marsh. The Poitevin Marsh is located in Western [SOURCE: Page_150]

France and subject to an oceanic climate. Mean Temperatures are moderated (11°C) and rainfall [SOURCE: Page_150]

is relatively high: 850 mm/year on average, 1000mm on the heights, and 800mm next to the [SOURCE: Page_150]

sea. Effective rainfall (about 280 mm) occurs between October and May. In particularly dry [SOURCE: Page_150]

years, such as 2005, effective rainfall can be as limited as 50mm with a total summer rainfall [SOURCE: Page_150]

amounting to 100 mm (Douez et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_150]

31 10 interviews were organized face to face and 1 by telephone, involving 13 persons in total. 5 interviews were dedicated to the Neste system, and 6 to the Poitevin Marsh Basin. Interviews lasted between 1 and 2 hours, and mainly focused on questions guiding the assessment described in Wheeler et al. (2017), while some also mentioned topics deemed important and/or interesting by the interviewee(s). A descriptive summary of all realized interviews can be found in Appendix 4. [SOURCE: Page_150]

149 [SOURCE: Page_150]

Three main geological structures coexist from the heights to the shoreline, as shown by [SOURCE: Page_151]

Figure 1. The primary bedrock is located in the Basin’s northern part and gives birth to a dense [SOURCE: Page_151]

hydrographic network. These rivers trickle down until they reach the second structure, formed [SOURCE: Page_151]

by limestone and karst layers. These layers are porous, and the hydrographic network shows a [SOURCE: Page_151]

lower density. Many sources flow out of the karstic groundwater body, defining a part of the [SOURCE: Page_151]

marsh said ‘wet’. The wet marsh receives the water trickling down from the bedrock and the [SOURCE: Page_151]

water from near groundwater bodies. This ring-shaped area is 10 to 15 kilometers wide in the [SOURCE: Page_151]

north and becomes wider and with a higher proportion of clay in the eastern part of the Basin. [SOURCE: Page_151]

It englobes and supplies water to the non-porous and more recent land of the Marsh through 4 [SOURCE: Page_151]

main rivers. These rivers join in two estuaries downstream: the Lay estuary and L’Aiguillon [SOURCE: Page_151]

Bay. The land in the Marsh has a large proportion of clay (“bri”), formed by the Flandrian [SOURCE: Page_151]

Progression. [SOURCE: Page_151]

150 [SOURCE: Page_151]

Figure 1: The Poitevin Marsh Basin [SOURCE: Page_152]

Source: GIS data layers from the SANDRE database (SAGE and Poitevin Marsh perimeter, Dams), and the CARTHAGE database (rivers). [SOURCE: Page_152]

Together, relief, hydrographic network and climate shaped this vast wetland. The [SOURCE: Page_152]

basin’s hydrology has been evolving throughout centuries, since the Middle Age when [SOURCE: Page_152]

agriculture began in the basin. [SOURCE: Page_152]

The water trickling down from the north is regulated by dams designed for drinking [SOURCE: Page_152]

water purposes and the compensation of irrigation water extractions. The specific natural flows [SOURCE: Page_152]

of river bodies on the bedrock vary around 8 liter/s/km². In the eastern part of the basin, this [SOURCE: Page_152]

natural flow reaches 10 l/s/km². The highest flows occur in January (25 to 30 l/s/km²) while [SOURCE: Page_152]

minimum flows occur in August (1.5 to 3 l/s/km²). The hydrologic functioning of the Poitevin [SOURCE: Page_152]

Marsh basin is uncommon, due to the existence of sources flowing out of the karstic aquifers, [SOURCE: Page_152]

around the clay soils of the Marsh: [SOURCE: Page_152]

151 [SOURCE: Page_152]

Figure 2: Schematic view of the hydrogeologic context in the northern part of the Poitevin Marsh Basin [SOURCE: Page_153]

Source: Douez et al., (2015) [SOURCE: Page_153]

The water supply of the Marsh can be deeply affected by a significant drop in the Dogger [SOURCE: Page_153]

aquifer water level. Water entering the Marsh flows through 8000km of canals and ditches, [SOURCE: Page_153]

whose water levels are monitored and supported by 200 dams spread throughout the territory. [SOURCE: Page_153]

The canals are used to drain water from the Marsh during the winter and to store water in [SOURCE: Page_153]

summer. Nowadays, ditches are often replaced by French drains and pumping systems directed [SOURCE: Page_153]

towards the canals. [SOURCE: Page_153]

The three main surface river bodies coming from the northern and eastern part of the [SOURCE: Page_153]

Basin are contained by embankments as they cross the ‘wet’ marshes towards the ‘dry’ marshes. [SOURCE: Page_153]

### 2.2.1. The Poitevin Marsh, a wetland hosting significant environmental values

The Poitevin Wetland is located downstream of the Basin, near its estuary in L’Aiguillon [SOURCE: Page_154]

Bay. It hosts important ecological values linked to the Basin’s different habitats: the ‘wet’ [SOURCE: Page_154]

marsh, the ‘dry’ marsh and intermediary marshes are home to about 250 registered bird species, [SOURCE: Page_154]

as well as a significant number of fish and vegetal species related to the marshes’ physical, [SOURCE: Page_154]

chemical and climatic characteristics (Ayphassorho et al., 2016). Water is vital to the [SOURCE: Page_154]

environmental values in the Poitevin wetland: environmental water needs in the Basin are thus [SOURCE: Page_154]

significant. [SOURCE: Page_154]

However, ecological values in the wetlands have been threatened in the last decade by [SOURCE: Page_154]

excessive water withdrawals, demographic pressure, water quality issues, the establishment of [SOURCE: Page_154]

invasive species, and artificialization. The occurrence of climate change and the related increase [SOURCE: Page_154]

in mean temperature and sea level are also sources of concern. In addition to this, within the [SOURCE: Page_154]

wetland, permanent pastures are vital to the local biodiversity. However, such pastures, [SOURCE: Page_154]

traditionally used by livestock and mixed crops farmers, have been progressively replaced by [SOURCE: Page_154]

cereal crops (maize and corn in particular) throughout the 20th century. As a result, France was [SOURCE: Page_154]

153 [SOURCE: Page_154]

condemned in 2000 by the European Court of Justice for failure to comply with the legislation [SOURCE: Page_155]

on the protection of wild birds in the Poitevin Marsh (EC, 199932). [SOURCE: Page_155]

### 2.2.2. Quantitative issues: irrigation-related withdrawals in the summer

Agriculture represents the most part (54.6%) of total water extractions within the [SOURCE: Page_155]

Poitevin Marsh basin, along with drinking water (42.7%) and industrial water use (2.7%) [SOURCE: Page_155]

(EPMP, 2015). Irrigation water use in the Poitevin Marsh Basin occurs during the French [SOURCE: Page_155]

summer, between June and September, and spring (March to May) to a lesser extent. Irrigation [SOURCE: Page_155]

withdrawals are concentrated within groundwater bodies in the dried marsh, located around [SOURCE: Page_155]

(and upstream of) the wetland. As the ground- and surface water bodies are largely [SOURCE: Page_155]

interconnected throughout the basin, excessive withdrawals in irrigation areas can lower the [SOURCE: Page_155]

water level in the marsh in summer, endangering environmental values and competing with [SOURCE: Page_155]

other water uses (drinking water, tourism, wastewater treatment…). [SOURCE: Page_155]

### 2.2.4. Water management in the Poitevin Marsh Basin: stakeholders and governance

Three different instruments are currently used to manage water within the Basin. First, [SOURCE: Page_156]

planification involves the definition of a Master Plan for Water Resource Management [SOURCE: Page_156]

(SDAGE) at the Loire-Bretagne Basin (upper) level, and 3 Local Plans for Water Resource [SOURCE: Page_156]

Management (SAGE) at the local level. Second, state regulations have been designed to [SOURCE: Page_156]

announce bans on irrigation water extractions in times of scarcity, and to enforce water rights. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Third, different contracts have been established between various institutions (including Water [SOURCE: Page_156]

Agencies) and local water users in order to reach the various goals defined by water [SOURCE: Page_156]

management schemes (Ayphassorho et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_156]

Many different actors are involved in water management of the Poitevin Marsh Basin. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Following the European Court’s ruling on France’s failure to comply with the Wild Birds [SOURCE: Page_156]

Directive in 2000, a State agency has been established with the aim of coordinating water [SOURCE: Page_156]

management at the Basin level: Etablissement public du Marais Poitevin (EPMP). The Basin is [SOURCE: Page_156]

crossing boundaries of various local administrative authorities, including 2 régions and 4 [SOURCE: Page_156]

départements, and implies different State representatives, including the local Préfet. Other [SOURCE: Page_156]

significant actors involve the private Marsh syndicates, maintaining the infrastructure within [SOURCE: Page_156]

the Marsh, mixed syndicates in charge of the infrastructures around the Marsh, the Poitevin [SOURCE: Page_156]

Marsh Regional Natural Park (PNRMP) monitoring biodiversity issues, The Gironde Estuary [SOURCE: Page_156]

155 [SOURCE: Page_156]

and Pertuis Charentais Sea Marine Natural Park (PNMGP) and the Sèvre Niortaise River sub- [SOURCE: Page_157]

basin institution (IIBSN). Environmental NGOs (ENGOs) are also playing an important role [SOURCE: Page_157]

within the local SAGEs Local Water Commissions (CLE). CLEs are ‘local parliaments’ [SOURCE: Page_157]

gathering all local stakeholders (State actors, water users, and local authorities), in order to [SOURCE: Page_157]

define the local water management plan (SAGE) according to what can be considered as a [SOURCE: Page_157]

common patrimony management approach (Calvo-Mendieta et al., 2017). Water governance in [SOURCE: Page_157]

the Poitevin Marsh Basin is therefore characterized by a plurality of actors, and sometimes a [SOURCE: Page_157]

lack of coordination (Ayphassorho et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_157]

As the Poitevin Marsh Basin’s average elevation is around 3 meters NGF, flooding [SOURCE: Page_157]

events during winter storms represent an important issue within and around the Marsh, [SOURCE: Page_157]

exacerbated by the general elevation of the sea level associated with climate change. During [SOURCE: Page_157]

the night between the 27th and 28th February 2010, the Xynthia storm flooded 16 000 ha and [SOURCE: Page_157]

killed 33 inhabitants. Considerable attention is therefore paid to the existing protections against [SOURCE: Page_157]

floods, and important modernization programs are currently being held along the Atlantic [SOURCE: Page_157]

Ocean. [SOURCE: Page_157]

### 2.3.1. Irrigation water rights in the Poitevin Marsh Basin

As the Poitevin has been defined as a Water allocation area (ZRE), irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_157]

rights in the Basin are gathered and jointly requested by EPMP acting as a unique collective [SOURCE: Page_157]

156 [SOURCE: Page_157]

management organism (OUGC). EPMP requests a common withdrawal authorization (AUP), [SOURCE: Page_158]

and then allocates water rights each year depending on the needs expressed by irrigators, in [SOURCE: Page_158]

coordination with the local Agricultural Chambers. Therefore, water rights (authorisations de [SOURCE: Page_158]

prélèvements) are granted on an annual basis and cannot be bought or sold. [SOURCE: Page_158]

In spite of the 2006 Water law requirements, no cap on total extraction has been defined [SOURCE: Page_158]

and enforced as of 2019 in the Poitevin Marsh Basin. Besides, some water extractions occurring [SOURCE: Page_158]

in the northern part of the basin (bedrock) are not monitored and considered disconnected from [SOURCE: Page_158]

the remaining hydrological system. [SOURCE: Page_158]

### 2.3.2. Environmental flows and crisis management

As the Poitevin Marsh is affected by irrigation and other water withdrawals [SOURCE: Page_158]

(concentrated between June and September), reference levels have been defined for [SOURCE: Page_158]

groundwater (piezometric levels) and surface water (flow values) throughout the summer [SOURCE: Page_158]

irrigation season. Three quantitative thresholds have been defined for groundwater bodies in [SOURCE: Page_158]

the basin. Drinking water has been granted priority of use and remain unaffected by scarcity [SOURCE: Page_158]

management measures. If the water level in a groundwater body decreases under the first [SOURCE: Page_158]

threshold (the alert threshold or seuil d’alerte), EPMP has established a collective water scarcity [SOURCE: Page_158]

management program with irrigators: in case of water scarcity episode, management measures [SOURCE: Page_158]

implying the temporary limitation of water quotas (10 to 40% volume reductions) can be [SOURCE: Page_158]

collectively applied. If the groundwater level decreases further down the second threshold (seuil [SOURCE: Page_158]

d’alerte renforcée), the French State is taking over and a 50% cut of water quotas volumes is [SOURCE: Page_158]

applied (arrêté-cadre sécheresse). Finally, the last threshold is defined as the groundwater level [SOURCE: Page_158]

under which a ban of irrigation water use is declared, although some exceptions exist for [SOURCE: Page_158]

157 [SOURCE: Page_158]

particular high-valued crops. As surface and groundwater are largely interconnected within the [SOURCE: Page_159]

Basin, similar thresholds have been defined for surface water bodies. [SOURCE: Page_159]

### 2.3.4. The substitution reservoirs: a debated supply augmentation policy

The 2010 Leading Water management Scheme (Comité de Bassin Loire-Bretagne, [SOURCE: Page_159]

2009) defined a necessary reduction in water quotas by 55%, to be reached by 2015 in order to [SOURCE: Page_159]

ensure that water extractions were compatible with all existing uses and a good ecological state [SOURCE: Page_159]

in the Marsh. To compensate the potential economic losses while limiting water withdrawals [SOURCE: Page_159]

during the summer, the local authorities suggested the use of a supply augmentation policy: the [SOURCE: Page_159]

construction of substitution reservoirs. Such reservoirs are filled during the winter, when the [SOURCE: Page_159]

surface river flows and groundwater levels are high, and the stored water is used as a substitute [SOURCE: Page_159]

to irrigation water withdrawals between June and September. Although different substitution [SOURCE: Page_159]

reservoirs have been built and are now functional in the northern part of the Basin, in its [SOURCE: Page_159]

southern part they generated significant political controversy. In 2019, most of the substitution [SOURCE: Page_159]

reservoirs were built or approved in the Northern part of the Basin and their operation was [SOURCE: Page_159]

158 [SOURCE: Page_159]

conceded to a private operator (Compagnie d’Aménagement des Coteaux de Gascogne, CACG) [SOURCE: Page_160]

also present in the Neste system. This enabled a 30% diminution of the annual volume of [SOURCE: Page_160]

granted water rights in practice, although a significant part of it was related to reductions in [SOURCE: Page_160]

unused water rights (Ayphassorho et al., 2016). In the southern part of the Basin, significant [SOURCE: Page_160]

political controversies led to several mediations supervised by the State authorities. This led to [SOURCE: Page_160]

a public management protocol specifying different agricultural practices to be adopted in [SOURCE: Page_160]

exchange for the reservoir’s construction. Many projects and negotiations were still ongoing at [SOURCE: Page_160]

the time of the study, and the associated reduction in water quotas was not finalized at the time [SOURCE: Page_160]

of our study. [SOURCE: Page_160]

### 2.3.5. Compliance and enforcement

About 99% of drills are metered within the Basin. Irrigators must provide meters’ [SOURCE: Page_160]

indexes several times a year, and penalties are imposed for each missing index. The local Water [SOURCE: Page_160]

Police is in charge of the compliance and enforcement of water rights in the Marais Poitevin [SOURCE: Page_160]

Basin. If irrigators are connected to a substitution reservoir, CACG is also conducting [SOURCE: Page_160]

verification on the basis of the contract established with the irrigator. In addition to this, [SOURCE: Page_160]

Agricultural Chambers can occasionally make sure that the water use remains within the [SOURCE: Page_160]

volume specified by the irrigator’s water right. However, given the limited budget of the water [SOURCE: Page_160]

police and the diversity of actors involved, unauthorized water use and water use in excess of a [SOURCE: Page_160]

water right can still happen at the margin. [SOURCE: Page_160]

### 2.4.1. Water demand and diversity of value-added uses

Irrigation represents most of the water demand in the Basin. Permanent and temporary [SOURCE: Page_161]

pastures cover about 31% of the total agricultural area, while the most important crops [SOURCE: Page_161]

cultivated include corn (29% of the total agricultural area) and maize (19%). Other crops [SOURCE: Page_161]

representing smaller agricultural areas include various other cereals, tobacco and seeds, and a [SOURCE: Page_161]

diversity of higher valued crops related to smaller water uses. Organic farming has been [SOURCE: Page_161]

developing in the last years, under what is generally perceived as favourable market [SOURCE: Page_161]

circumstances (high output prices in particular). Drinking water is also responsible for a [SOURCE: Page_161]

significant demand. Besides, tourism (the 2nd local economic activity, after agriculture) is [SOURCE: Page_161]

deeply related to the Marsh’s ecological values; its preservation and the associated [SOURCE: Page_161]

environmental flows are therefore representing a significant demand for water as well. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Specifically, navigation in the emblematic part of the Marsh represents an important water [SOURCE: Page_161]

demand from the tourism sector. Thus, the demand for irrigation water is dominated by a few [SOURCE: Page_161]

low value crops (maize, corn…), while the demand for other water uses diverse (tourism, [SOURCE: Page_161]

domestic water use) and involves a significant environmental water demand (EPMP, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_161]

### 2.4.3. Potential benefits

70% the water extracted for irrigation purposes in the Poitevin Marsh Basin is [SOURCE: Page_162]

groundwater (EPMP, 2015). Transportation costs would therefore be low in the context of a [SOURCE: Page_162]

groundwater market, as most of the aquifers in the ‘dry’ marsh are connected. Besides, [SOURCE: Page_162]

irrigation and drinking water use in the Basin can be compensated by the existing dams and (for [SOURCE: Page_162]

the northern part) substitution reservoirs. Furthermore, EPMP has establishing a public [SOURCE: Page_162]

information system (SIEMP, available online) monitoring the water flows (fur surface water) [SOURCE: Page_162]

and levels (for groundwater or in the marsh) for 204 stations across the basin. Thus, [SOURCE: Page_162]

environmental externalities related to water transfers could be adequately managed, provided [SOURCE: Page_162]

that a cap is defined in compatibility with the environmental needs of the marsh. [SOURCE: Page_162]

In order to comply with the 2016-2021 Master Plan for Water Resource Management [SOURCE: Page_162]

(SDAGE), significant cuts will be required to the annual volume of water rights granted [SOURCE: Page_162]

(Comité de Bassin Loire-Bretagne, 2015). In addition to this, the necessary diminution in water [SOURCE: Page_162]

extractions might be greater than what is described in the SDAGE: a study from an expert panel [SOURCE: Page_162]

(Groupe d’experts, 2007) preconized a smaller volume of annual extraction than the SDAGE. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Redirecting water from lower to higher value crops and less water-intensive crops, in this [SOURCE: Page_162]

perspective, would facilitate the transition while benefiting employment (Martin, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_162]

In this context, the use of water markets could be considered in order to mitigate the [SOURCE: Page_162]

economic losses related to a reduction in water extractions. Bouscasse and Duponteil (2014) [SOURCE: Page_162]

modelled water markets within the irrigation sector in the Poitevin wetlands, in the context of [SOURCE: Page_162]

a projected 55% decrease in water quotas. They find that groundwater markets could mitigate [SOURCE: Page_162]

0.5% of the 18% consecutive loss in gross margin. This limited impact is explained by the [SOURCE: Page_162]

relative homogeneity of farming systems, the hypothesized ability of farmers to adapt their [SOURCE: Page_162]

cropping patterns, but also by the modelling approach (monthly demand and the use of a mean [SOURCE: Page_162]

161 [SOURCE: Page_162]

farming profile that leads to a lower modelled heterogeneity among farmers’ profiles, as [SOURCE: Page_163]

compared to what could be witnessed in practice). They note, however, that achieving the same [SOURCE: Page_163]

reduction in quotas using a pricing policy only (i.e. increasing the water price in order to [SOURCE: Page_163]

decrease water consumption without allowing water transfers) would cause a higher loss in [SOURCE: Page_163]

farm gross margin. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Another benefit from the use of water markets, raised during our interviews, would be [SOURCE: Page_163]

that it could create an ‘incited solidarity’ between irrigators. In times of scarcity, irrigators [SOURCE: Page_163]

might be incited to lend or transfer their water to others in need by the monetary compensation [SOURCE: Page_163]

involved. [SOURCE: Page_163]
