### 2.4.4. Impediments

Impediments identified to the use of water markets in the Poitevin Marsh Basin are first [SOURCE: Page_163]

related to the French legislative framework. Currently, the annual water withdrawals [SOURCE: Page_163]

authorizations granted by OUGCs are not transferrable, and it is illegal to sell them. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Another impediment is the absence of a permanent cap in the current water management [SOURCE: Page_163]

plans. Allowing trade in absence of such a cap would expose the wetland to additional pressures [SOURCE: Page_163]

related to water withdrawals, in a context where the reduction in water extractions planned by [SOURCE: Page_163]

the previous water management plan (SDAGE) have not fully been realized, although our [SOURCE: Page_163]

interviews suggest that ‘sleeping’ water rights are rare in the Basin. Besides, ground- and [SOURCE: Page_163]

surface water resources are largely interconnected with and around the Poitevin Marsh: most [SOURCE: Page_163]

water extractions occur in the Dogger groundwater aquifer, directly connected to the Marsh. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Environmental externalities must thus be monitored closely. [SOURCE: Page_163]

162 [SOURCE: Page_163]

A third category of impediments resides in the low social acceptability of water markets [SOURCE: Page_164]

in the Poitevin Marsh Basin, identified both in the literature (Kervarec, 2014) and the semi- [SOURCE: Page_164]

structured interviews conducted. The important difference between management principles [SOURCE: Page_164]

applied to water markets and those applied to current water management in the Poitevin Marsh [SOURCE: Page_164]

Basin, objections on ethical grounds, fear of monopoly and market power, the use of water [SOURCE: Page_164]

policy as a more general management tool, concerns related to the concentration of water [SOURCE: Page_164]

withdrawals near the Marsh and about a deteriorated image of irrigators that would result have [SOURCE: Page_164]

been expressed by our interviewees. Thus, establishing water markets in the Poitevin Marsh [SOURCE: Page_164]

Basin would require a deep change in paradigm associated to local attitudes towards water [SOURCE: Page_164]

management. [SOURCE: Page_164]

### 3.1.   Context: geography, water resources and hydrology

The Neste river Basin covers about 9000 km². It includes the Garonne left-bank [SOURCE: Page_164]

tributaries (Rivières de Gascogne) as well as an upstream alpine sector (the natural Neste river). [SOURCE: Page_164]

The natural Neste spreads into 2 deep valleys, through summits culminating at a 3000m [SOURCE: Page_164]

elevation. The Neste river proceeds eastwards and flows into the Garonne river after a short [SOURCE: Page_164]

distance. [SOURCE: Page_164]

The Gascony rivers originate in the Piedmont plateaux. These plateaux are not supplied [SOURCE: Page_164]

water from the Pyrenees mountains. These rivers are tributaries of the Garonne river, and [SOURCE: Page_164]

previously follow a parallel route upstream of the confluence. Their catchments have very long [SOURCE: Page_164]

shapes (50 to 150 km, for 500 to 1000 km² catchments’ surface areas). The intense erosion of [SOURCE: Page_164]

163 [SOURCE: Page_164]

the Pyrenees mountains that ended in the early Quaternary period cut these rivers from their [SOURCE: Page_165]

initial water supply. [SOURCE: Page_165]

The territory is located in South-Western France with a semi-oceanic climate. The [SOURCE: Page_165]

average temperature is 13°C, and the winter is mild, although colder in the mountains (close to [SOURCE: Page_165]

0°C in the Pyrenean valleys in December and January). Annual rainfall is around 750mm and [SOURCE: Page_165]

increases with altitude (more than 1000 mm in the Pyrenees). Rainfall is quite stable, ranging [SOURCE: Page_165]

from 75mm (maximal, in December) to 50mm (minimal, in July). Beyond a 1500 m altitude, [SOURCE: Page_165]

the snow cover is persisting during winter months (High Neste). Effective rainfall occurs [SOURCE: Page_165]

between October and May and is close to 400mm in the south alpine sector, and 200mm in the [SOURCE: Page_165]

north. In the last years, drought episodes have been common in the territory, with dry and hot [SOURCE: Page_165]

summers. [SOURCE: Page_165]

The land around the Gascony rivers is mainly non-porous, including marl, clay [SOURCE: Page_165]

(molasses) and sand and clay colluviums created by alteration of the molasses. The structure of [SOURCE: Page_165]

this land, non-porous at its surface, explain the high density of the hydrographic network. It [SOURCE: Page_165]

also explains the high variation in flows for the tributaries that are not supplied water by the [SOURCE: Page_165]

Pyrenees mountains. These rivers show average specific flows (5 to 10 l/s/km²) et minimum [SOURCE: Page_165]

flows between 1 and 1.5 l/s/km². The natural Neste has a 30l/s/km² average flow regulated by [SOURCE: Page_165]

snowmelt. The minimum annual flow occurs in January and is around 20l/s/km². [SOURCE: Page_165]

About 20 lakes have been established in the mountains, originally for hydroelectric [SOURCE: Page_165]

purposes. They supply 48 Mm$_3$ to irrigation water supply annually. [SOURCE: Page_165]

164 [SOURCE: Page_165]

Figure 3: The Neste system and the Garonne river [SOURCE: Page_166]

Sources: GIS data layers from the CARTHAGE and SANDRE databases [SOURCE: Page_166]

The eastern proximity of a basin supplied water from the Pyrenees, the western existence [SOURCE: Page_166]

of rivers with a very low water supply (especially in summer) and the absence of any significant [SOURCE: Page_166]

groundwater aquifer explain the construction of the Neste canal in 1862, in order to develop [SOURCE: Page_166]

irrigated agriculture in the region. Later, different dams were built in the piedmont sector, across [SOURCE: Page_166]

the artificially replenished rivers, to guarantee a 73Mm$_3$ annual water volume. In its upstream [SOURCE: Page_166]

part, the canal is following the natural Neste. It then replenishes 17 rivers, through the use of [SOURCE: Page_166]

channels cumulating 90km in length. [SOURCE: Page_166]

165 [SOURCE: Page_166]

Figure 4: Schematic view of the Neste system [SOURCE: Page_167]

Source: Beaucoueste et al., 2001 [SOURCE: Page_167]

Today, about 250 Mm$_3$ transit through the Neste canal annually (Ricart and Clarimont, [SOURCE: Page_167]

2017). The water is shared between environmental needs, drinking water uses for about 300 000 [SOURCE: Page_167]

inhabitant (11Mm$_3$), the compensation of irrigation withdrawals (60 Mm$_3$) an industrial water [SOURCE: Page_167]

use (6Mm$_3$). This water is also used to fill the piedmont dams during the spring. [SOURCE: Page_167]

A 4 m^3/s minimum flow requirement (MFR) has been defined downstream of the canal [SOURCE: Page_167]

intake in the natural Neste river. Additional minimum flows are required downstream of the [SOURCE: Page_167]

system, before the Garonne confluence. The Neste system is monitored in real time, through [SOURCE: Page_167]

the use of performance indicators and regulatory requirements. A hydrological monitoring [SOURCE: Page_167]

system has been established to these ends. [SOURCE: Page_167]

### 3.2.1. Water use in the Neste system

Multiple water uses coexist in the system. These uses involve drinking water, irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_168]

hydropower (mills and dams), industrial uses (including cooling of the Golfech nuclear power [SOURCE: Page_168]

plant downstream of the system), navigation, and environmental uses (including dilution for [SOURCE: Page_168]

water quality purposes). Other non-consumptive uses include fishing, hunting, and diverse [SOURCE: Page_168]

recreational uses as kayaking. Irrigation represents the vast majority of water withdrawals from [SOURCE: Page_168]

the Neste system. In 2009, annual irrigation water needs were estimated between 75 and 100 [SOURCE: Page_168]

million cubic meters depending on climatic circumstances (Mm$_3$). Such needs are concentrated [SOURCE: Page_168]

within the low-flow period, between June and September. Drinking water and other domestic [SOURCE: Page_168]

uses, in comparison, represented about 6 Mm$_3$ of water withdrawals per year (Villocel et al., [SOURCE: Page_168]

2009), while industrial uses were relatively low (about 2 Mm$_3$). [SOURCE: Page_168]

### 3.2.2. Environmental water

About 70% of the annual 220 Mm$_3$ transiting from the Neste canal and through the [SOURCE: Page_168]

Neste system is environmental water, meant to ensure that flows in the system remain over the [SOURCE: Page_168]

defined minimum flow requirements (MFR). MFRs have been included in water management [SOURCE: Page_168]

plans since the 1996 Adour-Garonne Directing Scheme for Water Management (SDAGE). [SOURCE: Page_168]

Originally, MFRs were designed for water quality purposes and have evolved towards “Flows [SOURCE: Page_168]

above which the normal coexistence of all uses and the good functioning of the aquatic [SOURCE: Page_168]

environment are guaranteed, and which must thus be secured every year during the low water [SOURCE: Page_168]

period with defined tolerances” (Comité de Bassin Adour-Garonne, 1996). MFRs have played [SOURCE: Page_168]

an important role in the local water politics. Notably, they have been associated with actors and [SOURCE: Page_168]

167 [SOURCE: Page_168]

discourses legitimizing the construction of new reservoirs in order to guarantee minimum flows, [SOURCE: Page_169]

while ensuring other uses (including irrigation) were not impacted (Fernandez et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_169]

### 3.2.3. Quantitative issues: withdrawals in low-flow months

Water scarcity in the Neste system is generally occurring in two time periods. First, [SOURCE: Page_169]

although the Neste canal delivers about 250 Mm$_3$ a year to the Neste system (Ricart and [SOURCE: Page_169]

Clarimont, 2017), irrigation water use is concentrated on the French summer (June to [SOURCE: Page_169]

September), where temperature and potential evapotranspiration are higher while rainfall is [SOURCE: Page_169]

variable. Second, water in the Neste system depends on the amount of snow melting from the [SOURCE: Page_169]

Pyrenees. Therefore, scarcity episodes can be experienced outside of irrigation withdrawal [SOURCE: Page_169]

peaks in Winter (December, January, February) when lower amounts of water flow from the [SOURCE: Page_169]

Pyrenees. These water scarcity episodes endanger the ability of the system’s operator to satisfy [SOURCE: Page_169]

the different consumptive uses in the system while respecting the Minimum Flow Requirements [SOURCE: Page_169]

(MFR) as defined by the law, and therefore cause important management issues. [SOURCE: Page_169]

### 3.2.4. Water management in the Neste system: Stakeholders and governance

The applicable Master Plan for Water Resource Management (SDAGE) in the Neste [SOURCE: Page_169]

system is the 2015 Adour-Garonne SDAGE. No local management plan (SAGE) has been [SOURCE: Page_169]

defined as of 2019. Water management in the Neste system involves various actors and [SOURCE: Page_169]

stakeholders. The Compagnie d’Aménagement des Coteaux de Gascogne (CACG) is in charge [SOURCE: Page_169]

of managing river flows in the Neste System, by monitoring water diversions from the Neste [SOURCE: Page_169]

river and operating the associated dams and reservoirs. The local OUGC is the Gers [SOURCE: Page_169]

Agricultural Chamber, granting water extraction authorization (water rights) on an annual basis [SOURCE: Page_169]

168 [SOURCE: Page_169]

in collaboration with CACG. Electricité de France (EDF) oversees hydropower generation [SOURCE: Page_170]

using the 48hm^3 of water stored in dams upstream; in concertation with CACG, EDF can [SOURCE: Page_170]

release water in summer in order to keep flow levels over the minimum flows (MFRs) during [SOURCE: Page_170]

low-flow months based on a contractual relationship. Other stakeholders from the Neste system [SOURCE: Page_170]

involve state representatives, rural community (irrigators) and civil society representatives. [SOURCE: Page_170]

This latter category includes the local Fishing federation and environmental NGOs (ENGOs). [SOURCE: Page_170]

A recent analysis of stakeholders’ attitudes has been done by Ricard and Clarimont (2017). It [SOURCE: Page_170]

reveals multiple references to a latent conflict between rural community and ENGOs. While [SOURCE: Page_170]

rural community concerns focus on the future agricultural model in the Lannemezan plain, civil [SOURCE: Page_170]

society attitudes reveal concerns about privatizing the water management model and critics of [SOURCE: Page_170]

maize monoculture in the area. Public and private services tend to focus on the social [SOURCE: Page_170]

recognition of irrigation as a tool to develop the territory, the effect of irrigation on [SOURCE: Page_170]

environmental flows, and concerns about the aforementioned conflict. [SOURCE: Page_170]

3.3. Current framework: Legal responses, Legal framework, Local stakeholders involved (esp. irrigators) and current water management. [SOURCE: Page_170]

### 3.3.1. Irrigation water rights in the Neste system

Irrigation Water rights in the system are granted on an annual basis. A cap (volume [SOURCE: Page_170]

prélevable) has been defined by the State authorities (Préfet) in 2012, as required by the 2006 [SOURCE: Page_170]

water law. Water withdrawals in the Neste system are first defined by a pluriannual withdrawal [SOURCE: Page_170]

authorization (AUP) defining the total volume of water rights allowed for several years, in [SOURCE: Page_170]

accordance with the cap. The current AUP covers the years 2016 to 2021. Water rights are [SOURCE: Page_170]

granted for two separate time periods within the year: the low-flow period (June 1st to October [SOURCE: Page_170]

31st), and the remaining water season (November 1st to May 31st). The current AUP defined a [SOURCE: Page_170]

169 [SOURCE: Page_170]

global irrigation water right of 126 Mm$_3$ in the low flow period, and 16.4 Mm$_3$ for the [SOURCE: Page_171]

remaining season. An annual allocation plan (PAR) is then formulated in concertation with the [SOURCE: Page_171]

various actors involved in the Neste system. This plan is then approved by the State [SOURCE: Page_171]

representative (Préfet) and specifies all the granted irrigation water rights on an annual basis. [SOURCE: Page_171]

An important principle in this perspective is the droit acquis principle: once a water right is [SOURCE: Page_171]

granted, a water user cannot be denied this right (in the same conditions) in the following years, [SOURCE: Page_171]

as long as the user pays the related fee. Water rights in the Neste system are bundled to land, [SOURCE: Page_171]

and the land value generally doubles if a water right is attached to it. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Water rights and prices vary depending on the use considered. In all of France’s ZREs, [SOURCE: Page_171]

irrigation water rights are granted by a common management organism (OUGC). In the Neste [SOURCE: Page_171]

system, this role is shared between CACG, in charge of the infrastructure, the operational [SOURCE: Page_171]

monitoring and the delivery of the granted water rights, and the local agricultural [SOURCE: Page_171]

representatives (Chambre d’agriculture du Gers). Each irrigator must apply for a water right [SOURCE: Page_171]

each year. An irrigator is then granted a water right based on resource availability and the [SOURCE: Page_171]

respect of prior water rights. Water demand in the Neste system is currently exceeding water [SOURCE: Page_171]

supply. A waiting list has therefore been created and is updated each year. Each year, priority [SOURCE: Page_171]

is granted first to irrigators who request a right allocated to them in the previous year (droit [SOURCE: Page_171]

acquis), second to newcomers, and third to irrigators wishing the increase their allocated [SOURCE: Page_171]

volume. Each irrigator then establishes a contract with CACG in order to have the water [SOURCE: Page_171]

delivered. [SOURCE: Page_171]

### 3.3.2. Compliance and enforcement

CACG is monitoring water flows in the system on a permanent basis, in order to ensure [SOURCE: Page_171]

the availability of water to match individual water rights based on irrigators’ declared water [SOURCE: Page_171]

170 [SOURCE: Page_171]

needs. Withdrawals from the Neste river and dam releases upstream of the system are used in [SOURCE: Page_172]

order to manage flows in the system and match the allocated water rights. In this perspective, [SOURCE: Page_172]

water use in the Neste system is fully metered. In order to control for unauthorized uses, CACG [SOURCE: Page_172]

is conducting controls every year during summer months. Irrigators have strong incentives to [SOURCE: Page_172]

use water as defined by their water right: in case water extractions exceeding the water right, [SOURCE: Page_172]

CACG applies a tariff 4 to 11 times higher for the additional water used. Fines related to [SOURCE: Page_172]

unauthorized water use can also be applied. [SOURCE: Page_172]

### 3.3.3. Functional arrangements allocating water between different uses

As described in section 2A/, irrigation is the main consumptive water use in the Neste [SOURCE: Page_172]

system (75 to 100 Mm$_3$), whereas drinking water (6Mm$_3$) and industry (2Mm$_3$) are limited. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Irrigation water rights are jointly requested by the unique collective management organism [SOURCE: Page_172]

(OUGC) in the name of all irrigators, in conformity with the pluriannual withdrawal [SOURCE: Page_172]

authorization (AUP) and the cap. It is validated each year by the State representative (Préfet). [SOURCE: Page_172]

A convention has been established between hydropower use for the dams and reservoirs located [SOURCE: Page_172]

upstream of the Neste system, where the hydropower operator can release water during low- [SOURCE: Page_172]

flow periods but outside demand peaks for electricity, including a financial compensation for [SOURCE: Page_172]

the loss incurred by the company (Fernandez, 2014). Irrigation water rights costs remain the [SOURCE: Page_172]

same throughout the system. However, access to water for other uses (such as industrial water [SOURCE: Page_172]

use) have different prices as defined by the law. Specific minimum flows have been designed [SOURCE: Page_172]

within the Neste system in this perspective. [SOURCE: Page_172]

### 3.3.4. Crisis management in the low flow months and Risk assignment

Scarcity episodes in the Neste system mainly occur between June and September, when [SOURCE: Page_173]

most irrigation withdrawals occur. When flows decrease beyond a specific threshold in the [SOURCE: Page_173]

system, the Neste Commission is required to meet. This Commission, originally created by [SOURCE: Page_173]

CACG in the 1990s, gathers representatives from the different stakeholders in the System: State [SOURCE: Page_173]

representatives, elected officials, and civil society stakeholders. Typical water management [SOURCE: Page_173]

measures taken by the Neste Commission can include volumetric restrictions (decrease in water [SOURCE: Page_173]

quotas) or additional time constraints on irrigation practices. The risk assignment varies [SOURCE: Page_173]

according to the type of water used considered. As a higher priority is granted to drinking water [SOURCE: Page_173]

use and some industrial water uses, they remain unaffected by scarcity management. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Management measures thus adjust irrigation water rights and environmental flows objectives [SOURCE: Page_173]

(MFRs or their respective tolerances) in order to cope with scarcity conditions. Decisions at the [SOURCE: Page_173]

Neste Commission are taken based on a consensus between all participants. If, in spite of the [SOURCE: Page_173]

management measures decided at the Neste Commission, flows decrease further down past a [SOURCE: Page_173]

defined Crisis Flow (Débit de crise, DCR) then the State is taking over and a ban on all irrigation [SOURCE: Page_173]

withdrawals can be decided and announced by a specific legislation (arrêté-cadre sécheresse). [SOURCE: Page_173]

### 3.4.1. Water demand and diverse value-added uses

Water demand in the system is higher than the available water supply: unfulfilled [SOURCE: Page_173]

requests for irrigation water are gathered in a waiting list associated to irrigation water rights. [SOURCE: Page_173]

In our opinion, the main potential benefit of establishing water markets in the Neste system [SOURCE: Page_173]

would be to fluidify the waiting list, allowing irrigators to buy water rights. It has to be noted [SOURCE: Page_173]

172 [SOURCE: Page_173]

that the number of irrigators figuring in this list has been decreasing in the recent years; [SOURCE: Page_174]

additional research is needed to understand this evolution. [SOURCE: Page_174]

No study has been published on the potential economic benefits arising from the use of [SOURCE: Page_174]

water markets and their related gains from trade in the Neste system. Additional research on [SOURCE: Page_174]

functioning water markets would be required in this perspective. [SOURCE: Page_174]

Besides, as extractions within the Neste system are compensated by water releases [SOURCE: Page_174]

upstream and monitored in real time by CACG, the potential environmental externalities arising [SOURCE: Page_174]

from water transfers are limited. Although significant environmental flows are needed upstream [SOURCE: Page_174]

in order to maintain the existing ecosystem, such externalities could be compensated by the [SOURCE: Page_174]

flow control operated by CACG as long as the cap on total water extractions is enforced. The [SOURCE: Page_174]

significant amount of infrastructure in place would facilitate water transfer in the context of a [SOURCE: Page_174]

water market, considerably limiting transaction costs. [SOURCE: Page_174]

### 3.4.2. Impediments

However, significant impediments exist to the establishment of water markets in the [SOURCE: Page_174]

Neste system. First, as already mentioned, it is illegal in France to sell or transfer water rights [SOURCE: Page_174]

(annual withdrawals authorizations) on an individual basis. Second, water rights in the Neste [SOURCE: Page_174]

system are bundled to land: when agricultural land is sold, the corresponding water right is sold [SOURCE: Page_174]

with it. This is closely related to the droit acquis principle: an irrigator owning a water right [SOURCE: Page_174]

cannot currently be denied the same right in the next year. Abandoning this principle would [SOURCE: Page_174]

expose farmers to a loss of prior investments, as the price of land doubles when associated with [SOURCE: Page_174]

an irrigation water right. Third, the main impediment to the establishment of water markets in [SOURCE: Page_174]

the Neste river system is the strong cultural and political opposition to the use of markets to [SOURCE: Page_174]

173 [SOURCE: Page_174]

manage water resources. Already shown by the scarce literature dedicated to water markets in [SOURCE: Page_175]

France (Rinaudo et al., 2012; 2014; 2016; Figureau et al., 2015), this trend also appears in the [SOURCE: Page_175]

semi-structured interviews realized. While some interviewees consider that potential gains from [SOURCE: Page_175]

trade do exist, they are also worrying about the redistributive effects of such policies and do not [SOURCE: Page_175]

support the idea of establishing water markets in the Neste system. Water markets are [SOURCE: Page_175]

alternatively perceived as endangering the ability to use water policy as a tool for climate [SOURCE: Page_175]

change adaptation, favouring farmers that are financially at ease while hurting irrigators that [SOURCE: Page_175]

are already in a worrying financial situation, and representing a political choice hurting [SOURCE: Page_175]

interviewees on ethical grounds. [SOURCE: Page_175]

### 4.     Conclusion

This study applied the WMRA framework (Wheeler et al., 2017) to two case studies in [SOURCE: Page_175]

France: the Poitevin Marsh Basin and the Neste system. An overview of the assessment [SOURCE: Page_175]

provided in both cases can be found in Appendix 5. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Water management in France is currently not designed for water markets. It is illegal to [SOURCE: Page_175]

buy, sell, and (in most cases) transfer water extraction authorisations and no significant interest [SOURCE: Page_175]

has been expressed by the French authorities towards the use of market mechanisms applied to [SOURCE: Page_175]

water rights. In practice, water rights are still bundled to land, and the value of water rights is [SOURCE: Page_175]

partly reflected in the increased value of land when a water right has been attached to it. A cap [SOURCE: Page_175]

has been defined in most basins, but in some cases (including the Poitevin Marsh Basin) it has [SOURCE: Page_175]

not been implemented as of 2019. [SOURCE: Page_175]

The 2006 Water law recently established the basis of a water demand management [SOURCE: Page_175]

through the establishment of a cap and the use of common organisms (OUGC) to allocate [SOURCE: Page_175]

174 [SOURCE: Page_175]

irrigation water rights. Granting OUGCs with more flexibility to allocate water rights and [SOURCE: Page_176]

facilitate water transfers based on collectively established rules and considering the use of [SOURCE: Page_176]

markets might be of interest. [SOURCE: Page_176]

Within the Poitevin Marsh Basin, important reductions in the volume of water quotas [SOURCE: Page_176]

granted annually will necessarily be implemented in the coming years. Such a reduction, [SOURCE: Page_176]

required by the cap recommended by the 2006 water law, has been occurring in some parts of [SOURCE: Page_176]

the basin in association with the construction of substitution reservoirs. This policy, however, [SOURCE: Page_176]

has generated significant political backlash in the southern part of the basin, showing a low [SOURCE: Page_176]

social acceptability. In the perspective of additional reductions in water use due to climate [SOURCE: Page_176]

change or future SDAGE requirements, markets could be considered as a tool to limit the [SOURCE: Page_176]

resulting economic losses. In this perspective, additional research quantifying the potential [SOURCE: Page_176]

related economic benefits in the context of existing water markets would be highly relevant to [SOURCE: Page_176]

inform the debate. [SOURCE: Page_176]

In the Neste system, a cap is already in place and enforced. However, as the demand is [SOURCE: Page_176]

currently greater than supply in water rights, a waiting list has been created. Due to the droit [SOURCE: Page_176]

acquis principle, once an irrigator is granted an annual water right, he cannot be denied water [SOURCE: Page_176]

rights in the following years as long as he fulfils the corresponding contractual obligations. As [SOURCE: Page_176]

a result, some actors are currently excluded from access to water. As climate change is expected [SOURCE: Page_176]

to further reduce average and minimum flows in the area (CBAG, 2017), additional means of [SOURCE: Page_176]

demand management and flexibility would be of interest. Water markets could be considered [SOURCE: Page_176]

in this perspective. [SOURCE: Page_176]

However, significant impediments have been identified to the use of water markets in [SOURCE: Page_176]

our case studies. The Poitevin wetland is highly sensitive to water extractions and the most part [SOURCE: Page_176]

of the basin is hydrologically connected. Therefore, externalities arising from water transfers [SOURCE: Page_176]

175 [SOURCE: Page_176]

would need to be closely monitored. In the Neste system, implementing water markets would [SOURCE: Page_177]

require abandoning the droit acquis principle, thereby exposing irrigators to important losses [SOURCE: Page_177]

in capital investment as the value of their land would drop. Finally, the most important [SOURCE: Page_177]

impediment is the very low social acceptability of water markets in France. Concerns and [SOURCE: Page_177]

opposition to the use of water market mechanisms applied to water resources (redistributive [SOURCE: Page_177]

effects, ethics and water as a common good, fear of monopoly power…) have been expressed [SOURCE: Page_177]

across our interviews and clearly described by the literature. The French water management is [SOURCE: Page_177]

defined in a common patrimony perspective (Calvo-Mendieta et al., 2017) that seems hardly [SOURCE: Page_177]

compatible with the institutional changes required to establish water markets. In this [SOURCE: Page_177]

perspective, it seems to us that implementing water markets in France would require [SOURCE: Page_177]

considerable change in the local paradigms of water management, in a context where the [SOURCE: Page_177]

existing frameworks are already subjected to significant political debate. [SOURCE: Page_177]

### 6.   Appendix 1: Acronyms and definitions

Acronym   French initial component                            Definition [SOURCE: Page_182]

AUP:     Autorisation Unique de     Pluriannual water withdrawal authorization granted by the Prélèvement            local State authority (Préfet) to an OUGC, defining the volume of irrigation water to be extracted each year for a specific time period [SOURCE: Page_182]

CACG:          Compagnie             Company in charge of monitoring water flows in the Neste d’Aménagement des           system. CACG is also managing water stored in the Coteaux de Gascogne           Poitevin Marsh Basin’s Substitution reservoirs. [SOURCE: Page_182]

CLE:     Commission Locale de             Local Water Commission. It gathers local water l’Eau                   stakeholders (State representatives, local government representatives, and civil society stakeholders). In charge of the SAGE elaboration, it acts as a local water parliament. [SOURCE: Page_182]

EPMP:    Etablissement Public du        State institution coordinating the various actors and Marais Poitevin            stakeholders in the Poitevin Marsh Basin. It is also collaborating with local Agricultural Chambers to attribute water extraction authorizations each year. [SOURCE: Page_182]

OUGC:      Organisme Unique de        Institution in charge of allocating annual irrigation water Gestion Collective                 rights according to the 2006 Water Law. [SOURCE: Page_182]

SAGE:    Schéma d’Aménagement        Local Plan for Water Resource Management establishing et de Gestion des Eaux        water management objectives at the local level, in accordance with the Basin’s SDAGE. It is elaborated by the local CLEs. [SOURCE: Page_182]

SDAGE:       Schéma Directeur        Master Plan for Water Resource Management establishing d’Aménagement et de       water management objectives at the 7 French Basins level. I Gestion des Eaux [SOURCE: Page_182]

ZRE:     Zone de Répartition des      Zone of Water Allocation. Defined as an area where the Eaux                  imbalance between water demand and supply occurs frequently by the 2006 Water Law. [SOURCE: Page_182]

General Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_187]

Water markets have emerged as economic tools to deal with water scarcity in various [SOURCE: Page_187]

economic and cultural contexts. By reallocating water resources towards more productive [SOURCE: Page_187]

activities, they can foster an increased efficiency of water use. [SOURCE: Page_187]

The first chapter of this thesis attempts to measure this potential impact at the aggregated [SOURCE: Page_187]

regional level in Australia, where water markets have been in place for more than 20 years. We [SOURCE: Page_187]

compare areas with and without water markets, with various degrees of water trade intensity. [SOURCE: Page_187]

Results from our stochastic frontier modelling suggest that water markets are associated with a [SOURCE: Page_187]

higher efficiency of agricultural production, although the size of this impact does not increase [SOURCE: Page_187]

with water trade intensity. [SOURCE: Page_187]

Chapter two and three focus on two potential problems associated with the use of water [SOURCE: Page_187]

markets in practice. Chapter two questions the existence of insider trading practices, a well- [SOURCE: Page_187]

known market manipulation in the financial markets literature. We investigate the occurrence [SOURCE: Page_187]

of insider trading behaviour by analysing price movements around important market [SOURCE: Page_187]

announcements within the Murray-Darling Basin water markets, in Australia. We find evidence [SOURCE: Page_187]

of informed price movements (i.e. consistent with the content of the announcement to be made) [SOURCE: Page_187]

in the 5 to 10 days before announcements before 2014, when insider trading regulations were [SOURCE: Page_187]

put in place. After 2014, we detect weak evidence of informed price movements. Such [SOURCE: Page_187]

movements could be attributed to insider trading practices, or to an increased sophistication of [SOURCE: Page_187]

water trade leading to rational speculation behaviour. [SOURCE: Page_187]

Chapter three questions the dynamics of groundwater trade in the Murrumbidgee, where [SOURCE: Page_187]

surface water is also available and tradeable. We find that surface water trade significantly [SOURCE: Page_187]

186 [SOURCE: Page_187]

influenced groundwater trade, with evidence of a lead-lag relationship. In other words, market [SOURCE: Page_188]

sensitive information is first reflected in the surface water price, and then transmitted to the [SOURCE: Page_188]

groundwater price. In this context, groundwater temporary market demand in the [SOURCE: Page_188]

Murrumbidgee is found unitary elastic: in our period of study, a 1% increase in the water price [SOURCE: Page_188]

is associated with a 1.05% decrease in groundwater demand, a relatively high estimate [SOURCE: Page_188]

compared to the literature. Therefore, any policy increasing the water price should reduce [SOURCE: Page_188]

groundwater demand in the Murrumbidgee. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Chapter four questions the transferability of water markets systems to the Poitevin [SOURCE: Page_188]

Marsh Basin and the Neste system, in France. In each case, we present the geographic and [SOURCE: Page_188]

hydrological context, the current water management needs, the framework currently in place to [SOURCE: Page_188]

address those needs and the benefits and impediments identified to the use of water markets. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Overall, the current French water management system is not designed for the use of water [SOURCE: Page_188]

markets. Nevertheless, important potential benefits are identified in both cases, in relation to [SOURCE: Page_188]

the necessary transition towards higher-valued irrigation uses in a context of summer [SOURCE: Page_188]

overconsumption and environmental flow requirements. However, significant barriers remain [SOURCE: Page_188]

to the use of water markets in France, including legal barriers and a very low social acceptability [SOURCE: Page_188]

of markets applied to water management in France. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Four years of research dedicated to water markets highlight five important remarks. [SOURCE: Page_188]

First, there are important prerequisites in order for water markets to provide economic benefits. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Examples include (but are not limited to): a clearly defined and enforced cap (i.e. a maximal [SOURCE: Page_188]

defined amount of water rights); strong supporting institutions providing market information [SOURCE: Page_188]

and ensuring the enforcement and monitoring of water use rights; and a large, hydrologically [SOURCE: Page_188]

interconnected area implying a sufficiently diverse demand for water. Failure to comply with [SOURCE: Page_188]

these prerequisites can generate important damage to the environment through water overuse [SOURCE: Page_188]

or prevent markets to generate efficiency gains. 187 [SOURCE: Page_188]

Second, water markets are no self-sufficient tools. When water markets are chosen as [SOURCE: Page_189]

potential water management mechanisms, they should be used alongside other tools in order to [SOURCE: Page_189]

deal with the diverse challenges raised by water resources management. In an environmental [SOURCE: Page_189]

perspective, for example, restoring environmental flows has been attempted through a federal [SOURCE: Page_189]

program aiming to buy water rights from irrigators in the Australian case. Environmental [SOURCE: Page_189]

regulations are additional tools that might be required to manage challenges as salinity, water [SOURCE: Page_189]

quality or environmental flows. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Third, one consequence of our previous point is that water markets do not imply no [SOURCE: Page_189]

intervention from the regulator. The water authorities have a crucial role to play within water [SOURCE: Page_189]

markets. Enforcing water rights, continuously looking for and monitoring the potential [SOURCE: Page_189]

externalities, providing market information, avoiding asymmetrical information, and ensuring [SOURCE: Page_189]

that no market power emerges represent as many important tasks fulfilled by the regulator. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Fourth, water markets are embedded in a social, political, and cultural framework. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Therefore, they have been designed in practice in many different ways, reflecting different [SOURCE: Page_189]

contexts. Cultural preferences such as values and ethical positioning can affect water markets [SOURCE: Page_189]

when they are established or considered. This is supported by the recent evidence of an [SOURCE: Page_189]

important social pressure among Australian irrigators not to sell permanent water rights of an [SOURCE: Page_189]

area, or by the very low social acceptability of water markets in the French context. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Fifth and finally, in a policy perspective, analysing the costs and benefits of water [SOURCE: Page_189]

markets should be done in comparison to other feasible alternatives in terms of water resource [SOURCE: Page_189]

management. Where water markets might be deemed unsuitable to manage water resources and [SOURCE: Page_189]

scarcity, the identified benefits to their use – their ability to reallocate water towards higher- [SOURCE: Page_189]

valued agricultural uses, for example – might nevertheless inspire the design of policies based [SOURCE: Page_189]

on a different framework. In this sense, empirical studies detailing the benefits obtained through [SOURCE: Page_189]

188 [SOURCE: Page_189]

the use of different water management mechanisms and attempting to compare provides an [SOURCE: Page_190]

interesting research perspective in relation to water markets. [SOURCE: Page_190]

189 [SOURCE: Page_190]

Simon de Bonviller Empirical Essays on Water markets [SOURCE: Page_193]

Résumé Cette thèse porte sur les marchés de droits d’eau, des systèmes permettant l’achat, la vente ou le prêt de droits d’eau selon diverses modalités. L’objectif de la thèse est de contribuer à la connaissance empirique de ces systèmes, présentés par la littérature comme des outils de gestion de la rareté de l’eau permettant une réallocation des ressources en eau vers des usages plus créateurs de valeur. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Les chapitres 1 questionne l’impact de l’existence d’un marché de droit d’eau et de l’intensité des transactions sur l’efficience technique dans l’agriculture Australienne. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Les chapitres 2 et 3 sont consacrés à deux sources de dysfonctionnements potentiels liés à l’usage de marchés de droits d’eau en pratique : les délits d’initiés, et les interactions entre eaux de surface et eau souterraine en présence du marché. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Le Chapitre 4, finalement, questionne la transférabilité des marchés de droits d’eau à deux cas d’étude en France : le bassin du Marais Poitevin, et le système Neste. [SOURCE: Page_193]

English Summary This PhD focuses on water markets. Such systems involve the ability to buy, sell or lease water use rights under different modalities. The main objective of this thesis is to contribute to the empirical knowledge on water markets, described in the literature as tools to manage water scarcity by reallocating water resources towards higher valued uses. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Chapter 1 is dedicated to the economic impacts of water markets and questions the link between the existence of a water market, the intensity of water trade and technical efficiency in the agricultural sector. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Chapters 2 and 3 focus on two potential problems arising with the use of water markets: insider trading, et interactions between surface water and groundwater in presence of a market. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Finally, Chapter 4 questions the transposability of water markets to two case studies in France: the Poitevin Wetlands Basin and the Neste system. [SOURCE: Page_193]
